# Chapter 6 — Fundamentals of Technique

Most men have one technique: the one they discovered at 13, performed with the dominant hand, at one speed, with one grip, until it works. This chapter dismantles that and rebuilds it as a repertoire. Every element — grip, stroke, speed, pressure, hand position, what part of the penis gets stimulated, whether you're dry or lubricated — is a variable you can control. Mastery is knowing the variables and choosing among them.

Read Chapter 2 (anatomy) first if you haven't: technique is applied anatomy.

## 6.1 The variables

Every stroke has these dimensions:

| Variable | Range | What it changes |
|---|---|---|
| **Grip type** | Full fist, thumb-and-fingers ring, two fingers, palm, fingertips | Which surfaces are contacted and how much of the shaft |
| **Pressure** | Barely touching → firm | Depth of sensation (skin vs. erectile bodies), speed of arousal, risk of desensitization |
| **Speed** | Glacial (one stroke per several seconds) → very fast (several per second) | Arousal rate, ability to stay aware, character of orgasm |
| **Stroke length** | Glans-only, upper shaft, full length, base-only | Which nerve zones are recruited |
| **Direction/motion** | Up-down, twist, circular, tap, squeeze-release, static hold | Type of stimulation (friction, pressure, stretch, vibration) |
| **Hand** | Dominant, non-dominant, both | Novelty, coordination, different pressure/angle |
| **Position** | Lying supine, sitting, standing, kneeling, prone, side-lying | Blood flow, pelvic floor engagement, accessibility of other zones |
| **Lubrication** | Dry, light, generous | Friction vs. glide; sensation quality; skin safety |
| **Focus zone** | Frenulum, corona, glans, shaft, base, scrotum, perineum, everything | Intensity and location of sensation |
| **Rhythm** | Steady, accelerating, wave-like, irregular, paused | Predictability (habituation) vs. surprise |

You don't need to think about all ten at once. But recognizing that each one exists — and that changing any of them changes the experience — is the foundation.

## 6.2 Grips

### 6.2.1 The full grip (fist)

The default for most men. All four fingers wrap the shaft, thumb closes the circle, palm contacts the underside or top depending on hand orientation.

- **Overhand (palm toward the body, thumb on top or on the frenulum side)**: the most common. The thumb rides over the frenulum/glans on the upstroke — often the main source of sensation.
- **Underhand (palm facing away, thumb on the underside)**: less common, rotates which fingers contact which surface, changes the angle. Worth trying — many men find it distinctly different.
- **Pressure control**: this grip makes it easy to squeeze too hard. Calibrate: hold your erect penis with a grip just firm enough that the skin moves with your hand rather than your hand sliding over it. That's the *maximum* for a lubricated full stroke; you should spend much of your time lighter than that.

### 6.2.2 The ring (thumb and index/middle fingers)

An "OK" sign around the shaft. Contacts a narrow band, allowing precise placement — over the corona, over the frenulum, at the base.

- Excellent for **corona-focused** stimulation: the ring passes back and forth over the ridge.
- Lower total pressure than a fist; harder to over-grip.
- Pairs well with the other hand doing something else.

### 6.2.3 Two-finger / fingertip techniques

Index and middle finger (or just the pads of fingers) on a specific spot — usually the frenulum or the underside of the glans.

- **Frenulum rub**: lubricated fingertip(s) moving in small circles or short strokes on the frenulum. One of the highest-sensation-per-effort techniques available. Can bring a man from 5 to 9 quickly; use for pushing arousal or for finishing.
- **Glans polish**: lubricated palm or fingers moving over the head. Intense, borderline overwhelming for many men — use light pressure and lots of lube. Some men can't tolerate it; some find it their favorite thing.
- **Underside stroke**: fingers pressing gently along the raphe/corpus spongiosum from base to frenulum. Deeper, less sharp sensation.

### 6.2.4 The palm

Flat palm against the underside, penis pressed against the abdomen, moving up and down or in circles. Broad, diffuse stimulation. Different from any gripping technique because it's pressure and warmth over a large area rather than friction on a band. Good for slow, low-arousal phases.

### 6.2.5 Two-handed techniques

- **Stacked hands**: both hands wrapped one above the other, moving together — essentially a longer grip, contacting the full shaft.
- **Alternating**: one hand strokes up and releases as the other begins at the base, creating a continuous upward (or downward) motion with no return stroke. Feels quite different from a single hand.
- **Base hold + stroke**: one hand holds the base firmly (mild constriction, increasing glans engorgement and giving a sense of "fullness"), the other strokes the upper shaft/glans.
- **Stroke + secondary zone**: one hand on the penis, the other on the scrotum, perineum, nipples, inner thigh, or anus. This is the single most underused category of technique (Chapter 11).
- **Twist**: one hand strokes while rotating around the shaft; or both hands rotate in opposite directions (gently).

### 6.2.6 No-hands

- **Thrusting into a sleeve or pillow** — recruits the hips and pelvic floor as in intercourse. Different muscle engagement, different sensation, useful for training thrusting stamina.
- **Prone grinding** — against a mattress. See Chapter 5's cautions (perineal pressure, and it's a known contributor to delayed ejaculation if it's the exclusive method). Fine as one item in a repertoire.
- **Vibration** — Chapter 13.

## 6.3 Strokes and motions

### 6.3.1 The linear stroke

Up and down along the shaft. The universal default. Variables within it:

- **Full-length**: base to glans and back. Recruits all zones.
- **Upper-half**: focuses on corona and glans. Higher intensity per stroke.
- **Lower-half / base**: shaft-only, avoids the glans. Lower intensity, good for sustaining arousal at a plateau without approaching orgasm. Also good if the glans is oversensitive.
- **Skin-moving vs. skin-sliding**: in uncircumcised men (and circumcised men with enough loose skin), the hand can move the skin over the underlying shaft — the "gliding" action. Or with lube, the hand can slide over stationary skin. These are distinct sensations. Gliding produces stretch on the frenulum and corona; sliding produces friction on the surface. Try both.

### 6.3.2 The twist

Hand rotates around the shaft as it moves, so the palm/fingers spiral. Especially intense as the twist passes over the corona. Common in sleeves with internal texture for this reason. Slow twists are one of the best techniques for extending plateau without tipping over.

### 6.3.3 Circular / glans-focused

Palm or fingers moving in circles over the head, lubricated. Intense. Best in small doses or with very light pressure.

### 6.3.4 Tapping and percussion

Fingers lightly tapping the glans, frenulum, or shaft. Novel, produces a fluttering sensation. Can be surprisingly effective for men who've habituated to stroking.

### 6.3.5 Squeeze-and-release (pulsing)

Hand stationary, rhythmically squeezing and releasing the shaft. No friction. Stimulates via pressure on the erectile bodies. Excellent for holding at a plateau, and for men whose skin needs a break from friction.

### 6.3.6 Static hold

Simply holding the erect penis, still, firmly or lightly, while attention rests on the sensation — often combined with pelvic floor contractions (which move the penis within the hand) or breathing. Sounds like nothing; it isn't. Used in edging to let arousal subside without losing contact, and in mindfulness practice.

### 6.3.7 Stretch and traction

Gently pulling the penis away from the body (by the shaft, not the glans) and holding, or pulling the skin down toward the base to tension the frenulum. Distinct, deep sensation. Do not do this forcefully or at full erection with a tight frenulum.

### 6.3.8 The "milking" stroke

A single direction only — e.g., base to tip, release, repeat — using alternating hands. Emphasizes anticipation because each stroke is a complete event. Also useful post-ejaculation to express remaining semen (some men find this pleasurable or unpleasant — individual).

## 6.4 Speed

Speed is the variable most men use most and understand least.

- **Fast (2–4+ strokes/second)**: raises arousal quickly; narrows attention; often produces a shorter, sharper orgasm; hard to stay aware of arousal level (you blow past 9 before you notice); encourages tight grip; skin-punishing without lube. Appropriate for the final approach to orgasm, or for a deliberately quick session. Inappropriate as a default for anything else.
- **Moderate (about 1 stroke/second)**: sustainable; good arousal ramp; aware of where you are.
- **Slow (one stroke every 2–5 seconds)**: this is where most of the pleasure lives, and where most men never spend time. Each stroke is felt as a complete sensation with a beginning, middle, and end. Arousal climbs slowly and can be held at a plateau. Orgasm reached after a long slow build is typically longer and more intense.
- **Glacial (one stroke per 10+ seconds)**: a mindfulness practice as much as a technique. Every millimeter of movement is noticed. Used in the "extreme slow" exercise (Chapter 11).

**The general principle:** speed should be inversely related to how much of the session remains. Start slow, stay slow through most of it, accelerate only when you've decided to finish — and even then, many men find that a slow approach to orgasm produces a better orgasm than a fast one.

## 6.5 Pressure

- **Calibration exercise.** Erect, lubricated, full grip. Squeeze as hard as you'd usually squeeze. Note it. Now halve the pressure. Stroke for one minute. Note the sensation — it's probably less intense but more detailed; you feel more distinct zones. Now halve it again — barely touching. Stroke for one minute. This third level is where men who've never tried it discover they've been missing the entire surface of their skin.
- **Pressure and zone.** The glans and frenulum want light pressure. The shaft tolerates and often prefers moderate pressure. The base can take firm pressure.
- **Pressure and arousal.** Firmer pressure raises arousal faster but with less nuance. As a rule: light pressure to explore and extend, moderate pressure to build, firm pressure only briefly and only for finishing.
- **The tight-grip trap.** If you need a tight grip to feel anything, that's conditioning (Chapter 5, 16), not your anatomy. Two to four weeks of deliberately lighter, lubricated technique recalibrates it.

## 6.6 Dry vs. lubricated

**Dry** masturbation relies on skin mobility. It works well for uncircumcised men (the foreskin is the lubricant, in a sense) and for circumcised men with sufficient loose shaft skin. Advantages: no mess, no preparation, distinct "gliding" sensation. Disadvantages: higher friction, more skin stress, tends to encourage tighter grip, and eliminates a whole category of glans/frenulum techniques that only work with glide.

**Lubricated** masturbation allows the hand to slide over the skin. Advantages: dramatically lower skin stress; opens up glans, frenulum, and twist techniques; more similar to partnered sex sensation (which is relevant for conditioning); allows lighter pressure to produce more sensation. Disadvantages: mess, preparation, cost, and some men initially find the sensation "too slippery" or less intense — this usually passes within a few sessions as sensitivity recalibrates.

**Recommendation:** every man should be competent in both. If you've only ever done one, spend two weeks doing the other. Chapter 7 covers lubricant selection in detail.

## 6.7 Circumcised vs. uncircumcised technique notes

**Uncircumcised:**

- The foreskin's gliding motion over the glans is the primary mechanism. Strokes that move the skin — rather than sliding over it — are usually most effective.
- The glans is typically more sensitive (protected tissue). Direct glans stimulation may be intense; approach gradually.
- Lube is optional but adds the sliding category of sensations. Lube under the foreskin, directly on the glans, then stroking with the foreskin partially covering it, is a distinct and popular technique.
- Foreskin can be held forward over the glans and the hand can stroke the outer foreskin, stimulating the glans through it — a gentler, diffuse sensation.
- The ridged band (inside the tip of the foreskin) is a specific zone; light fingertip pressure there while the foreskin is partially retracted is worth exploring.
- Cleanliness under the foreskin before and after matters more (Chapter 5).

**Circumcised:**

- Less mobile skin means the hand slides more than it glides. Lube becomes much more important — for both sensation and skin protection.
- The glans is typically less sensitive (keratinized from exposure), which means direct glans techniques are often more tolerable and more useful.
- The scar line and remaining frenulum tissue are usually the most sensitive zones; find yours precisely.
- With lube, the full range of techniques is available. Without it, the repertoire is narrower and skin stress is higher.

## 6.8 Positions

Position changes blood flow, muscle engagement, and which hands and zones are accessible.

- **Supine (on back)**: the classic. Relaxed; both hands free; easy access to everything; good for long sessions. Pillows under the knees relax the pelvic floor.
- **Sitting**: convenient, often at a screen. Tends to hunch the body and tense the pelvic floor; less full-body. Lean back and support the spine if you use it.
- **Standing**: increases pelvic floor and leg engagement; erections are often firmer (gravity, muscle tone); some men find orgasm more intense standing. Knees soft, not locked.
- **Kneeling / all fours**: excellent access to the perineum and anus with one hand; engages the core; a common position for prostate exploration.
- **Prone (face down)**: see cautions in Chapter 5. Fine in moderation on a soft surface; limits hand technique.
- **Side-lying**: relaxed; one hand for the penis, the other easily reaches the perineum or anus; also good for prostate toys.
- **Squatting / on a chair edge / legs elevated**: opens the pelvis; changes perineal and anal access; can intensify pelvic floor sensations.

**Try changing position mid-session.** Moving from sitting to standing when close to orgasm, or from supine to kneeling to reach the perineum, changes the character of the experience.

## 6.9 Building a session: structure

A basic template that embodies the principles above:

1. **Warm-up (2–5 min, arousal 1→4).** Not touching the penis yet, or barely. Hands on thighs, abdomen, chest, scrotum. Breathing slow. Let arousal come to you. If using visual/fantasy material, start here.
2. **Build (5–15 min, 4→7).** Slow strokes, light-to-moderate pressure, lubricated. Vary grip and stroke every minute or two. Notice which zones respond today.
3. **Plateau (5–30+ min, 7→8.5, hovering).** This is the heart of the session and the subject of Chapters 9 and 11. Sustain high arousal without tipping over. Change technique. Add secondary zones. Approach 9, back off to 7, approach again.
4. **Approach (1–3 min, 8.5→9.5).** Decide to finish. Choose the technique and speed. Slow and light produces one kind of orgasm; fast and firm another. Neither is "correct."
5. **Orgasm (5–20 sec, 10).** Continue stimulation through the contractions or stop at the first pulse — different men prefer different things. Pelvic floor engagement (Chapter 10) intensifies contractions.
6. **Afterglow (2–10 min).** Don't jump up. Breathe. Notice the body. This is where the prolactin/oxytocin relaxation lives, and it's better with attention.

Total: 20–60 minutes for a full session. This is not the only kind of session — a 4-minute quickie has its place — but the full version is where the skills develop.

## 6.10 The most common technique mistakes

1. **One technique, always.** Habituation flattens sensation. Rotate.
2. **Too fast, too soon.** Skipping the build robs the orgasm of intensity.
3. **Too tight.** Conditioning, skin stress, reduced nuance.
4. **Dry (for circumcised men especially).** Unnecessary friction; narrows the repertoire.
5. **Glans neglect or glans overload.** Either ignoring the most sensitive tissue or grinding it into numbness.
6. **Ignoring everything but the penis.** Scrotum, perineum, nipples, thighs — untouched.
7. **Fixed attention on a screen.** Sensation is amplified by attention to sensation, not to pixels (Chapter 8).
8. **Tense body.** Clenched jaw, held breath, hunched shoulders — all sympathetic, all working against you.
9. **Racing past 9.** Not knowing where you are on the arousal scale until it's over.
10. **Getting up immediately afterward.** Skipping the afterglow.

## 6.11 A technique inventory to work through

Over the next several sessions, try each of these once, slowly, with lube, for at least 2 minutes, and note how each feels (see the journaling templates in Chapter 21):

1. Full grip, overhand, full-length, moderate pressure
2. Full grip, underhand
3. Ring grip over the corona only
4. Two-finger frenulum circles
5. Palm on glans, circular
6. Palm flat, penis against abdomen, up-down
7. Base-only strokes, no glans contact
8. Squeeze-release pulsing, no friction
9. Slow twist, full length
10. Static hold with pelvic floor pulses
11. Alternating hands, single direction
12. Non-dominant hand only, full session
13. Tapping the glans and frenulum
14. Stroking with one hand while cupping/tugging the scrotum with the other
15. Stroking with one hand while pressing the perineum with the other
16. Standing
17. Kneeling
18. Glacial speed (one stroke per 10 seconds) for 5 minutes
19. Barely-touching pressure for 5 minutes
20. Uncircumcised: foreskin forward, stroking through it; lube under the foreskin. Circumcised: scar-line focus with fingertips.

By the end of this inventory you will know more about your own body than most men learn in a lifetime, and you will have a repertoire instead of a habit.

---

### Key takeaways

- Technique is a set of **variables** — grip, pressure, speed, stroke length, motion, hand, position, lube, zone, rhythm — not a single fixed act.
- **Slower and lighter** than your habit is almost always more pleasurable and always safer. Speed and pressure are for finishing, not for the whole session.
- Build **competence in both dry and lubricated** technique; lube opens an entire category of sensation and protects skin.
- **Circumcised** men rely on sliding and benefit greatly from lube; **uncircumcised** men have built-in gliding and a more sensitive glans.
- A full session has a **structure**: warm-up → build → plateau → approach → orgasm → afterglow. Plateau is where the skill and pleasure live.
- Work through the **20-item inventory** to replace habit with repertoire.

# Chapter 7 — Lubricants: The Deep Dive

Lubricant is the most consequential product choice in solo sex. The right one transforms sensation, protects skin, and opens techniques that are impossible dry. The wrong one irritates, degrades toys, or — in the case of anal use — damages tissue. This chapter covers the chemistry, the categories, what to avoid, and how to choose.

## 7.1 Why lubricate at all

- **Sensation.** Friction masks fine-touch detail. A lubricated hand transmits texture, temperature, and pressure variation in a way a dry hand cannot. Most men who switch from dry to lubricated report initially "less intense, more detailed," then, after adaptation, "much better."
- **Skin protection.** Friction damage — micro-tears, redness, calluses — is the most common masturbation injury (Chapter 5). Lube nearly eliminates it.
- **Technique range.** Glans polishing, frenulum circles, twists, palm techniques — all require glide.
- **Conditioning.** Partnered sex is wet. Training exclusively with dry, high-friction stimulation can make the wetter, softer sensation of a partner feel insufficient [Perelman 2016]. Lubricated solo practice keeps the sensory expectation realistic.
- **Toys.** Sleeves and prostate toys require lube; dry use is uncomfortable and damages both you and the toy.
- **Anal.** Non-negotiable. The rectum produces no lubrication.

## 7.2 The four families

### 7.2.1 Water-based

**Composition:** water + a thickener (cellulose derivatives, carbomers, xanthan) + humectants (glycerin, propylene glycol, or newer alternatives) + preservatives (parabens, phenoxyethanol, potassium sorbate) + pH adjusters.

**Pros:** compatible with everything (latex, polyisoprene, silicone toys, TPE sleeves); easy cleanup with water; non-staining; widely available; huge variety.

**Cons:** dries out as water evaporates/absorbs — needs reapplication or a spritz of water to reactivate (this is the most common complaint); can get sticky or tacky as it dries, especially glycerin-heavy formulas; many formulas are hyperosmolar (see 7.4) and irritating for some users; preservatives are the most common allergens.

**Best for:** general use, toy use, anyone who wants easy cleanup, anal use *if* the osmolality is reasonable.

**Sub-types:**
- *Glycerin-based*: cheap, slick, slightly sweet, but high osmolality and can feed yeast in prone individuals. Common in drugstore brands.
- *Glycerin-free / "natural"*: often use aloe, carrageenan, or hydroxyethylcellulose. Usually lower osmolality, less sticky, less irritating. Dry out faster.
- *Thick / gel*: stays in place; good for anal.
- *Thin / liquid*: spreads easily; good for full-hand techniques.
- *Cream-textured hybrids*: see 7.2.4.

### 7.2.2 Silicone-based

**Composition:** dimethicone, dimethiconol, cyclomethicone, and related polymers. No water.

**Pros:** extremely long-lasting — does not dry out or absorb; a small amount goes a long way; silky rather than slick; waterproof (works in the shower); hypoallergenic for most people; iso-osmotic-irrelevant (no water to draw); doesn't feed yeast.

**Cons:** should not be used with silicone toys (the lube's solvents can degrade the toy's surface, making it sticky and porous — the effect is real, especially with lower-grade silicone toys, though high-quality platinum silicone is more resistant; the safe rule is "don't"); requires soap to remove; can stain fabrics; more expensive per bottle (but far less used per session); some people find the texture "too slippery" or unnatural.

**Best for:** long sessions, hand-only or with non-silicone toys (glass, steel, ABS, TPE sleeves), shower use, anal play (excellent staying power), men whose skin reacts to water-based preservatives.

**Note on TPE sleeves:** most sleeve manufacturers recommend water-based only. Silicone lube on TPE is generally fine for the material itself, but check the manufacturer's guidance — some TPE formulations do degrade.

### 7.2.3 Oil-based

**Composition:** plant oils (coconut, almond, olive, sunflower), mineral oil, petroleum jelly, or commercial oil-based blends.

**Pros:** very long-lasting; excellent for skin (moisturizing); cheap and often already in the house; natural options available; good for massage that transitions into genital touch.

**Cons:** **destroys latex condoms** (irrelevant for solo, critical for partnered); can degrade some toy materials; hard to clean; stains fabrics; coconut oil is comedogenic for some (acne on nearby skin); mineral oil/petroleum jelly should not be used anally by anyone who might later have receptive anal sex with a condom, and some data associates petroleum-based products with altered vaginal flora (not relevant to solo male use, but a reason it's not used as a general recommendation); rancidity — plant oils oxidize, so use fresh oil and small quantities.

**Best for:** hand-only sessions where cleanup is manageable, men who want a skin-nourishing option, long slow sessions, "massage-first" approaches.

**Common household oils ranked for solo use:**
- *Coconut oil (fractionated or virgin)*: excellent glide, antimicrobial properties, pleasant; can clog pores in the groin for some men.
- *Sweet almond oil*: light, absorbs slowly, minimal odor. Nut allergy caution.
- *Olive oil*: works; heavier; distinct smell.
- *Jojoba*: technically a wax ester; close to skin's own sebum; very well tolerated.
- *Mineral oil / baby oil*: works; petroleum-derived; some prefer to avoid; check that "baby oil" isn't fragranced.
- *Petroleum jelly*: works but thick; hard to remove; best as a barrier for healing skin rather than a primary lube.
- **Avoid:** butter, margarine, cooking oils that go rancid quickly, anything with added fragrance, lotions with alcohol or fragrance.

### 7.2.4 Hybrids and specialty

**Hybrid (water + silicone)**: creamy texture, longer-lasting than pure water-based, easier cleanup than pure silicone. Small silicone percentage means usually (not always) toy-safe — check the label. Popular "middle ground."

**Cream / lotion-type lubes**: emulsions, often oil-in-water. Thick, opaque, very good for hand-only sessions; not for toys or condoms unless specified.

**Warming / cooling / tingling**: contain menthol, capsaicin derivatives, niacin, or peppermint. Novelty sensation. Irritating for many; keep off the urethral opening; never use anally.

**Flavored**: for oral use; typically high in sugar/glycerin; irrelevant for solo.

**"Natural"/organic**: marketing term. Look at the actual ingredients and osmolality.

**Anal-specific**: usually thicker gels; sometimes contain numbing agents (benzocaine, lidocaine) — **avoid numbing lubes**. Pain is information. Numbing allows injury you won't feel until later.

**Masturbation-specific creams** (e.g., products marketed as "stroke" lotions): usually hybrid or oil-based emulsions optimized for hand use. Often excellent for the purpose.

## 7.3 Ingredients to avoid or be cautious with

| Ingredient | Concern | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| **Nonoxynol-9** (spermicide) | Damages epithelial cells, increases infection risk | Avoid |
| **Glycerin (high concentration)** | Hyperosmolar; can feed yeast; sticky | Fine for most on the penis; avoid for anal; avoid if prone to irritation |
| **Propylene glycol (high concentration)** | Hyperosmolar; irritant for some | Same as glycerin |
| **Parabens** (methyl-, propyl-) | Weak estrogenic activity in lab studies; allergen for some | Low concern for most; avoid if reactive |
| **Chlorhexidine gluconate** | Kills beneficial bacteria; WHO flagged | Avoid, especially anal |
| **Polyquaternium-15** | Some evidence of increasing HIV replication in vitro | Avoid |
| **Benzocaine / lidocaine** | Numbs — masks injury | Avoid |
| **Fragrance / parfum** | Common allergen | Avoid on genitals |
| **Menthol, capsaicin, cinnamon** | Irritants | Novelty only; not anal |
| **Alcohol (ethanol, denatured)** | Drying, stinging | Avoid |
| **Petroleum (anal)** | Persistent; may harbor bacteria; condom-incompatible | Avoid anal |
| **Sugar (flavored lubes)** | Feeds yeast | Avoid on penis/anal |

## 7.4 Osmolality and pH: the science most guides skip

**Osmolality** is the concentration of dissolved particles in a solution. Body tissues sit at roughly 280–300 mOsm/kg. A lubricant with much higher osmolality (**hyperosmolar**) pulls water out of the epithelial cells it contacts, causing them to shrink, slough, and lose barrier integrity. In laboratory tissue models, hyperosmolar lubricants markedly reduced epithelial barrier function [Ayehunie 2017], and in a human study, a hyperosmolar lubricant caused visible epithelial damage in the distal colon within 60–90 minutes [Fuchs 2007].

The **World Health Organization** (2012 advisory note on lubricants for condom use) recommended that lubricants ideally have osmolality below **380 mOsm/kg**, with an interim maximum of **1,200 mOsm/kg**. Many popular commercial water-based lubricants measure 2,000–6,000+ mOsm/kg — 10 to 20 times body tissue. The culprits are typically high glycerin and propylene glycol content.

**Why this matters for men:**

- **Anal use:** rectal epithelium is a single cell layer and highly vulnerable. Hyperosmolar lube → micro-damage → irritation, and in partnered contexts, increased STI transmission risk. Choose iso-osmotic or near-iso-osmotic lubes for anything anal.
- **Penile use:** the penile shaft skin is keratinized and tough; osmolality matters less. But the glans, inner foreskin, and urethral opening are mucosal-type tissue. Men who experience stinging, redness, or a "dried out" feeling after using lube are often reacting to osmolality, not to an allergen.

**pH.** Rectal pH is around 7; a lube for anal use should be near-neutral (5.5–7). Highly acidic lubes (formulated for vaginal use around pH 4) are not ideal for anal use.

**How to find out.** Osmolality is rarely printed on the label, but several independent testers and some manufacturers publish it. Iso-osmotic brands market this fact. Rules of thumb: glycerin-free and propylene-glycol-free water-based lubes are usually much lower; silicone and oil lubes have no relevant osmolality (no water phase). If a lube stings, it's probably hyperosmolar or contains an irritant — switch.

## 7.5 Compatibility matrix

| | Latex condom | Polyurethane condom | Silicone toy | TPE/TPR sleeve | Glass/steel/ABS | Skin (penis) | Anal |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Water-based** | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ if low osmolality |
| **Silicone** | ✅ | ✅ | ❌ | ⚠️ check maker | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ |
| **Oil** | ❌ | ✅ (most) | ⚠️ | ⚠️ | ✅ | ✅ | ⚠️ (not petroleum) |
| **Hybrid** | ✅ usually | ✅ | ⚠️ check | ✅ usually | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ |

## 7.6 Choosing: a decision guide

**"I want one bottle for everything hand-related."** → Silicone. Lasts, silky, minimal reapplication, waterproof. Keep it away from silicone toys.

**"I use a TPE stroker."** → Water-based (manufacturer standard). Have a spray bottle of water nearby to reactivate. Or a hybrid if the maker approves.

**"I use a silicone prostate massager."** → Water-based, ideally glycerin-free and low-osmolality. Thick gel formulas stay put.

**"My skin gets irritated by lubes."** → Try silicone first (fewest ingredients, no water-phase preservatives). If not, a glycerin-free, paraben-free, fragrance-free water-based. Patch test on the inner forearm for 24 hours.

**"I want something natural and I don't use toys."** → Fractionated coconut oil or jojoba. Small quantities. Fresh bottles.

**"I want to try anal play."** → Thick water-based with low osmolality, or silicone. Never numbing. Lots of it.

**"I want the most intense glans sensation."** → Thin water-based or silicone; the glide lets the hand move fast and light over the head.

**"I want long, slow sessions."** → Silicone or a good hybrid cream. Both last 30–60 minutes without reapplication.

**"I'm on a budget."** → Silicone lube is the best value per session despite the higher bottle price. Or coconut oil.

## 7.7 Application technique

- **Amount.** Start with a coin-sized dollop for a hand session; more for toys; a lot more for anal. Under-lubing is the common error. You should be able to stroke with the lightest pressure and have the hand glide.
- **Warm it.** Cold lube on the glans is unpleasant and briefly suppresses arousal. Rub it between palms for a few seconds, or keep the bottle in a pocket/under a pillow beforehand.
- **Where.** Glans, frenulum, and shaft. Under the foreskin if uncircumcised (retract, apply to the glans, let the foreskin return, then stroke with the foreskin gliding over a lubricated glans — a highly effective technique).
- **Reapply.** Water-based: when it starts to feel tacky, either add more or add a few drops of water (which reactivates most formulas). Silicone: rarely needed.
- **Layering.** A thin layer of silicone under water-based ("silicone base coat") extends the water-based lube's life. Some men use oil on the skin as a base and water-based on top for toy compatibility (check the toy).
- **Cleanup.** Water-based: rinse. Silicone: soap and water, or a wipe first. Oil: soap; a bit of dish soap cuts oil quickly if needed.

## 7.8 DIY and household substitutes — cautions

Things people reach for, and whether they should:

- **Hand lotion / body lotion**: often contain fragrance, alcohol, and irritants. Some plain, fragrance-free lotions work; most are a bad idea on the glans. Not for anal.
- **Saliva**: works briefly; dries fast; not a real lubricant; fine in a pinch for hand use.
- **Soap / shampoo / conditioner**: no. Alkaline, drying, irritating to the urethra. Conditioner is the least bad (it's essentially a cationic emulsion) but still not recommended.
- **Aloe vera gel**: pure aloe (no alcohol, no lidocaine) works reasonably as a light water-based lube. Check the ingredients — "aloe gel" from a pharmacy often has alcohol.
- **Egg whites, yogurt, honey**: no. Sugar, protein, bacteria.
- **Vaseline**: works as a hand lube; hard to clean; not for anal; not with latex.
- **Cooking spray**: contains propellants and additives; no.
- **Vegetable glycerin (pure)**: extremely hyperosmolar; sticky. No.
- **Cornstarch + water (homemade lube)**: a folk recipe (boil 1 tbsp cornstarch in 1 cup water, cool). Actually works reasonably for hand use, is iso-osmotic-ish, and is cheap. Spoils in 1–2 days; refrigerate; make small batches. Not for anal.

**General rule:** if it's not designed for the mucosa or you wouldn't put it in your mouth, be cautious about putting it on your glans.

## 7.9 Storage and shelf life

- Keep lube out of heat and direct sunlight.
- Water-based: typically 1–3 years sealed; 6–12 months after opening. Discard if it changes color, smell, or texture, or separates.
- Silicone: very stable; years.
- Oils: rancidity is the issue. Use within a few months of opening; smell it — rancid oil smells like old crayons or stale nuts.
- Pump bottles are more hygienic than open-mouth jars (fingers contaminate jars).

---

### Key takeaways

- Lubricant increases **sensation detail**, protects **skin**, opens **technique range**, and keeps sensory expectations **realistic** for partnered sex. Every man should use it at least some of the time; circumcised men, most of the time.
- **Water-based**: universal compatibility, dries out. **Silicone**: lasts forever, not with silicone toys. **Oil**: skin-friendly, kills latex, messy. **Hybrid**: middle ground.
- **Osmolality matters**, especially anally. Ideal <380 mOsm/kg; avoid >1,200. High glycerin/propylene glycol = high osmolality. Stinging = switch.
- **Avoid**: nonoxynol-9, numbing agents, fragrance, chlorhexidine, polyquaternium-15, alcohol, sugar.
- **Use more than you think.** Warm it. Reapply or add water when tacky.
- Household items: coconut oil, jojoba, pure aloe, and homemade cornstarch lube work for hand use. Soap, lotion with fragrance, and food products don't.

# Chapter 8 — Setting, Mindset & Mindfulness

The same physical technique produces radically different experiences depending on where your attention is, how relaxed your body is, whether you feel safe, and what you expect. This chapter covers the environmental and mental conditions that let physiology do its best work, and introduces mindful masturbation — the practice of paying attention to sensation as it happens — which may be the single highest-leverage skill in this guide.

## 8.1 Why setting matters physiologically

Recall from Chapter 3: erection and the slow build of arousal are **parasympathetic** — they need the nervous system in a "safe, unhurried" state. Vigilance, time pressure, fear of interruption, cold, discomfort, and multitasking all push toward **sympathetic** dominance, which weakens erection, speeds ejaculation, and narrows the range of sensation you can perceive.

This isn't mystical. If part of your brain is listening for footsteps, it's not fully available to process pleasure. Amygdala activity (threat detection) has to drop for the prefrontal "letting go" of orgasm to happen [Georgiadis 2006]. A man masturbating in a rush in a shared bathroom with a locked door and one ear on the hallway is physiologically constrained in a way that no technique can fully overcome.

## 8.2 The environment checklist

Not every session can be ideal. But knowing what "ideal" looks like lets you get as close as circumstances allow.

**Privacy and time.**
- Genuine privacy — a locked door and knowledge that no one will need you for the duration. Not "probably no one will come in."
- No deadline. Even a 15-minute session is better with no clock than a 40-minute session with one eye on the time. If you have a hard stop, set an alarm and then forget about it.
- Phone on silent, face down or in another room, unless it's your source of erotic material (and even then, notifications off).

**Comfort.**
- **Temperature**: warm enough to be naked without goosebumps. Cold is sympathetic; it also retracts the scrotum and reduces blood flow. A warm room or a blanket makes a measurable difference.
- **Surface**: a bed is better than a chair; a chair with back support is better than a hard stool. Pillows under the knees (supine) relax the lower back and pelvic floor.
- **Cleanliness**: a clean body and clean sheets/towel remove a low-level background distraction.
- **Lighting**: dim, warm lighting is more relaxing than overhead fluorescent or a bright screen in a dark room. Candlelight-level is ideal for most men — enough to see, not enough to feel observed.

**Supplies within reach.**
- Lubricant (warmed).
- Towel or tissues.
- Water to drink.
- Any toys, cleaned and ready.
- A spray bottle of water if using water-based lube.

Having to get up mid-session to find something drops arousal and breaks immersion.

**Sound.**
- Silence, ambient music, nature sounds, or erotic audio depending on preference. Music without lyrics at a slow tempo (60–80 bpm) tends to entrain slower breathing and slower stroking. Some men find rhythmic music helps them regulate speed.
- Headphones increase immersion and privacy.

**Scent.**
- Underrated. Scent connects directly to the limbic system. A consistent, pleasant scent used only for sessions (a specific candle, oil, or even a particular lube's smell) becomes a conditioned arousal cue over time — a classical-conditioning trick that works.

## 8.3 Body preparation

**Bladder.** Empty it beforehand. A full bladder presses on the prostate and pelvic floor and creates urgency that competes with arousal; it also increases the risk of retrograde flow and reduces pelvic floor control. Some men deliberately masturbate with a moderately full bladder for a "fuller" sensation; that's fine occasionally, but it's not the default.

**Bowels.** For anal play, have a bowel movement an hour or more before (Chapter 5).

**Shower.** A warm shower before a session relaxes muscles, warms the body, cleans, and creates a transition from "day mode" to "session mode." Many men find this ritual dramatically improves session quality.

**Stretching.** Two minutes of hip openers (deep squat, butterfly stretch, happy-baby pose) and a few slow pelvic tilts loosen the pelvic floor and hips, which are where men store the most sex-relevant tension. See Chapter 10.

**Food and substances.** A very full stomach diverts blood and makes you sluggish. Alcohol beyond one drink impairs erection and sensation. Caffeine within a few hours can raise sympathetic tone. Cannabis has mixed effects: low doses enhance sensation and time-dilation for many men, higher doses cause distraction or erectile softening. (Chapter 18 covers substances.)

**Fatigue.** Sessions when exhausted are shorter, less sensitive, and often unsatisfying. Late night is the most common time for masturbation and often the worst time for quality. If you can, try a morning or afternoon session and compare.

## 8.4 The mental transition

Most men go from whatever they were doing straight to genital stimulation in under a minute. The body hasn't caught up. Build a **transition** — even 2–3 minutes:

1. Lie down. Close your eyes.
2. Take ten slow breaths, exhaling longer than you inhale (e.g., 4 seconds in, 6–8 out). Long exhales activate the vagus nerve — the fastest way to shift toward parasympathetic.
3. Scan the body from head to feet. Notice tension. Let the jaw drop open slightly. Let the shoulders fall. Unclench the hands. Let the belly be soft.
4. Notice the pelvic floor. Is it gripping? Let it drop.
5. Only now, begin touching — and not the genitals first.

This ritual, repeated, becomes a cue. Within a couple of weeks, the ten breaths alone begin to produce the first flickers of arousal, because the body has learned what comes next.

## 8.5 Attention: the master variable

Here is the key claim of this chapter: **the amount of pleasure you experience is, to a first approximation, the amount of sensation multiplied by the amount of attention you pay to it.**

A stroke you don't notice generates almost no pleasure. A stroke you notice completely — its beginning, the passage over the corona, the tug on the frenulum, the change in pressure, the temperature of your hand — generates a great deal. Same stroke.

Where does attention go instead?

- **Onto a screen.** Watching pornography or scrolling images places attention on visual processing. The visual input drives arousal (the "wanting" system), but the *sensation* in the penis is processed in the background. Many men who masturbate exclusively to visual material report that they barely notice their own hand — which is one reason they need fast, tight, high-intensity stimulation to get the sensory signal above the noise floor. Chapter 14 covers this in depth.
- **Into fantasy.** Internal imagery is less attention-hungry than a screen, but it can still take most of the bandwidth.
- **Into monitoring.** "Am I hard enough? Is this taking too long? What if someone hears?" Self-monitoring is prefrontal activity, and it competes directly with the letting-go that orgasm requires. It's the mechanism of performance anxiety and a primary contributor to delayed ejaculation and situational ED.
- **Into the future.** "Almost there, almost there, hurry up" — goal-focused attention on the orgasm-to-come. This is the most common pattern and the most pleasure-destroying: you skip the present sensation to chase a future one, and then the future one lasts ten seconds.

**Mindful masturbation** is the practice of putting attention on sensation, here, now. It's not about eliminating fantasy or visuals; it's about noticing where attention is and choosing to return it to the body regularly.

## 8.6 Mindful masturbation: the practice

Sensate focus, developed by Masters and Johnson and used in sex therapy for fifty years, is the partnered version: touch without a goal, attention on sensation. Mindfulness-based interventions have strong evidence for improving sexual function and satisfaction, mostly studied in women [Brotto 2016] with promising pilot data in men [Bossio 2018]. The solo version is straightforward.

### The basic practice

1. **Set up** as in 8.2–8.4. Warm room, private, no clock, transition done.
2. **No goal.** For this session, orgasm is optional. You may or may not have one. The point is to notice.
3. **Begin with non-genital touch.** Hands on chest, belly, thighs. Notice texture, temperature, pressure. Two to three minutes.
4. **Move to the genitals slowly.** Scrotum, perineum, the base of the shaft. Light touch. Notice.
5. **Stroke slowly.** One stroke every 2–5 seconds. With each stroke, put attention on one thing: the feeling at the corona as the hand passes over; the stretch at the frenulum; the pressure on the underside; the warmth. Change the object of attention every few strokes.
6. **When attention wanders — and it will — notice, and return it.** Without judgment. This *is* the practice. Every return strengthens the skill. You'll do it hundreds of times.
7. **Notice arousal as a whole-body event.** Where do you feel it besides the penis? Chest? Belly? Face? Thighs? Let attention rest there sometimes.
8. **Notice breath.** Is it shallow and held (sympathetic) or slow and full (parasympathetic)? Let it be slow and full. Exhale through the mouth.
9. **Notice the urge to speed up.** It will come. Notice it as a sensation — a kind of impatience in the body — and choose not to obey it, this time. Stay slow.
10. **If orgasm comes, stay with it.** Attention on the contractions, the sensation in the pelvis, the spreading warmth, the breath. Don't check out. Don't reach for tissues in the middle of it.
11. **Afterglow.** Five minutes of stillness. Notice the body. This is often where the deepest relaxation lives.

### Common experiences the first few times

- "It felt like less was happening." Correct — you removed the high-intensity stimulus and the mental noise. The sensation is quieter but far more detailed. Within a few sessions, quiet-and-detailed begins to feel like *more*.
- "I couldn't stop thinking." Normal. The mind wanders every few seconds. Returning attention is the exercise, not a failure of it.
- "I lost my erection." Normal in slow, low-stimulation sessions. It returns. Not having a full erection is not a problem for a mindfulness session.
- "I got bored." Boredom is what the mind calls low-stimulus attention before it learns to find detail. It passes. Also: boredom is sometimes the accurate signal that your habitual technique was working mostly through *intensity* and not through *sensation*. Sit with it.
- "The orgasm was different." Usually longer, more spread through the body, less sharp, more satisfying. Some men initially miss the sharpness. Most stop missing it.

### Variations

- **Body scan first.** A full 5–10 minute body scan before any touching. Deepens the parasympathetic state.
- **Eyes closed throughout.** Removes visual input entirely, forcing attention to touch.
- **Non-dominant hand.** The unfamiliarity keeps attention from going automatic.
- **Blindfold and headphones.** Sensory reduction amplifies the remaining channel.
- **Counting strokes.** A concentration anchor. Count to 20, then start again. When you lose count, you've wandered.
- **Narrating silently.** "Hand at the base... rising... over the ridge... light pressure on the head... down..." Verbal labeling keeps attention anchored.

## 8.7 Breath

Breath is the one autonomic function you can consciously steer, and it's a lever on the whole system.

- **Slow, deep breathing** (5–6 breaths per minute, belly rising) → parasympathetic → firmer erections, slower ejaculation, wider sensation.
- **Held breath / shallow chest breathing** → sympathetic → faster ejaculation, narrower sensation. This is what most men do involuntarily as arousal rises. It's the single most correctable contributor to premature ejaculation.
- **Long exhale** at high arousal (8–9) drops arousal a notch, reliably. It's the fastest edging tool.
- **Breathing "into" the sensation** — imagining the inhale drawing sensation up from the pelvis through the body — is subjectively powerful for many men, and physiologically it keeps the breath full and the body relaxed.
- **Vocalizing on the exhale** — a low hum or sigh — extends the exhale, relaxes the jaw and throat (which are reflexively linked to the pelvic floor), and deepens immersion. Not always possible for privacy reasons, but worth using when it is.

**Practice:** during any session, once every minute or two, check: am I breathing? Is it slow? Take one long exhale. That alone changes the session.

## 8.8 Body tension

At high arousal the body clenches involuntarily — jaw, shoulders, fists, thighs, feet (curled toes), abdomen, buttocks. This is normal myotonia. Some of it (pelvic floor, buttocks) contributes to arousal and orgasm intensity. Much of it (jaw, shoulders, hands, held breath) is pure sympathetic bracing and works against you.

**The paradox:** men trying to *not* orgasm typically tense everything — which speeds ejaculation. Men who can *relax* the non-pelvic body while allowing pelvic arousal can sustain much higher arousal for much longer. This is the core physical skill of edging (Chapter 9) and full-body orgasm (Chapter 12).

**Practice:** at arousal 7–8, do a quick scan. Jaw — drop it. Shoulders — let them fall. Hands — the free hand open and soft. Feet — flat, uncurled. Belly — soft, breathing. Then notice: arousal is still there, maybe more diffuse, and you're further from the edge.

## 8.9 Expectation and self-talk

- **"This will be good."** Expectation shapes experience through top-down modulation of sensory processing. Approaching a session as something to savor produces a different physiology than approaching it as something to get done.
- **"I have time."** Even if you have 15 minutes, telling yourself there's no rush changes the nervous system's mode.
- **"There's nothing to achieve."** Orgasm will happen if it happens. This removes the performance frame, which is the direct cause of most situational dysfunction.
- **"Whatever I feel is fine."** Softening erection, wandering attention, an unexpected emotion, a fantasy you didn't plan — none of these need to be corrected. Noticing without judgment is the skill.

**Shame.** For many men, a low background hum of shame accompanies masturbation, absorbed from religious, cultural, or family sources. Shame is sympathetic (it's a threat response). It shortens sessions, speeds ejaculation, reduces pleasure, and makes afterglow uncomfortable ("get up, clean up, forget it"). Chapter 19 covers the cultural history; here the practical point: notice if you're rushing to finish and hide, and gently ask why. The information in Chapter 4 helps. Time helps. Deliberately lingering in afterglow — not leaping up — helps.

## 8.10 Fantasy and visual material: the attention trade-off

This deserves its own chapter (14), but the mindfulness-relevant point is: **erotic material drives arousal ("wanting") efficiently, but pulls attention away from sensation ("feeling").** The optimal use for most men is a hybrid:

- Use material to get to arousal 5–6, then close the eyes or look away and return attention to the body.
- Or alternate: a minute of watching, a minute of eyes closed on sensation.
- Or use audio-only material, which leaves the eyes closed and attention more available for touch.
- Or use material at the start and finish, with the plateau spent on sensation.

Men who try this almost universally report that the session is more pleasurable and the orgasm stronger than with continuous screen focus. The screen is a tool for the wanting system; the hand and the attention are the tools for the feeling system.

## 8.11 Rituals and conditioning

Everything in this chapter can be turned into a **ritual** — a repeated sequence that the body learns to associate with arousal and relaxation. Shower → dim lights → specific scent → lie down → ten breaths → body scan → begin. After a few weeks, the ritual itself begins the arousal, and the transition into a deep, unhurried state becomes fast and reliable.

This is classical conditioning, and it's exactly the same mechanism that makes pornography habits hard to break — the cue triggers the response. The difference is that you're choosing the cue and the response deliberately.

---

### Key takeaways

- Arousal and erection are **parasympathetic**; privacy, warmth, comfort, no clock, and no vigilance are physiological requirements, not luxuries.
- Build a **transition ritual**: shower, breath, body scan. It becomes a conditioned arousal cue.
- **Pleasure ≈ sensation × attention.** Attention on a screen, on monitoring, or on the future orgasm is attention not on the sensation.
- **Mindful masturbation**: slow, goal-free, attention on sensation, return when it wanders. Initially feels like "less"; quickly becomes "more."
- **Breath** is the master lever: slow deep breathing → firmer erection, slower ejaculation, wider sensation. Long exhale at the edge drops arousal.
- **Relax the non-pelvic body** at high arousal. Tension speeds ejaculation and narrows pleasure.
- Use **visual material for the "wanting" system**, then close the eyes and return attention to the body for the "feeling" system.

# Chapter 9 — Edging, Stamina & Arousal Control

Arousal control is the pivot skill. It is what turns a 3-minute reflex into a 45-minute practice; it's the foundation for non-ejaculatory and multiple orgasms; it's the evidence-based treatment for premature ejaculation; and it's the single most effective way to intensify orgasm. This chapter teaches it systematically.

## 9.1 What "edging" is

**Edging** means deliberately bringing arousal close to orgasm — to the "edge" — and then reducing stimulation so that orgasm doesn't occur, then building again. Repeated cycles extend the session and, most men find, produce a substantially more intense eventual orgasm.

The term is from the 2000s internet, but the practice is old. Sex therapists have used the same mechanics since the 1950s under the names **stop-start technique** (Semans 1956) and **squeeze technique** (Masters & Johnson 1970) as treatments for premature ejaculation. Tantric and Taoist traditions describe similar practices. What's new is doing it deliberately for pleasure rather than as therapy — though it turns out the pleasure practice and the therapy are the same thing.

## 9.2 The arousal scale, refined

Chapter 3 introduced a 1–10 scale. Edging requires you to know it with precision, especially the top end. Here's the refined version most men find useful:

| Level | Name | What you feel | Physical signs |
|---|---|---|---|
| 6 | Cruising | Fully aroused, comfortable, could go indefinitely | Firm erection, relaxed body, normal breath |
| 7 | Rising | Pleasure is building, stopping would be mildly frustrating | Erection at max, breath starting to deepen |
| 8 | Committed | The body "wants" to continue; effort to stop | Testes rising, glans darkening, breath faster, mild pelvic tension |
| 8.5 | Approaching | Orgasm feels *available*; you could get there in 30–60 seconds | Pelvic floor starting to tense involuntarily, thighs tightening |
| 9 | The edge | Orgasm is 5–15 seconds away at current stimulation | Involuntary pelvic pulses beginning, breath catching, sensation focusing in the glans and base |
| 9.5 | Point of no return | Emission has begun; orgasm is coming regardless | Deep pressure/fullness at the base and perineum, an unmistakable "tipping" |
| 10 | Orgasm | | |

**The learning task** is to reliably identify 8, 8.5, and 9 — and especially to distinguish 9 from 9.5. Most men who "can't edge" are simply reaching 9 without noticing they passed 8.5, and by the time they act, they're at 9.5.

**Signals to attend to** (learn yours; they vary):

- **Breath** — the most reliable early signal. It gets faster and shallower at 8, catches at 9.
- **Testicular elevation** — the testes rise progressively; at 9 they're tight against the body.
- **Pelvic floor** — the muscles start contracting involuntarily around 8.5–9; you'll feel a "pulsing" or "gripping" at the base and perineum.
- **Glans** — engorgement and darkening; sensation sharpens and focuses there.
- **Thighs and buttocks** — tighten involuntarily.
- **The "inevitability" feeling** — 9.5. A deep, pressurized fullness at the base. If you feel this, you're done; stopping now produces a ruined orgasm (see 9.7).
- **Mental** — attention collapses onto the sensation; thoughts stop. This is 9.

## 9.3 The basic method: stop-start

1. **Build** to 7–8 with slow, moderate strokes.
2. **Continue** with attention on the signals. As you approach 8.5–9 — *before* the edge, not at it — **stop stimulation completely.** Hand still, or hand off.
3. **Let arousal subside** to 6–7. This takes 15–60 seconds. Breathe slowly with long exhales. Relax the thighs, buttocks, jaw.
4. **Resume.** Build again. The second approach is often faster.
5. **Repeat** 3–10+ times.
6. **Finish** when you choose — or don't (see Chapter 12 on non-ejaculatory sessions).

**Key refinement:** stop *early* while learning. Aim for 8.5, not 9. You'll undershoot sometimes and it'll feel like you stopped for no reason; that's fine. Overshooting even once resets the session (or produces a ruined orgasm). As you learn your signals, you can stop closer and closer to the edge.

**Progression:** most men can do 2–3 cycles the first time and 8–10+ within a few weeks. The eventual orgasm after a well-edged session is, for most men, noticeably longer, with more contractions, more spread through the body, and often with more ejaculate force.

## 9.4 Tools for dropping arousal

Stopping stimulation alone works but is slow. These accelerate the descent:

- **Long exhale.** One slow exhale through the mouth, all the air out, pause. Drops arousal half a point immediately. The most important tool.
- **Full-body relaxation.** Consciously release thighs, buttocks, abdomen, jaw, shoulders, hands. Arousal is partly held in tension; release the tension, release some arousal.
- **Reverse Kegel.** Push the pelvic floor *down and out* as if gently bearing down (Chapter 10). This directly counters the involuntary pelvic tension that precedes orgasm. Extremely effective once learned.
- **Testicle traction.** Gently pull the testes down, away from the body. Testicular elevation is part of the ejaculatory sequence; reversing it interrupts the reflex.
- **Change zone.** Move the hand from the glans to the base, or off the penis to the thigh or chest. Sensation continues but at lower intensity.
- **Squeeze.** The Masters & Johnson technique: firm squeeze just below the glans (thumb on the frenulum, fingers on the corona), or at the base, for 5–10 seconds. Reduces arousal and erection somewhat. Effective but interrupts more than the other methods.
- **Cold.** A cold hand, cold air, or (for the committed) a cold wet cloth nearby. Sympathetic shock; very effective; not subtle.
- **Mental shift.** Deliberately think of something neutral for 10 seconds. Works but pulls you out of the experience; less desirable than the physical methods.
- **Eyes open / look away from material.** If using visual material, removing it drops arousal reliably.

**What NOT to do:** the **Kegel squeeze at the edge** (contracting the pelvic floor hard) is often recommended online. It works for some men, but for many, a hard Kegel at 9 *triggers* ejaculation rather than preventing it, because pelvic floor contraction is part of the ejaculatory sequence. Reverse Kegel (relaxation/bearing down) is more reliable. Experiment carefully at 8, not 9.

## 9.5 Beyond stop-start: continuous edging

Once stop-start is reliable, the advanced form is **modulated stimulation** — never fully stopping, but adjusting technique to hold arousal in the 8–9 band continuously:

- At 9, shift from fast to glacial, or from glans to base, or from stroke to static hold, or from firm to barely-touching. Arousal drops to 8 while stimulation continues.
- At 8, gradually increase again.
- Hold in the 8–9 band for 5, 10, 20, 30+ minutes.

This is more pleasurable than stop-start (no gaps) and more demanding. It requires fine-grained awareness of arousal and a repertoire of techniques with different intensities (Chapter 6). It is also the state in which most of the phenomena in Chapter 12 (non-ejaculatory orgasm, whole-body arousal) become accessible.

**The "wave" pattern.** Many men describe the experience as riding waves: arousal surges toward 9, they ease off, it drops to 8, they build, it surges. Each wave is itself pleasurable — the surge, the near-miss, the ease. After enough waves, the boundaries between them blur and arousal becomes a sustained, high, spreading sensation that's qualitatively different from the sharp, localized build of a quick session.

## 9.6 Edging and pelvic floor state

The pelvic floor is central to this. At 8.5–9, the pelvic floor is contracting involuntarily. Two things follow:

1. **If you can relax the pelvic floor at 9, you can hold at 9 much longer.** The reverse Kegel is the key. It takes practice because the reflex is strong. Chapter 10 has the progression.
2. **If you deliberately contract the pelvic floor while stimulation is low (6–7), you can raise arousal without touching.** Rhythmic Kegels during a pause keep arousal from dropping too far and are pleasurable in themselves.

The skilled edger uses the pelvic floor as a throttle: relax to slow down, pulse to speed up.

## 9.7 Ruined orgasms and near-misses

**Ruined orgasm**: stimulation stops at or just after 9.5. Emission has begun; ejaculation occurs, but without the full expulsion contractions, or with weak ones; semen may dribble rather than spurt; the orgasm sensation is muted, partial, or absent; and the refractory period usually kicks in anyway. Unpleasant for most men (though a deliberate practice in some kink contexts). It's the cost of overshooting.

**If you overshoot:** either resume stimulation immediately and have a full orgasm, or accept the ruined one. Trying to "hold" at 9.5 doesn't work — the process is already running.

**Near-miss (9.0–9.4 and back)**: this is the goal state. The body was almost there; it wasn't; arousal drops back to 8. Each near-miss is intensely pleasurable — many men rate the near-miss itself as more pleasurable than a quick orgasm.

## 9.8 Edging for premature ejaculation

Premature ejaculation (PE) is defined clinically as ejaculation within about one minute of penetration (lifelong PE) or a bothersome reduction in latency (acquired PE), with inability to delay, and distress [ISSM 2014]. It affects perhaps 20–30% of men by self-report; 3–5% by strict criteria. Chapter 16 covers it fully; here, the training component.

The stop-start and squeeze methods were developed *for PE* and remain the first-line behavioral treatment. Solo practice is where it's learned:

**Program (adapt as needed):**

- **Weeks 1–2.** Stop-start with hand, lubricated, 3 sessions/week. Goal: identify 8 and 8.5 reliably. Stop at 8. Three cycles, then finish.
- **Weeks 3–4.** Stop at 8.5. Five cycles. Add long-exhale and body relaxation at each stop.
- **Weeks 5–6.** Stop at 9. Five to eight cycles. Add reverse Kegel at each stop.
- **Weeks 7–8.** Continuous edging: don't fully stop, modulate instead. Hold 8–9 for a target time (start at 3 minutes; extend).
- **Ongoing.** Vary technique (sleeve, different grips, thrusting motion — which mimics intercourse and matters for transfer). Practice with less-lubricated, warmer, more enveloping stimulation as you approach partnered conditions.

Combined with pelvic floor training (Chapter 10), which has independent evidence in lifelong PE [Pastore 2014 — 82.5% of men achieved control after 12 weeks], this is the core of non-pharmacological PE treatment. Many men also benefit from combining with medication (SSRIs, topical anesthetics) during the learning phase; a doctor can advise.

**Transfer to partnered sex** requires practicing under conditions closer to the real thing: a warm, lubricated sleeve; thrusting rather than stroking; higher arousal from fantasy/anticipation. And it requires communicating with a partner about pausing. Partnered sex is harder than solo — higher arousal, less control over stimulation — so solo control is necessary but not sufficient.

## 9.9 Edging for delayed ejaculation — a caution

Men with delayed ejaculation (difficulty reaching orgasm) should *not* focus on edging in the usual sense — they already don't reach 9 easily. For them, the training is the reverse: identifying what *does* raise arousal, expanding it, and reducing the self-monitoring that inhibits orgasm. Chapter 16 covers this. The arousal-scale awareness taught here is still useful — many DE men have never noticed they plateau at 7 and never learned what gets them to 8.

## 9.10 Long sessions: practicalities

Sessions of 45–120 minutes are common among men who practice edging seriously. Things to know:

- **Erection will fluctuate.** It softens during pauses and returns. Normal.
- **Skin needs lube throughout.** Reapply. Long sessions are where friction damage happens.
- **Hand fatigue is real.** Alternate hands; use a sleeve for part; use non-friction techniques (squeeze-release, static hold, pelvic floor pulses) for stretches.
- **Hydrate.** You're sweating and breathing hard.
- **Position changes** help with fatigue and blood flow. Don't stay hunched.
- **Prostate "fullness."** After extended edging, many men notice a heavy, full, or mildly aching sensation in the perineum/prostate area — the seminal vesicles and prostate are engorged. It resolves with ejaculation or over several hours. Mild "blue balls" (testicular ache from prolonged arousal — technically vasocongestion) is real and harmless; it resolves with ejaculation or time. If pain is significant or persists for more than a few hours after a session, that's not normal — see Chapter 16.
- **Time distortion.** Immersion at 8–9 alters time perception; an hour can feel like twenty minutes. Set an alarm if you have somewhere to be.
- **Diminishing returns.** Beyond a certain point (individual — often 60–90 minutes), sensation plateaus or dulls, the skin tires, and the eventual orgasm isn't further improved. Longer isn't automatically better.
- **The compulsivity check.** Long edging sessions, especially with continuous visual material, are where "gooning" lives (Chapter 15). Occasional long sessions are a legitimate pleasure. Daily multi-hour sessions that displace sleep, work, or relationships are a pattern to examine. Know the difference.

## 9.11 The orgasm after edging

Consistently, men report that the orgasm following an extended edging session is:

- **Longer** — more contractions (15–25 vs. 10–15).
- **More forceful** — greater ejaculate propulsion, from a longer build-up of seminal fluid and a "primed" pelvic floor.
- **More whole-body** — sensation spreading to thighs, abdomen, chest rather than staying in the pelvis.
- **Preceded by a longer "peak"** — the 9–9.5 phase stretches to several seconds rather than being instantaneous.
- **Followed by a deeper afterglow** — more profound relaxation, sometimes trembling or emotional release.

The physiological rationale: prolonged plateau maximizes seminal vesicle and prostate filling, maximizes pelvic floor priming, maximizes neurochemical build-up (dopamine, anticipatory), and — because you've been practicing awareness — you're *present* for the orgasm rather than surprised by it.

**How to finish after edging:** it's a choice. Fast and firm produces a sharp, explosive orgasm. Slow and light — continuing the same glacial strokes past the edge — produces a longer, rolling one. Adding perineal pressure or prostate stimulation at the moment of orgasm intensifies both (Chapter 12). Contracting the pelvic floor hard at the first contraction intensifies force; relaxing it lets the orgasm spread. Experiment.

## 9.12 Practice notes

- **Start with the hand, not toys.** Toys deliver stimulation you don't control finely; learn the scale with your hand first.
- **Lube always.** Edging without lube is how frenulum tears happen.
- **Don't edge every session.** Quick sessions have their place, and edging every time can make orgasm without a long build-up feel inadequate (a mild version of the conditioning problem). Vary.
- **Journal** (Chapter 21). Note how many cycles, how close you got, which signals you noticed, how the orgasm compared. Progress is fast when tracked.
- **Frustration is normal early.** Overshooting, ruining orgasms, and stopping too early all happen. Two to three weeks of consistent practice resolves most of it.

---

### Key takeaways

- **Edging** = approach orgasm, back off, repeat. It's the pleasure practice and the PE treatment in one.
- Learn your **signals** for 8, 8.5, 9, and 9.5: breath, testicular elevation, involuntary pelvic pulses, glans focus, the "inevitability" feeling.
- **Stop early** while learning (8.5). Overshooting to 9.5 = ruined orgasm.
- **Drop arousal** with long exhale, full-body relaxation, reverse Kegel, testicle traction, zone change, squeeze. Avoid hard Kegels at 9 — they can trigger ejaculation.
- Progress from **stop-start** to **continuous modulated edging** in the 8–9 band.
- **The pelvic floor is the throttle**: relax to slow, pulse to speed.
- Edged orgasms are **longer, stronger, more whole-body**. How you finish (fast/slow, Kegel/relax, added perineal pressure) shapes the orgasm.
- Long sessions: lube, alternate hands, hydrate, expect erection fluctuation, and watch for diminishing returns and compulsive patterns.
