# Chapter 13 — Toys, Sleeves & Devices

Tools extend what hands can do: they deliver sensations (vibration, enveloping pressure, internal stimulation) that hands can't, they free the hands for other zones, and they rest the hands during long sessions. They also introduce material safety, hygiene, and cost considerations. This chapter is a buyer's and user's guide organized by category, with a materials-safety primer first, because the material matters more than the brand.

We don't recommend specific products or brands — models change, and this guide should stay useful. We describe categories, features, and what to look for.

## 13.1 Materials: the most important decision

A toy touches mucosal or near-mucosal tissue, sometimes internally, for extended periods. What it's made of determines whether it's safe, whether it can be cleaned, and how long it lasts.

### Body-safe, non-porous (can be fully sanitized)

- **Platinum-cure silicone.** The gold standard for soft toys. Non-porous, hypoallergenic, phthalate-free, temperature-responsive, can be boiled or bleached. Feels smooth-matte or slightly grippy. Ranges from very soft to firm. Downside: cost; and shouldn't be used with silicone lube (surface degradation). Watch for: "silicone blend" or "silicone-like" — often a TPE mix. Pure silicone doesn't have a strong chemical smell.
- **Borosilicate glass.** Non-porous, hard, holds temperature (warm or chill it), essentially indestructible in normal use (drop-tested; check for chips before each use). Excellent for prostate toys and temperature play.
- **Stainless steel (surgical grade).** Non-porous, heavy, holds temperature, firm pressure ideal for prostate. Expensive. Excellent.
- **ABS plastic.** Hard, non-porous, common for vibrator bodies and bullet vibrators. Safe.
- **Aluminum (anodized).** Non-porous; used in some premium toys. Fine.
- **Ceramic (glazed).** Non-porous if the glaze is intact. Check for cracks.

### Non-toxic but porous (cannot be fully sanitized; degrade over time)

- **TPE / TPR (thermoplastic elastomer/rubber).** The material of most strokers/sleeves (including the major brands' "realistic" skin materials — all their proprietary names are TPE variants). Very soft, very realistic feel, inexpensive. **Porous**: bacteria and mold colonize the interior over time and can't be fully removed. Requires meticulous drying and regular replacement. Not to be shared. Fine for external use by one person with good hygiene.
- **"Cyberskin" / "UR3" / "Real Feel" and similar.** Soft TPE/PVC blends. Same story: porous, hard to dry, shorter lifespan, often oily. Frequent replacement.
- **Latex rubber.** Porous; allergenic for some; degrades. Uncommon in quality toys now.
- **Elastomer blends (unspecified "rubber").** Assume porous.

### Avoid

- **Jelly / "jelly rubber" / soft PVC.** Historically the cheapest toy material. Porous, often contains **phthalates** (plasticizers linked to endocrine disruption; a Danish EPA study found phthalates and other volatile compounds in a range of jelly/PVC toys), and often has a strong chemical smell. The smell is the tell. Not regulated as medical devices in most countries, so "phthalate-free" claims are unverified. Never internal. Ideally never at all.
- **Anything with a strong chemical or "shower curtain" smell.**
- **"Novelty use only" labeling** — a legal dodge that means the maker takes no responsibility for body contact.
- **Unlabeled materials from anonymous marketplace sellers.**

### The porous-toy rule

If it's porous (TPE, jelly, cyberskin), use it with a condom for internal use, dry it completely after every wash, store it dry, and replace it on a schedule (every 6–12 months for regular use, sooner if it discolors, smells, gets sticky, or develops tears). If you see dark spots inside a sleeve, that's mold — throw it out.

### Testing at home

- **Smell.** Body-safe silicone has almost no odor; TPE has a faint rubbery smell; jelly/PVC has a strong chemical smell.
- **The flame test** (folk method): touching a lighter to silicone leaves white ash and no melting; TPE/PVC melts and smokes. Roughly indicative, not reliable, and ruins the spot you test. Don't rely on it.
- **Price.** Pure silicone toys are rarely very cheap. Very cheap "silicone" is usually a blend.
- **Buy from reputable retailers** who publish materials and stand behind them.

## 13.2 Strokers and sleeves

A tube or sleeve, usually TPE, that the penis moves inside. The largest category of men's toys.

### Types

- **Open-ended sleeves.** Short, penetrable from both ends. Cheap; easy to clean and dry; portable; less "enveloping."
- **Closed-end sleeves in a case (the canister style).** A long TPE sleeve inside a hard case with an end cap that adjusts suction. The most popular format. Most realistic; heavier; more cleaning effort; suction is a distinct sensation.
- **Textured vs. smooth.** Internal textures range from realistic (ribs, bumps) to abstract. More texture = more sensation but also more to clean. Very intense textures can be too much for some men and are often best with an overlubed, slow approach.
- **Orifice styles.** Realistic (modeled on anatomy) or non-representational. Purely preference.
- **Tightness.** Varies. Tighter isn't better — very tight sleeves reduce lube flow and increase friction.
- **Automatic strokers.** Motorized; move the sleeve for you at adjustable speed/patterns. Hands-free. Some sync to video or partner devices. Higher cost, more maintenance. Sensation can become monotonous (machine rhythm doesn't vary); the best have irregular patterns.
- **Vibrating strokers.** Sleeve with an embedded motor. The vibration transfers through the TPE, muted.
- **Suction / "blowjob" machines.** Combine sleeve motion with suction cycles. Distinct sensation.
- **Silicone strokers.** Firmer, less realistic, but non-porous and indefinitely cleanable. Growing category.

### Using them well

- **Lube generously** — much more than for hand use. Water-based (standard; silicone lube may degrade some TPE — check the maker). Keep a spray bottle of water nearby to reactivate water-based lube.
- **Warm it.** Cold TPE is unpleasant. Soak the sleeve in warm water for 5 minutes beforehand (or use the maker's warming rod). This is a large upgrade in sensation.
- **Slow.** The instinct is to use a stroker fast. Try slow — one stroke per 2–3 seconds — and feel the texture pass over the corona.
- **Vary the grip on the case** — loose and tight change the internal pressure.
- **Twist** the sleeve as it moves.
- **Adjust suction** (if there's an end cap): more suction on the outstroke is a distinct sensation.
- **Thrust into it** rather than moving it — different muscle engagement, closer to intercourse.
- **Combine** with a hand on the perineum, scrotum, or a prostate toy.
- **For premature ejaculation training**, a warm, lubricated sleeve is the closest solo simulation of intercourse. Use it for stop-start practice.
- **For delayed ejaculation / death-grip retraining**, a sleeve provides moderate, enveloping pressure that's hard to turn into a "death grip."

### Cleaning and care (TPE)

1. Remove sleeve from case. Rinse with warm (not hot — TPE deforms) water, flushing through both ends.
2. Use a toy cleaner or very mild unscented soap. **No soap for some brands** — they say it degrades the material; follow the maker. Isopropyl alcohol (70%) rinse is used by some for disinfection; it dries the material somewhat over time.
3. Rinse thoroughly.
4. **Dry completely.** This is the critical step. Pat the outside; hang or prop upright so air flows through; use a fan; or use a dedicated drying stick. Takes hours. A sleeve put away damp grows mold.
5. Once dry, dust lightly with **cornstarch** (not talc — talc is a respiratory and possible carcinogenic concern; not flour — feeds bacteria) to restore the soft texture. Some makers sell "renewal powder" — it's cornstarch.
6. Store in the case or a breathable bag, away from other toys (TPE reacts with other soft materials), out of heat.

Replace when: sticky, discolored, torn, smelly, or showing any dark spots.

## 13.3 Vibrators for men

Vibration recruits Pacinian receptors that hands can't stimulate efficiently (Chapter 11). Men are late adopters, largely due to marketing, but the sensation is categorically different and worth exploring.

### Types

- **Bullet vibrators.** Small, cheap, versatile. Held against the frenulum, glans, shaft, perineum, nipples. The best starting point.
- **Wand vibrators.** Large, powerful, rumbly (low-frequency, deep). Held against the shaft or perineum, or with the penis pressed between the wand and the abdomen. Very strong; can produce orgasm hands-free; can cause numbness with prolonged use — take breaks.
- **Penis-specific vibrators.** Shaped to cradle the glans, ring the shaft, or wrap the frenulum. Often the "hands-free" category.
- **Vibrating rings.** A stretchy ring with a motor, worn at the base. Mild sensation for the wearer; mostly a partner toy.
- **Vibrating prostate massagers.** See 13.5.
- **Vibrating strokers.** See 13.2.
- **Glans-cup vibrators.** Cup-shaped, sit over the head. Intense; often with multiple patterns.

### What matters

- **Rumbly vs. buzzy.** Low-frequency, high-amplitude ("rumbly") vibration penetrates deeper and feels richer. High-frequency, low-amplitude ("buzzy") feels surface-level and causes numbness faster. Rumbly is generally better for men. Look for reviews using these terms.
- **Patterns.** Steady, pulsing, escalating, random. Random/irregular patterns fight habituation.
- **Power range.** You want the low end to be genuinely low (the frenulum is sensitive) and the high end to be strong.
- **Waterproofing.** Useful for cleaning and shower use.
- **Rechargeable vs. battery.** Rechargeable is better long-term.
- **Noise.** Varies enormously; matters for privacy.
- **Material.** Silicone or ABS. Nothing else.

### Using them well

- **Start low, on the frenulum**, with a light touch — not pressed hard. Move it. A stationary vibrator on one spot causes numbness and habituation within a minute or two.
- **Through fabric or a hand** to mute it if too intense.
- **On the perineum** during penile stimulation — one of the best combinations.
- **On the shaft base / pubic bone** — deep vibration transmits into the roots.
- **On the scrotum** — gently; many men love this.
- **Nipples.**
- **Hands-free orgasm**: a wand or cradle-style vibrator held in place (pillow, thighs) on the frenulum/underside of the glans, with no stroking. The orgasm from pure vibration is different — often described as building slower, then very intense, with more involuntary contraction. Some men find it too intense; some find it the best thing they've tried.
- **Numbness**: vibrators cause transient numbness with prolonged direct use — this is normal and resolves in minutes to an hour. If it persists, reduce intensity and duration. Chronic heavy vibrator use can raise the stimulation threshold (a variant of the conditioning issue); rotate with hand sessions.

## 13.4 Rings (constriction rings, "cock rings")

A ring worn around the base of the penis (and sometimes behind the scrotum too) that restricts venous outflow, making the erection firmer, fuller, and longer-lasting, and increasing glans sensitivity. Also delays ejaculation for some men and intensifies orgasm for others (the ring compresses the urethra, which changes the sensation of ejaculation).

### Types

- **Stretchy (silicone, TPE).** Safe for beginners. Can always be stretched off.
- **Adjustable (lasso/snap style).** Adjustable tightness with a quick release.
- **Rigid (metal, glass, hard plastic).** Firmer sensation; the most "effective"; also the ones that get stuck. For experienced users who know their size (measure the flaccid circumference at the base, add a little — a rigid ring must go on flaccid or semi-erect and come off flaccid).
- **Vibrating rings.** Covered above.
- **Ring + ball strap combos.** Around the shaft and behind the scrotum. More restriction; more to go wrong.

### Rules (non-negotiable)

- **Maximum 20–30 minutes.** Set a timer.
- **Remove immediately** at pain, numbness, coldness, purple/blue color, or if the erection becomes uncomfortably hard.
- **Never sleep with one on.** Never wear under alcohol or sedation (you won't notice the warning signs).
- **Never a rigid ring you haven't sized.** Stuck rigid rings require emergency departments and tools.
- **Lube the ring** for application and removal.
- **Start with stretchy.** Rigid rings are a later step, if at all.

### Sensation

Many men find rings enhance solo sessions modestly — firmer erection, more sensitive glans, a "full" feeling. Others find them uncomfortable or that they blunt the orgasm. Try a cheap stretchy one before investing.

## 13.5 Prostate massagers and anal toys

### Types

- **Prostate massagers (contoured).** Shaped with a curved shaft, a bulbous head to reach the prostate, a narrow neck for the sphincter to grip, a flared base with a perineum arm that presses the external spot. Designed to be held in place by the pelvic floor and worked hands-free by contracting/releasing. The classic design. Sizes from small (beginner) to large.
- **Vibrating prostate massagers.** Same shape plus motors — sometimes in the head, the base, and the perineum arm. Remote-controlled options exist. Vibration on the prostate is a distinct sensation that many men find far easier to build arousal from than static pressure.
- **"Come-hither" / stroking massagers.** Motorized head that moves in a beckoning motion, mimicking a finger. Newer category; strong reports.
- **Butt plugs.** Fullness without specific prostate targeting. Useful for beginners to adapt to the sensation, and during penile stimulation for the added dimension.
- **Anal beads.** Graduated spheres; the sensation is in insertion and especially removal at orgasm.
- **Dildos (curved).** For men who prefer motion to static pressure; needs a hand.
- **Glass and steel prostate toys.** Firm, heavy, temperature-holding. Excellent for firm, precise pressure. Not for beginners due to firmness.
- **Inflatable plugs.** Adjustable fullness. Advanced.

### Choosing your first

- **Small.** Diameter 2.5–3 cm (1–1.2 in) at the widest. Beginners overestimate.
- **Silicone.** Non-porous, some flex.
- **Flared base**, obviously.
- **Contoured prostate design** if prostate pleasure is the goal; **small plug** if adaptation is the goal.
- **Vibration** is optional but helpful for beginners — it "shows" you where the prostate is.
- **Budget $30–80** for a good silicone beginner massager. Expensive premium models are for later.

### Using them

Everything in Chapter 5.7 and 12.1 applies. Specifically:

- Arousal 7+ before insertion. Heavily lubricated toy and opening. Slow. Press, wait for the sphincter to release on an exhale, ease in. Once past the widest point, the neck sits in the sphincter and the toy stays.
- **Lie on your side** with knees drawn up (easiest insertion), then move to your back or knees.
- **Pelvic floor contractions** move the head against the prostate. Rhythmic contract-release is the primary "technique." Add hip rocking.
- **Don't pull it in and out rapidly** at first — the sensation is about pressure and pelvic floor movement, not thrusting.
- **Combine with penile stimulation** for the first many sessions.
- **Sessions of 20–45 minutes** for prostate development.
- **Remove slowly**, on an exhale, relaxed.
- **Wash immediately** with warm water and soap (silicone) or boil/bleach-solution periodically.

## 13.6 Other categories

- **Penis pumps (vacuum).** Medical (ED) or pleasure. Use with a gauge and limit; 10–15 minutes; stop at pain or dark discoloration. Produces a temporary swollen, hypersensitive state some men enjoy. See Chapter 12.5.
- **Nipple toys.** Clamps (adjustable, start loose), suction cups, small vibrators. Nipple sensitivity develops with use.
- **Textured gloves and touch tools.** Fleece, silk, faux-fur mitts, soft brushes, feather ticklers, Wartenberg pinwheels (light, gently). Cheap, low-tech, and effective for sensory layering (Chapter 11).
- **Temperature tools.** Glass and steel toys warmed or chilled; warm damp cloths; ice (briefly, never directly on the glans for long). A hot-water bottle on the abdomen.
- **Massage oils and candles** (low-temp massage candles) for non-genital warm-up.
- **Blindfolds, headphones.** Sensory reduction.
- **Restraints (solo).** Limited utility alone and a real safety concern — never anything you can't release instantly with one hand.
- **Masturbation "machines" / sex machines.** Thrusting devices for anal use or with a sleeve. Expensive; niche; follow safety instructions.
- **Interactive / teledildonic devices.** Sync with content or a partner remotely. Data privacy concerns (Chapter 20).
- **Penis extenders / traction devices.** Medical use for Peyronie's; consumer use for "enlargement" — modest gains in length (~1–2 cm) are documented with months of daily traction [Nikoobakht 2011 and others], with significant time commitment and some risk. Not a pleasure device; mentioned for completeness.
- **Ball stretchers / weights.** Not recommended (Chapter 12.5).
- **Sounds.** Not recommended (Chapter 12.5).

## 13.7 Hygiene and storage

- **Clean before and after** every use. Non-porous: soap + warm water; periodically boil (silicone, glass, steel — check motors aren't in the boiled part) or soak in 10% bleach solution then rinse thoroughly. Porous: warm water + toy cleaner; dry completely; replace on schedule.
- **Dry fully** before storage. Moisture is the enemy.
- **Store separately.** Different soft materials can react and degrade each other when touching (especially TPE against TPE or silicone). Individual cloth bags.
- **Away from heat and sunlight.**
- **Check before each use** for tears, cracks, sticky surfaces, discoloration, odor. Discard damaged toys.
- **Don't share** porous toys, ever. Non-porous toys can be shared with a condom or after full disinfection.
- **Lube compatibility**: water-based with everything; silicone lube not on silicone toys; oil not on TPE or latex.

## 13.8 Buying guide summary

| Goal | First purchase | Later |
|---|---|---|
| Different sensation than hand | Bullet vibrator (silicone/ABS) | Wand; glans cup |
| Intercourse-like feel; PE training; DE retraining | Open-ended TPE sleeve or canister stroker | Automatic stroker; silicone stroker |
| Prostate exploration | Small silicone contoured massager (with or without vibration) | Come-hither massager; steel/glass |
| Firmer erection; different orgasm | Stretchy silicone ring | Adjustable; rigid (sized) |
| Sensory layering | Cheap touch tools (fleece mitt, soft brush), bullet vibe | Nipple toys, temperature toys |
| Hands-free | Wand or cradle-style vibrator | Automatic stroker; vibrating prostate massager |

**Where to buy:** reputable specialty retailers (online or physical) that list materials, have return policies, and don't sell jelly/PVC. Avoid anonymous marketplace sellers for anything internal or soft.

**What to spend:** a good beginner kit — bullet vibe, stretchy ring, open sleeve, small prostate massager, body-safe lube — runs $100–200. You don't need more to explore every category in this chapter.

---

### Key takeaways

- **Material first.** Silicone, glass, steel, ABS = non-porous, sanitizable, safe. TPE = porous, fine for external solo use with strict drying and replacement. Jelly/PVC = avoid.
- **Strokers**: lube generously, warm them, go slow, dry completely after washing, cornstarch to restore, replace when degraded.
- **Vibrators**: choose rumbly over buzzy; start low on the frenulum; keep it moving; use on the perineum and scrotum; take breaks to avoid numbness.
- **Rings**: stretchy first; 20–30 minutes max; remove at any warning sign; never sleep in one; never an unsized rigid ring.
- **Prostate massagers**: small, silicone, flared, contoured; arousal 7+ before insertion; pelvic floor contractions do the work; combine with penile stimulation.
- **Clean, dry, store separately, inspect, replace.** Don't share porous toys.

# Chapter 14 — Fantasy, Erotica & Pornography

Most male masturbation involves erotic material of some kind — internal fantasy, memories, written erotica, audio, images, or video. Pornography specifically is the most contested subject in this guide: one camp says it causes erectile dysfunction, addiction, and brain damage; another says the harms are moral panic with no evidence. Both are partly wrong. This chapter lays out what's actually known, then gives practical guidance for using erotic material in a way that serves pleasure and sexual health rather than undermining them.

## 14.1 Fantasy

### What it is and why it works

Sexual fantasy is deliberate or spontaneous mental imagery with erotic content. Nearly everyone has them — surveys find over 95% of men and women report sexual fantasies [Lehmiller 2018, *Tell Me What You Want*, survey of 4,000+ Americans]. The most common themes across genders: multi-partner sex, power/control (dominance and submission), novelty and adventure, taboo or forbidden scenarios, partner-sharing, and passion/romance.

Fantasy works because the brain's arousal circuits respond to imagined stimuli nearly as strongly as to real ones. Imagined touch activates somatosensory areas; imagined scenarios activate the same reward and anticipation systems as real ones. Fantasy is the original erotic technology.

### Fantasy vs. desire vs. behavior

A fantasy is not a wish. Lehmiller's data and decades of clinical experience are consistent: most people don't want to act out most of their fantasies, and fantasizing about something says nothing reliable about what a person would do or wants to do. Fantasies about non-consent, for example, are extremely common (in women as well as men) and have no relationship to desire for actual non-consent. Fantasies about people other than one's partner are near-universal and are not infidelity. Fantasies that conflict with one's values or identity are common and are not evidence about one's "true self."

The practical point: **fantasy content is not a moral or diagnostic issue**. What matters is whether the material is legal and whether it's serving you.

### Using fantasy deliberately

- **Fantasy is low-bandwidth.** Unlike a screen, it leaves most of your attention available for sensation. Men who move from screen-dependence back to fantasy nearly always report richer physical sensation.
- **It's infinitely customizable and infinitely novel.** No habituation problem if you're willing to be creative.
- **It can be aligned with reality.** Perelman's clinical work on delayed ejaculation identifies a "disparity between fantasy and reality" as a contributor — when the mental content required for arousal is far from anything a partner can provide, partnered sex suffers [Perelman 2016]. Including fantasies that resemble your actual or plausible sexual life keeps the arousal system connected to reality. This isn't a rule against exotic fantasy; it's a suggestion to keep the range wide.
- **It can be somatic.** Instead of a visual narrative, fantasize *sensation* — imagine a mouth, a hand, a body, and put attention on the imagined feeling in your own body. This blends fantasy with mindful attention rather than competing with it.
- **Memory** is a special case: recalling actual experiences in detail. Powerfully arousing and inherently reality-aligned.
- **Rotating** fantasies prevents the same habituation that affects any stimulus.
- **Guilt about content** is common and counterproductive. If the fantasy is legal and stays in your head, the guilt is the problem, not the fantasy.

## 14.2 Written and audio erotica

**Written erotica** (stories, literature) is the oldest recorded form. It engages imagination, is low-bandwidth relative to video, has effectively infinite variety, and is text — so it requires *active* mental participation, which many men find produces a more engaged and less passive arousal than video.

**Audio erotica** (narrated stories, guided sessions, ASMR-adjacent content, recorded improvisations) has grown enormously. It lets you close your eyes — all visual cortex freed for body attention — while still providing external content. It's a natural fit for mindful masturbation (Chapter 8). Guided audio for edging, for slow sessions, and for specific practices exists and can be genuinely useful for men learning pacing.

Both formats avoid many of the concerns raised about video pornography (below): less habituation pressure, no performer-ethics concerns with written fiction, more attention available for the body, and less "supernormal stimulus" intensity.

## 14.3 Pornography: the evidence

### Prevalence

Video pornography use is near-universal among men in countries with internet access. Studies typically find 70–95% of men report use, with most young men using weekly or more [Herbenick 2020; Bőthe 2024]. It's a normal behavior in the statistical sense. The question is not whether men use it but whether, how, and how much it causes problems.

### The claims and the evidence

**Claim: Pornography causes erectile dysfunction ("PIED").**

- Against: multiple large studies find no association between pornography use per se and erectile function [Grubbs & Gola 2019, three samples n>3,000; Landripet & Štulhofer 2015, European men]. One laboratory study found men who watched more porn had *greater* arousal responsiveness to sexual stimuli [Prause & Pfaus 2015]. A 2024 narrative review concluded the evidence for porn as a cause of ED is inconsistent, with some studies showing associations with *better* function [Rowland 2024].
- For: consistent cross-sectional associations between *problematic* or *compulsive* pornography use (as distinct from frequency) and ED [Grubbs & Gola 2019; Jacobs 2021 n≈3,400]. Clinical case series describe young men with situational ED (fine with porn, not with a partner) that improved when they reduced pornography [Park 2016 — a much-cited but methodologically weak paper]. Rising rates of ED in young men in some surveys, temporally coinciding with high-speed internet porn — a correlation with many candidate explanations.
- **Assessment:** "porn-induced ED" as a distinct medical entity is not established. The best-supported picture: for most men, porn has no effect on erectile function. For some men, a *pattern* — compulsive use, extreme reliance on porn for arousal, conditioning to specific stimuli, and/or the anxiety and shame that accompany self-perceived problematic use — contributes to situational difficulties. The mechanism is likely psychological (conditioning, performance anxiety, attention) more than neurological "damage." Reversibility is generally good.

**Claim: Pornography is addictive / causes "brain changes like drugs."**

- The imaging studies cited for this (e.g., Kühn & Gallinat 2014 — smaller striatal volume in heavier users; Voon 2014 — cue reactivity in "compulsive" users similar to drug cue reactivity) are small, cross-sectional, and can't distinguish cause from effect or from pre-existing traits. All rewarding activities produce "brain changes" — that's what learning is. The question is whether the changes are dysfunctional.
- The DSM-5 does not include pornography or sex addiction. The ICD-11 includes **Compulsive Sexual Behaviour Disorder** as an *impulse control* disorder, not an addiction, and its authors explicitly note that the evidence doesn't yet support an addiction model [Kraus 2018].
- **Assessment:** some men develop compulsive patterns of pornography use that meet clinical criteria for CSBD and cause real harm (Chapter 15). Whether this is "addiction" in the neurobiological sense is unresolved and partly semantic. The behavioral reality — difficulty controlling use despite negative consequences — is real for an estimated 3–10% of users, and the language matters less than the help.

**Claim: Pornography causes escalation to more extreme content.**

- Habituation to any stimulus is real (Chapter 3). Some users report seeking progressively more novel or intense content. Survey data find that a minority report this pattern; most users' preferences are stable over time [e.g., Bőthe 2021]. Escalation is more associated with problematic use than with use per se.
- **Assessment:** escalation is a real pattern for some users, driven by the novelty-seeking of the dopamine system, and is a reasonable thing to monitor in oneself. It is not inevitable.

**Claim: Pornography harms relationships.**

- Mixed. Some studies find associations between solo porn use and lower relationship/sexual satisfaction, especially when use is secretive or when partners disapprove; other studies find no effect or positive effects when use is shared or open [Kohut 2017; Willoughby 2016]. Effects are heavily moderated by relationship context, communication, and moral attitudes.
- **Assessment:** secrecy and value conflict cause more harm than use. Open communication about porn in a relationship is protective.

**Claim: Pornography shapes sexual expectations and scripts unrealistically.**

- Well-supported for adolescents and for men with little partnered experience. Porn depicts sex that is performative, non-representative in bodies, duration, acts, and responses. Men who use it as a sexual education source hold more distorted expectations [Rothman 2021 and others].
- **Assessment:** true and important, especially for younger men. Porn is fiction; treating it as documentary is the error.

**Claim: "Porn is harmless."**

- Overstated in the other direction. The evidence shows *most* use is not associated with measurable harm; it doesn't show that no use is ever harmful. Compulsive patterns, conditioning effects, attention displacement, expectation distortion, and ethical concerns about production are all real for a subset of users and content.

### The "supernormal stimulus" argument

A more nuanced version of the concern, independent of "addiction": internet pornography provides unlimited novelty (infinite partners, infinite scenarios) with zero effort, at intensity levels (visual, auditory, editing) that exceed anything in real sexual life. The dopamine system responds to novelty and prediction of reward; a stimulus engineered for maximal novelty is a **supernormal stimulus** — like junk food is to hunger. The concern is not damage but *comparison*: real partners and real sensation compete with an artificially intensified reference point.

This argument is plausible and consistent with basic neuroscience; it's also hard to test directly. Its practical implication is the same as the conditioning argument: **variety in the type of stimulus, including stimulus-free sessions, keeps the reference point realistic.**

### The moral-incongruence finding

One of the most robust findings in this literature: **the distress associated with pornography use is largely predicted by the gap between a person's use and their moral beliefs about it**, not by the amount of use [Grubbs 2019, "Pornography Problems Due to Moral Incongruence" — meta-analytic review]. Religious men who use porn report far more "addiction" and distress at the same or lower frequencies than non-religious men. This doesn't mean their distress is fake — it means the problem is often the conflict, not the behavior, and that resolving it may involve examining the belief as much as changing the behavior. ICD-11 explicitly excludes moral-conflict distress from the CSBD diagnosis for this reason.

## 14.4 Practical guidance

Given the above, here's what a reasonable man should do.

### Principles

1. **Porn is optional.** Many men use it, many don't, both are fine. It is not necessary for good masturbation, and Chapters 8 and 11 argue that screen-free sessions are generally *more* pleasurable once you adapt.
2. **Use, don't be used.** The design of pornography platforms (infinite scroll, autoplay, algorithmic recommendation) is optimized for time-on-site, not for your pleasure or well-being. Choosing content deliberately and then *stopping browsing* is different from letting the platform drive.
3. **Attention is the cost.** Every minute watching is a minute not feeling. The trade is sometimes worth it; it shouldn't be the default for the whole session.
4. **Keep the range wide.** Screen sessions, fantasy sessions, audio sessions, eyes-closed sessions, mindful sessions. If you can't get aroused or reach orgasm without a screen, the range has narrowed — widen it.
5. **Watch for the pattern, not the number.** Compulsive use looks like: using when you don't want to; escalating time or intensity; failing to stop when you intend to; using to avoid feelings; continuing despite consequences; distress. Frequency alone doesn't define it (Chapter 15).
6. **Porn is fiction.** Bodies, durations, acts, responses, and dynamics are performed. It teaches nothing reliable about real sex.

### Techniques for hybrid use

- **Front-load.** Use material to get to arousal 6–7, then close it, close your eyes, and spend the plateau and orgasm on sensation. Most men who try this find the orgasm markedly stronger.
- **Alternate.** One minute watching, two minutes eyes closed. The material becomes a periodic input rather than a continuous one.
- **Audio only.** Listen; don't watch. Eyes closed throughout.
- **Single piece, no browsing.** Choose one thing beforehand. Watch it. Don't open a second tab. Browsing *is* the compulsive mechanic — the search for the "perfect" clip is a novelty-seeking loop that can consume the entire session and dilute arousal.
- **Still images or written content** over video: lower intensity, more imagination, more attention left over.
- **Schedule screen-free sessions** — at least one in three, or one week per month. Not as penance; as range maintenance.
- **No porn during edging plateau.** Use it for the build if you like; then put it away. The plateau is for the body.
- **Never porn as the "finishing move" every time.** If orgasm always coincides with a screen, the screen becomes the conditioned trigger.

### If you think you have a problem

Signs worth taking seriously:

- You've tried to cut back and couldn't.
- Use has escalated in time, frequency, or content intensity, and you're not comfortable with the trajectory.
- You need porn to get or stay aroused, including with a partner.
- You're using it to manage emotions (loneliness, anxiety, boredom) more than for pleasure.
- It's displacing sleep, work, social life, or partnered sex.
- You feel worse afterward, consistently.
- You're hiding the extent of it.

If several apply: Chapter 15 covers self-assessment and strategies. Briefly: a structured break (2–6 weeks) from pornography specifically — not necessarily from masturbation — is the most commonly effective first step. During the break, masturbate with fantasy and attention to sensation. Most men find that arousal to non-screen stimuli returns within 2–4 weeks and that the break recalibrates their relationship to the material. If the break isn't possible or doesn't help, a sex therapist (AASECT-certified or equivalent) is the right resource; porn-problem-specific programs vary widely in quality and some are religiously motivated in ways that add shame — choose carefully.

**A note on "reboots" and NoFap.** Extended abstinence from both porn and masturbation is the approach of large online communities. The evidence: no physiological benefits from abstinence (Chapter 4); the psychological benefits reported by many participants are real experiences that most likely come from breaking a compulsive pattern, community support, and redirected attention. The costs: rigid, all-or-nothing frameworks; shame on "relapse"; unsupported pseudoscience (the testosterone claims, "brain rewiring" timelines like "90 days"); and, in some communities, misogyny and moral panic. If a structured break helps you, take one — but a break from *porn* and *compulsive patterns* is what most men actually need, and masturbation itself isn't the problem.

## 14.5 Ethics of content

This guide is about health and pleasure, but content choices have ethical dimensions worth naming:

- **Legality.** Content involving minors is illegal everywhere and causes direct harm. Content depicting real non-consent is illegal in most places. This is absolute.
- **Consent of performers.** Professional studio content generally involves consenting adult performers with contracts; amateur content uploaded without consent ("revenge porn," leaked content, hidden cameras) does not. The proliferation of non-consensual content on free tube sites is well-documented. Paying for content from performers or studios that verify consent, or using platforms with strong verification, addresses this.
- **Labor conditions.** Vary enormously. Performer-owned platforms and content have grown and give more control to the people in it.
- **AI-generated content.** Emerging ethical and legal issues around likeness, consent, and training data. Deepfakes of real people without consent are harmful and increasingly illegal.
- **Written fiction and audio** involving only adult creators have the fewest ethical concerns.

Chapter 20 covers legal and privacy aspects.

---

### Key takeaways

- **Fantasy** is near-universal, isn't a wish or a diagnosis, is low-bandwidth (leaves attention for sensation), infinitely novel, and can be kept reality-aligned. It's the original and often best erotic technology.
- **Written and audio erotica** engage imagination, leave the eyes closed, and avoid most concerns raised about video.
- **Pornography and ED**: no association for use per se; consistent association for *problematic/compulsive* use. "PIED" as a distinct disorder isn't established; conditioning and anxiety are the likely mechanisms; reversible.
- **"Addiction"**: unresolved and partly semantic. Compulsive use meeting CSBD criteria is real for an estimated 3–10%. **Moral incongruence** predicts distress better than frequency.
- **Porn is fiction** and a poor sex educator. **Escalation** is a real but not inevitable pattern.
- **Practical**: front-load material then close your eyes; no browsing; audio over video; schedule screen-free sessions; never porn as the exclusive finishing trigger.
- If use feels problematic: a **2–6 week break from porn** (not necessarily masturbation) usually recalibrates. Beyond that, a certified sex therapist.
- **Ethics**: legal, consenting adult performers; be aware that free tube sites host non-consensual content; fiction and audio have the fewest concerns.

# Chapter 15 — Frequency, Compulsivity & "Gooning": When Is It a Problem?

This guide has spent fourteen chapters teaching you to masturbate more skillfully, more pleasurably, and for longer. This chapter is the counterweight: how to know if your relationship with masturbation has become a problem, what the clinical criteria actually are, what "gooning" is and where it sits, and what to do if you need to change something. It's written without moralizing, because moralizing is the main obstacle to men getting accurate information about this.

## 15.1 Frequency is the wrong metric

Every man wonders at some point whether he does it "too much." The answer is that the number doesn't tell you.

Population data (Chapter 1, 4): men masturbate anywhere from never to several times daily; the middle of the distribution for men under 40 is a few times per week; daily is common; multiple times daily is not rare in younger men. No frequency has been associated with harm in the absence of other problems.

A man who masturbates twice a day, enjoys it, sleeps well, works, has relationships, and can skip it without distress when circumstances require does not have a problem. A man who masturbates three times a week but does it only to numb anxiety, feels terrible afterward, has tried to stop and can't, and is avoiding a partner because of it might.

**Replace "how much" with these questions:**

1. **Control.** Can I choose not to when I decide not to? When I intend to stop, do I stop?
2. **Interference.** Is it costing me sleep, work, study, relationships, money, or health?
3. **Function.** Is it displacing partnered sex I'd otherwise want, or making partnered sex harder?
4. **Motive.** Am I doing it primarily for pleasure — or primarily to escape, numb, procrastinate, or manage a mood?
5. **Enjoyment.** Is it still good? Or has it become mechanical, joyless, something I do on autopilot?
6. **Escalation.** Is the time, frequency, or intensity of content increasing in a way I'm not comfortable with?
7. **Aftermath.** How do I feel afterward, consistently? Relaxed and content — or empty, ashamed, anxious?
8. **Physical.** Pain, irritation, numbness, exhaustion?

Green on all eight: no problem, whatever the number. Red on several: worth attention, whatever the number.

## 15.2 Compulsive Sexual Behaviour Disorder: the clinical picture

In 2019, the World Health Organization included **Compulsive Sexual Behaviour Disorder (CSBD, code 6C72)** in ICD-11 under impulse control disorders. It is the only formal diagnostic category for "out of control" sexual behavior in a major classification system (the DSM-5 declined to include "hypersexual disorder"). The criteria, paraphrased [Kraus 2018; WHO ICD-11]:

> A persistent pattern of failure to control intense, repetitive sexual impulses or urges, resulting in repetitive sexual behaviour, manifested in one or more of:
> - Sexual activity becomes a central focus of life to the point of neglecting health, personal care, or other interests, activities, and responsibilities.
> - Numerous unsuccessful efforts to significantly reduce the behaviour.
> - Continued behaviour despite adverse consequences or deriving little or no satisfaction from it.
> - Continued behaviour even when the person derives little or no satisfaction from it.
>
> The pattern is manifested over an extended period (e.g., 6 months or more) and causes marked distress or significant impairment in personal, family, social, educational, occupational, or other important areas of functioning.
>
> **Distress that is entirely related to moral judgments and disapproval about sexual impulses, urges, or behaviours is not sufficient to meet this requirement.**

Several things follow:

- **It's about control and consequence, not amount.** High frequency without loss of control or harm is not CSBD.
- **Moral distress alone doesn't count.** A religious man who masturbates weekly and feels sinful about it does not have CSBD. His distress is real and deserves attention — but the clinical category is designed to exclude it, because the "problem" is the conflict between behavior and belief, and treating it as a disorder of the behavior is a category error [Grubbs 2019; Gola 2020].
- **It's an impulse control disorder, not an addiction.** The WHO working group considered and declined the addiction framing on evidence grounds. This matters for treatment: addiction models emphasize abstinence and powerlessness; impulse-control models emphasize learning regulation. The latter is more consistent with the evidence and with most successful treatment.
- **Prevalence** estimates run 3–10% of adults depending on criteria and population, higher in men than women [Bőthe 2024; Dickenson 2018]. It's not rare, and it's not most men.
- **Masturbation and pornography** are the most common behaviors involved, but CSBD can involve any sexual behavior.
- **Comorbidity** is high: depression, anxiety, ADHD, OCD, trauma history, and substance use disorders are common alongside. Often the sexual behavior is downstream of these — a regulation strategy for something else.

## 15.3 "Gooning"

**The term.** "Gooning" (from "goon," a slang term for a trance-like or mindless state) refers to prolonged masturbation sessions — often hours — characterized by extended edging, continuous consumption of pornography, and a deliberately sought dissociative, trance-like state of high arousal ("goon state" or "gooned out"). The associated online communities and content often feature escalating, sometimes deliberately degrading self-talk, "porn addiction" framed as a fetish, and an identity built around the practice.

**What's actually happening physiologically.** Extended edging at 8–9 with continuous novel visual stimuli produces sustained high dopamine (anticipation) with delayed or absent consummation (orgasm). The "trance" is the narrowed attention and mild prefrontal deactivation of high arousal (Chapter 3), extended for hours, combined with the hypnotic effect of continuous scrolling through novel content. The self-degrading content is a form of arousal amplification through taboo/shame. None of this is neurologically exotic; it's the wanting system run at maximum for a very long time.

**Is it a problem?** It depends entirely on the 15.1 questions.

- A man who, once a month, sets aside an evening for a three-hour edging session with pornography, enjoys it, has an intense orgasm, and goes about his life — is doing an intense version of the practices in Chapters 9 and 11. Not a problem.
- A man who does this most nights, into the early hours, skipping sleep; who can't have a session under two hours; who finds ordinary orgasms unsatisfying; who has adopted an identity ("gooner") organized around it; who has escalated content; who feels wrecked afterward; who has tried to cut back and failed — has a pattern that maps closely onto CSBD, whatever he calls it.

**The specific risks of the gooning pattern, as distinct from ordinary long sessions:**

1. **Time.** Multi-hour sessions, regularly, consume a life.
2. **Sleep.** The pattern is overwhelmingly nocturnal. Chronic sleep loss is a serious health problem on its own and worsens impulse control (a vicious cycle).
3. **Conditioning to extremes.** Hours of edging with continuous novel content sets the arousal reference point at a level no partner, and no ordinary session, can match. This is the supernormal-stimulus concern (Chapter 14) at its most concentrated.
4. **Attention displacement.** Gooning is almost entirely screen-focused. The body is barely felt. It's the opposite of the mindful approach — arousal without sensation. Men who goon heavily often report that they can no longer enjoy a "normal" session.
5. **Identity.** Building an identity around a sexual behavior — especially one framed in terms of addiction and degradation — is psychologically sticky. It converts a behavior into a self-concept, which makes change feel like self-betrayal.
6. **The content culture.** The self-degrading, "porn addict as fetish" content that accompanies gooning eroticizes loss of control. This is a direct obstacle to regaining it: the shame becomes part of the arousal loop.
7. **Physical.** Hours of stimulation without lube breaks, dehydration, prostate/testicular congestion, wrist strain, posture.

**The honest assessment:** the gooning *practice* (long edging with pornography) is an intensity setting that some men can use occasionally without harm. The gooning *culture and pattern* (nightly, escalating, identity-forming, degradation-oriented) is a description of compulsive sexual behavior with a subculture built around it. The distinguishing factor is not the length of the session; it's control, consequence, and the trajectory.

If you recognize yourself in the second description and don't like it, section 15.5 is for you. If you recognize yourself and are fine with it, the 15.1 questions are still worth answering honestly.

## 15.4 Self-assessment

Answer honestly, thinking about the past six months.

**Part A — Control**
1. I've tried to reduce or stop and haven't been able to. (Y/N)
2. I often masturbate for longer than I intended. (Y/N)
3. I often masturbate when I've decided not to. (Y/N)
4. I've broken commitments to myself about it repeatedly. (Y/N)

**Part B — Consequences**
5. It regularly costs me sleep. (Y/N)
6. It's affected my work or study. (Y/N)
7. It's affected a relationship (partner, family, friends). (Y/N)
8. I've had physical problems from it (pain, irritation, injury). (Y/N)
9. I've spent money I couldn't afford on it. (Y/N)

**Part C — Function**
10. I have difficulty getting aroused or reaching orgasm without pornography. (Y/N)
11. I have difficulty getting aroused or reaching orgasm with a partner but not alone. (Y/N)
12. I've avoided partnered sex or intimacy because of masturbation/porn use. (Y/N)

**Part D — Motive and aftermath**
13. I mainly do it to cope with negative feelings (stress, loneliness, boredom, anxiety) rather than for pleasure. (Y/N)
14. I usually feel bad afterward (empty, ashamed, anxious, depressed). (Y/N)
15. It's become mechanical — I do it without really enjoying it. (Y/N)

**Part E — Escalation and preoccupation**
16. The time I spend has increased substantially. (Y/N)
17. The content I need has become more extreme than I'm comfortable with. (Y/N)
18. I think about it a lot when I'm not doing it. (Y/N)
19. I've organized my schedule or life around it. (Y/N)

**Part F — The moral check**
20. If I imagine that masturbation were completely morally neutral — as neutral as eating lunch — would I still be concerned about my pattern? (Y/N)

**Interpretation:**

- **0–3 Y in A–E:** Normal range. Whatever your frequency.
- **4–7 Y, spread across sections:** Worth attention. Not necessarily a disorder; a pattern that would benefit from the strategies in 15.5. Consider talking to someone.
- **8+ Y, or 3+ in Part A, or 2+ in Part C:** Consistent with CSBD or a significant problematic pattern. A professional consultation is a good idea.
- **Part F = N with several Y elsewhere:** Your concern may be primarily moral incongruence. This is worth working on — with a therapist, a thoughtful religious advisor, or through reading — but the intervention is on the belief-behavior conflict, not necessarily on the behavior. Shame-based abstinence programs often make this worse.

This is not a validated instrument. Validated ones exist (the Compulsive Sexual Behavior Disorder Scale [CSBD-19, Bőthe 2020]; the Problematic Pornography Consumption Scale [PPCS]; the Hypersexual Behavior Inventory [HBI]) and a clinician can administer them.

## 15.5 Changing the pattern

If you've decided you want to change — not because someone told you to, not because of shame, but because your honest answers say the pattern is costing you — here is what works, roughly in order of evidence and practicality.

### Reframe

- **You are not an addict; you have a habit with a strong reward loop.** The impulse-control frame is more accurate and more useful. Habits are changed by changing cues, routines, and rewards.
- **Masturbation is not the enemy.** For most men with a problematic pattern, the problem is a specific *configuration* — usually late-night + screen + browsing + hours + isolation. Change the configuration; keep the masturbation.
- **The goal is agency, not abstinence.** You're aiming to masturbate when you choose, how you choose, for as long as you choose, and to enjoy it. That's the mastery this guide is about. Abstinence is one tool, not the goal.
- **Shame is fuel for the loop.** Feeling terrible → needing relief → masturbating → feeling terrible. Breaking the shame link breaks the loop. Chapter 4's facts help. So does saying it out loud to someone who won't judge.

### Change the configuration

The most effective interventions change *how* rather than *whether*:

- **No screens.** For a defined period (start with 2–4 weeks), masturbate only with fantasy or audio. This is the single highest-yield change for most men with a problematic pattern, because the screen is where the browsing loop, the time loss, the escalation, and the attention displacement all live. You'll likely find arousal harder at first and then find it comes back with more sensation (Chapter 8, 14).
- **Time limits.** Decide the length before you start. Set a timer. 15 minutes, or 30. When it goes off, finish or stop. This restores the experience of choosing.
- **Not in bed / not at night.** Move sessions to a different time and place. The late-night bed is the strongest cue for the compulsive pattern. Morning or afternoon, in a chair or on the floor, changes the context and breaks the cue.
- **Never as a response to a feeling.** When you notice the urge, name the feeling underneath it (bored? anxious? lonely? avoiding something?). Address that feeling directly, even briefly. Then, if you still want to masturbate, do it — as a choice, not a reflex.
- **Intentional, planned sessions.** Decide in advance: "Wednesday evening, 30 minutes, no screen, full attention." Then keep the appointment. Planned pleasure is the opposite of compulsive pleasure.
- **Mindful practice.** Chapter 8. Compulsive sessions are dissociated — attention elsewhere, body barely felt. Mindful sessions are the opposite. Retraining attention onto sensation is directly therapeutic.
- **Friction on the platform.** Block or uninstall the specific sites/apps. Use a blocker with a delay. Remove saved logins. This isn't about willpower — it's about making the compulsive path slower than the deliberate one.

### Address what's underneath

Compulsive masturbation is almost always doing a job. Common ones:

- **Anxiety regulation.** Orgasm is a reliable, fast anxiolytic. If it's your main one, you need others: exercise, breath work, actual treatment for anxiety.
- **Loneliness.** Porn simulates connection. It's a poor substitute and it can reduce motivation to seek the real thing.
- **Procrastination / avoidance.** Masturbation is the perfect procrastination — absorbing, rewarding, and it "resets" the mind. If sessions cluster around deadlines or difficult tasks, that's the job it's doing.
- **Sleep avoidance / insomnia.** Late-night sessions can be a way of not confronting the difficulty of sleep, or of not ending the day.
- **Depression.** Anhedonia + a reliable pleasure source = heavy reliance.
- **ADHD.** Impulsivity, dopamine-seeking, and difficulty with delayed gratification all predispose to compulsive patterns. Untreated ADHD is heavily overrepresented in CSBD populations.
- **Trauma.** Sexual compulsivity is common in trauma survivors. Dissociative sexual states can be a re-enactment or a coping mechanism.
- **Sexual frustration in a relationship.** Desire discrepancy that isn't being discussed.

Treating the underlying thing is usually more effective than fighting the symptom. If you recognize one of these, it's the place to focus.

### Structured breaks

A **defined break** — 2–6 weeks — from pornography specifically (not necessarily from masturbation) is the most commonly useful reset:

- It breaks the cue-routine loop.
- It recalibrates arousal to non-screen stimuli.
- It creates a period in which you find out what the behavior was doing for you (the feelings that surface when it's unavailable are the information).
- It's finite, so it's not an identity ("I don't watch porn" vs. "I'm a recovering porn addict").

Rules for a good break: pick a defined end date; decide in advance what's allowed (masturbation with fantasy? audio? nothing?); tell one person; expect the first week to be the hardest; treat a lapse as data, not failure ("what was the cue?") and continue; at the end, decide deliberately what your relationship to the material will be going forward.

A break from *all* masturbation is a different and larger intervention, useful mainly if masturbation itself (not just porn) has become compulsive, or if you want to reset sensitivity after heavy use. The physiological benefits claimed by abstinence communities aren't real (Chapter 4); the psychological ones can be.

### Professional help

When to seek it:

- The self-assessment scores high.
- You've tried the above for a couple of months and the pattern persists.
- There's a co-occurring issue (depression, anxiety, ADHD, trauma, substance use).
- It's affecting a relationship significantly.
- You feel hopeless about it.

Who:

- **A certified sex therapist** (AASECT in the US, COSRT in the UK, or equivalent). They treat CSBD without moralizing and understand the difference between compulsivity and moral incongruence. This is the best first choice.
- **A psychologist or psychiatrist** with experience in impulse control or behavioral addictions, especially if there's comorbidity.
- **Cognitive-behavioral therapy (CBT)** and **acceptance and commitment therapy (ACT)** have the best evidence base for CSBD. Mindfulness-based approaches also show promise.
- **Medication** is sometimes used — SSRIs (which also reduce libido), naltrexone (opioid antagonist, some evidence for compulsive sexual behavior), or treating underlying ADHD/depression. Prescribed by a psychiatrist.
- **12-step groups** (SAA, SLAA, SA) help some men through structure and community. Caveats: the addiction/powerlessness model isn't well-supported by evidence; some groups have moral or religious framings that add shame; SA in particular defines any sex outside marriage as acting out. Choose a group whose framework fits your values.
- **Online "porn addiction recovery" programs**: hugely variable. Some are reasonable; many are religiously motivated, pseudoscientific ("rewiring in 90 days"), shame-heavy, or expensive coaching without credentials. Evaluate carefully.

What to avoid: anyone who tells you masturbation itself is the disease; anyone who promises a fixed-day cure; anyone who explains your problem primarily in terms of testosterone, "dopamine receptors," or "brain damage"; anyone selling supplements.

## 15.6 The other direction: too little, or shame-driven avoidance

Less discussed: men who avoid masturbation out of shame, religious guilt, or misinformation, and who experience:

- Unwanted nocturnal emissions (normal, but distressing to some).
- Difficulty with partnered sex from lack of self-knowledge (not knowing what works, poor ejaculatory control from never practicing).
- Sexual anxiety generally.
- A cycle of suppression → intense urge → "giving in" → shame → suppression, which mimics compulsivity but is actually driven by restriction.

For these men, the intervention is the opposite: permission, accurate information (Chapter 4), and deliberate, guilt-free practice. The restriction is the problem. Moral incongruence work applies here too.

## 15.7 Relationship contexts

- **Masturbation in a relationship is normal** and doesn't indicate dissatisfaction. Most partnered men masturbate. Desire discrepancy — one partner wanting sex more often — is the most common sexual complaint in couples, and solo sex is the healthiest way to manage it.
- **Secrecy is the problem, not the behavior.** Partners who feel deceived about porn or masturbation react to the deception. Open, non-defensive conversation ("I masturbate; here's roughly how often; here's what I use; it's not about you") usually defuses it.
- **When it displaces partnered sex you'd want**, or when a partner is sexually available and you choose solo instead consistently, that's information about the relationship, the pattern, or both.
- **Partners who demand total abstinence** from masturbation are making an unusual and often unhealthy demand. Negotiation about porn is common and reasonable; prohibition of solo sex is a different thing.

## 15.8 A closing note

The skills in this guide — edging, pleasure maximization, long sessions, toys, deliberate use of erotic material — are dual-use. They can make solo sex extraordinary and they can, in a different configuration, feed a compulsive pattern. The difference is not the technique. It's whether you're driving.

The test is simple and worth running periodically: **Can I skip it easily when I choose to? Do I enjoy it when I do it? Is my life better for it?** Yes, yes, yes: you're fine, and you should enjoy it. Anything else: worth a look, without shame.

---

### Key takeaways

- **Frequency is not the metric.** Control, interference, function, motive, enjoyment, escalation, aftermath, and physical symptoms are.
- **CSBD (ICD-11)**: failure to control + neglect of life / repeated failed attempts to cut down / continuing despite harm or lack of satisfaction, for 6+ months, with real impairment. **Moral distress alone doesn't qualify.** ~3–10% of adults. Impulse control, not addiction.
- **"Gooning"**: the *practice* (long edging + porn) is an intensity setting some men can use occasionally. The *pattern and culture* (nightly, escalating, identity-forming, degradation-eroticizing, sleep-destroying) describes compulsive behavior. Length isn't the issue; control and trajectory are.
- **Self-assess** with the 20 questions. Note Part F — the moral check.
- **To change**: reframe (habit, not addiction; masturbation isn't the enemy; goal is agency); **change the configuration** (no screens, time limits, not in bed at night, planned sessions, mindful practice, platform friction); **address what's underneath** (anxiety, loneliness, avoidance, sleep, depression, ADHD, trauma, relationship); **structured 2–6 week porn break**; **certified sex therapist** if needed.
- Avoid shame-based, pseudoscientific, or moralizing programs.
- **Restriction and shame** create their own dysfunction; permission and information fix it.
- **Periodic test**: Can I skip it easily? Do I enjoy it? Is my life better for it?


---

# Chapter 16 — Troubleshooting: Common Problems and Fixes

This is the clinical chapter. Each section covers a problem: what it is, what causes it, what you can do yourself, and when to see a professional. The self-help here is drawn from sex therapy practice and the research literature; it isn't a substitute for a clinician when one is needed, and each section says when that is.

## 16.1 Premature ejaculation (PE)

**Definition.** The International Society for Sexual Medicine defines lifelong PE as ejaculation that always or nearly always occurs within about one minute of vaginal penetration, from the first sexual experiences; acquired PE as a clinically significant and bothersome reduction in latency (often to ≤3 minutes) in men who previously had normal control. Both require inability to delay and negative consequences (distress, avoidance) [ISSM 2014]. For solo purposes, the relevant feature is: **you ejaculate sooner than you want to, and can't reliably delay it.**

**Prevalence.** Self-reported PE affects 20–30% of men; strict-criteria lifelong PE is 2–5%.

**Causes.** Lifelong PE likely has a neurobiological component (serotonin receptor sensitivity, penile hypersensitivity, and genetic factors are all implicated). Acquired PE is associated with ED (rushing before losing the erection), anxiety, hyperthyroidism, prostatitis, and relationship factors. In both, a **hypertonic pelvic floor**, **anxiety/sympathetic dominance**, and **learned rushing** (from adolescent masturbation habits of finishing fast to avoid discovery) are common contributors and are the modifiable targets.

**Self-help (evidence-based):**

1. **Stop-start and squeeze** (Chapter 9) — the original behavioral treatments [Semans 1956; Masters & Johnson 1970]. Practice solo with a lubricated hand, then a warm sleeve, then with thrusting motion. 8–12 weeks.
2. **Pelvic floor training with emphasis on release** (Chapter 10) — 82.5% of men with lifelong PE gained control after 12 weeks in Pastore's study [2014]. The reverse Kegel at 8.5–9 is the key skill.
3. **Breath** — slow, exhale-emphasized breathing throughout; a long exhale at the edge (Chapter 8).
4. **Body relaxation at high arousal** — thighs, buttocks, jaw, hands (Chapter 8.8).
5. **Arousal awareness** — learn the 1–10 scale until you can name your level in real time.
6. **Slow down the whole session** — men with PE often masturbate fast and hard, training the reflex. Two weeks of slow, light, lubricated sessions alone often produces measurable change.
7. **Masturbate before partnered sex** — 1–2 hours prior; the second arousal cycle is slower for most men. A legitimate, widely used tactic.
8. **Reduce anxiety** — PE is self-reinforcing through performance anxiety. Mindfulness practice, and taking the pressure off ("we'll have sex; if I come fast we'll continue other ways") help.

**Medical options** (via a doctor): daily or on-demand SSRIs (paroxetine, sertraline, dapoxetine where available) — well-evidenced; topical anesthetics (lidocaine/prilocaine sprays or creams) — effective, use with a condom or wash off to avoid numbing a partner; PDE5 inhibitors if ED coexists; tramadol (off-label, less preferred). Combining medication with behavioral training often works better than either alone [Perelman 2006].

**See a doctor if:** self-help hasn't helped after 3 months; PE is acquired and sudden (check thyroid, prostate); ED is also present; distress is significant.

## 16.2 Delayed ejaculation (DE) and anorgasmia

**Definition.** Marked delay in, infrequency of, or absence of ejaculation/orgasm despite adequate stimulation and desire, causing distress. "Adequate" is subjective; men with DE commonly report needing 30+ minutes of intense stimulation, or being unable to finish with a partner (but able alone), or unable to finish at all.

**Prevalence.** 1–4% in most surveys; more common than assumed and rising in clinical caseloads.

**Causes.**

- **Idiosyncratic masturbatory style** [Perelman 2005, 2016] — the most common contributor in men who can orgasm alone but not with a partner. A technique (speed, pressure, grip, prone rubbing, specific position) that no partner can replicate; high frequency (Perelman: >3x/week correlates); and a gap between the fantasy required and the partner present.
- **Medications** — SSRIs are the most common cause of acquired DE; also antipsychotics, some blood pressure medications, opioids, alcohol.
- **Neurological** — diabetes, MS, spinal injury, pelvic surgery.
- **Hormonal** — low testosterone, hyperprolactinemia, hypothyroidism (uncommon but checkable).
- **Psychological** — self-monitoring/spectatoring, anxiety, fear of pregnancy or of "letting go," relationship issues, religious guilt about ejaculation.
- **Age** — latency increases with age normally; DE is more common over 50.
- **Reduced penile sensitivity** — with age, diabetes, or heavy pressure conditioning.

**Self-help:**

1. **Masturbation retraining** (Perelman's approach):
   - **Identify** your idiosyncratic pattern honestly. Speed? Pressure? Position? Dry? Prone? Specific fantasy?
   - **Suspend** the idiosyncratic method entirely for 2–6 weeks. Not necessarily suspend masturbation — suspend the *method*.
   - **Substitute** stimulation that resembles what a partner could provide: lubricated, moderate pressure, moderate speed, supine, enveloping (a warm sleeve is ideal). Initially this may not get you to orgasm. That's expected. Stay with it; arousal adapts to the available stimulation within a few weeks in most men.
   - **Reduce frequency** temporarily if it's high (Perelman recommends this). Every 3–4 days rather than daily, so arousal accumulates.
   - **Align fantasy** — include content resembling your actual or plausible partnered sex.
   - **Gradually reintroduce** variety once orgasm with "partner-like" stimulation is reliable.
2. **Reduce spectatoring.** DE men are often watching themselves, evaluating, worrying about how long it's taking. Mindfulness (Chapter 8) — attention on sensation, not on progress — is directly therapeutic. Remove the clock. Remove the goal for several sessions.
3. **Increase arousal inputs.** Many DE men plateau at 7 and never learn what gets them to 8–9. Systematic exploration (Chapter 6 inventory, Chapter 11 mapping) — nipples, perineum, prostate, vibration, fantasy variety — often reveals unused inputs. Vibration in particular helps many men with DE because it recruits receptors that hands don't.
4. **Pelvic floor.** Both strengthening (weak expulsion) and release (a tight floor can inhibit the reflex).
5. **Medication review.** If you're on an SSRI or similar, talk to the prescriber — dose adjustment, drug switch (bupropion, mirtazapine have less sexual side effect), or timing strategies exist.
6. **Reduce alcohol.**

**Medical options:** no approved drug for DE. Off-label: cabergoline (dopamine agonist), bupropion, oxytocin (experimental), amantadine; testosterone if low. Vibratory stimulation devices (medical-grade) are used clinically. A sex therapist is the main resource.

**See a doctor if:** DE is new and unexplained (check meds, hormones, glucose); there's no ejaculation at all (anejaculation — may be retrograde ejaculation or neurological); it's causing significant distress or fertility issues.

## 16.3 "Death grip" and reduced sensitivity

Covered in Chapters 5 and 6; consolidated here.

**Presentation.** Needing very firm pressure and/or fast speed to feel much or to reach orgasm; reduced sensation with lighter touch or with a partner; sometimes visible skin changes (thickening, redness at the grip zone).

**Cause.** Learned threshold (conditioning), not nerve damage. Possibly some transient skin/nerve contribution from chronic compression, which resolves.

**Fix — the 4-week recalibration:**

- **Week 1:** all sessions lubricated. Grip at half your habitual pressure. Speed at half. It will feel like less. Orgasm may be hard. Don't compensate by going harder; if you don't finish, stop without finishing.
- **Week 2:** same, plus one session with the non-dominant hand only, and one with a sleeve (moderate, enveloping pressure).
- **Week 3:** grip at a quarter of your old pressure. Add mindful attention (Chapter 8). Add a secondary zone. Notice the sensation returning.
- **Week 4:** experiment with the barely-touching level. Most men find by now that light, lubricated stimulation is producing more sensation than the death grip ever did.
- **Ongoing:** rotate techniques; never let one grip/speed become the only path.

If you also have DE, this is the same program as 16.2's retraining. If sensation doesn't return in 6–8 weeks, or if there's numbness rather than just reduced sensation, see a doctor (check for diabetes, pudendal nerve issues, medications).

## 16.4 Erectile difficulties

**Definitions.** Erectile dysfunction is the persistent inability to attain or maintain an erection sufficient for satisfactory sexual performance. Occasional difficulty is universal and not ED. Solo-relevant presentations: difficulty getting hard alone; getting hard alone but not with a partner (situational); losing erection during long sessions; softer erections than before.

**First, the normal stuff:**

- **Erections fluctuate during long sessions.** Softening during pauses or low-stimulation phases is normal. It comes back.
- **Mindful/slow sessions** produce less rigid erections than fast, high-stimulation ones. Normal.
- **Refractory period** — you won't get hard soon after ejaculating. Normal. Duration increases with age.
- **Tired, drunk, stressed, cold, distracted** — all produce weaker erections. Normal.
- **Morning erections** present → the plumbing works; difficulties are more likely psychological/situational.

**Causes of real ED:**

- **Vascular** — the most common cause over 40. ED is an early warning sign of cardiovascular disease (the penile arteries are small and show atherosclerosis before the coronary arteries). New ED in a man over 40 warrants a cardiovascular check.
- **Metabolic** — diabetes, obesity, metabolic syndrome.
- **Hormonal** — low testosterone (less common than assumed as a sole cause), thyroid, high prolactin.
- **Neurological** — diabetes, MS, pelvic surgery, spinal issues.
- **Medications** — antidepressants, antihypertensives (especially beta-blockers, thiazides), antipsychotics, 5-alpha-reductase inhibitors (finasteride), antihistamines, opioids.
- **Substances** — alcohol, tobacco (major), cannabis (heavy), stimulants.
- **Psychological** — performance anxiety (the dominant cause in men under 40), depression, stress, relationship issues, guilt.
- **Situational conditioning** — reliably hard with porn/specific stimulus, not otherwise (Chapter 14).
- **Pelvic floor hypertonicity** — can compress arteries and nerves (Chapter 10).
- **Sleep** — sleep apnea is strongly associated with ED.

**Self-help:**

1. **Rule out the normal** (above).
2. **Cardiovascular health** — exercise, weight, blood pressure, smoking cessation. This is the single most effective ED intervention for men over 40 and takes months. Aerobic exercise alone improves erectile function measurably [Gerbild 2018 meta-analysis].
3. **Pelvic floor training** — 40% cured, 35% improved in Dorey's RCT [2005].
4. **Reduce performance pressure.** For situational/psychogenic ED: sessions with no goal, mindful, in which erection is not required and not monitored. The paradox of erection: trying to get one prevents it. Sensate focus without erection as the goal is the sex-therapy standard.
5. **Screen-free arousal** if you suspect conditioning. A 2–6 week break from pornography with fantasy-based masturbation (Chapter 14).
6. **Sleep, alcohol, tobacco.**
7. **Medication review** with the prescriber.
8. **PDE5 inhibitors** (sildenafil, tadalafil) — highly effective, safe for most men, available by prescription (and over the counter in some countries). Using them for a period can break the anxiety cycle in psychogenic ED by restoring confidence. They don't create arousal; they amplify the response to arousal that's present. Not with nitrates; caution with poppers.

**See a doctor if:** ED is persistent (>3 months), especially if new and you're over 40 (cardiovascular workup); morning erections have disappeared; there's pain, curvature, or a lump; you're on medications that may contribute; it's causing significant distress.

## 16.5 Pain

**During or after ejaculation (dysorgasmia / painful ejaculation).** Causes: prostatitis/CPPS (most common), pelvic floor hypertonicity, urethritis (STI or not), seminal vesicle inflammation, post-surgical, some medications (certain antidepressants), psychological. **Do:** pelvic floor release (Chapter 10.5), warm baths, hydration; if persistent >2 weeks or with fever/discharge — doctor (urine test, prostate exam).

**Testicular ache after prolonged arousal ("blue balls" / epididymal hypertension).** Real, benign, from vasocongestion. Resolves with ejaculation or in a few hours. Not dangerous. Severe, sudden, or persistent testicular pain is different — torsion (emergency), epididymitis (infection, doctor), or trauma.

**Pain in the penis with erection.** New curvature + pain + possibly a lump = Peyronie's; see a urologist early. Pain at the frenulum = tear or frenulum breve. Pain in the shaft without curvature after a session = probably friction/bruising; rest.

**Skin pain/rawness.** Friction. Chapter 5.

**Pelvic/perineal pain, chronic.** Chronic pelvic pain syndrome. Often pelvic floor tension. Pelvic floor physiotherapy has the best evidence [Anderson 2005]. Urologist to rule out other causes.

**Pain with anal play.** Stop. Insufficient lubrication, insufficient relaxation, too large, too fast, hemorrhoid or fissure. Persistent pain after — doctor.

**Lower back pain after masturbation.** Posture and muscle tension (arching, bracing). Stretch, change position, relax the body at high arousal.

**Headache at orgasm.** "Coital cephalalgia" — sudden severe headache at or near orgasm. Usually benign (a form of exertional headache) but the first occurrence should be evaluated because the same presentation can indicate a hemorrhage. Recurrent benign cases are managed with medication (indomethacin, beta-blockers) if needed.

## 16.6 Blood in semen (hematospermia)

Alarming, almost always benign, especially under 40. Causes: minor vessel rupture from vigorous sex/masturbation, prostatitis, urethritis, recent prostate procedure, hypertension, and rarely, more serious conditions (more relevant over 40 with recurrent episodes). **Do:** a single episode that resolves — note it; recurrent episodes, over 40, or with other symptoms (pain, urinary symptoms, weight loss) — see a doctor.

## 16.7 Retrograde ejaculation

Orgasm with little or no semen, and cloudy urine afterward (semen went into the bladder). Causes: alpha-blockers (tamsulosin and others, for prostate or blood pressure), some antidepressants, diabetes (nerve damage), prostate/bladder-neck surgery, and — deliberately — the perineal "hold-back" or strong "clench" (Chapter 12). Harmless in itself except for fertility. If unexpected and persistent, see a doctor (medication review, glucose check).

## 16.8 Post-orgasmic illness syndrome (POIS)

Flu-like symptoms — fatigue, muscle aches, fever/chills sensation, cognitive fog, irritability, congestion — beginning within minutes to hours after ejaculation and lasting 2–7 days. Rare; ~50 published cases but likely underreported [Nguyen 2018]. Cause unknown; hypotheses include an allergic/autoimmune reaction to seminal components and neuroendocrine dysregulation. Diagnosis is clinical (the pattern). Treatment is trial-and-error: antihistamines, NSAIDs, SSRIs, hyposensitization to autologous semen (experimental), and others [Ponce 2025 systematic review]. If this is you, it's not psychological, and a urologist or immunologist with an interest in it is the resource. Non-ejaculatory orgasm (Chapter 12) may help some POIS patients, suggestively implicating ejaculation specifically.

## 16.9 Post-coital dysphoria (post-orgasm sadness)

Feelings of sadness, tearfulness, irritability, anxiety, or emptiness after orgasm, without an obvious reason. Reported by ~40% of men at some point and ~4% regularly [Maczkowiack & Schweitzer 2019]. Cause unclear — possibly the hormonal shift (prolactin, drop in dopamine), possibly psychological (shame, unresolved feelings surfacing when defenses drop). Usually brief. **Do:** don't rush to fix it; stay in the afterglow; let the feeling be there; it typically passes in minutes. If persistent, frequent, and distressing, or if it's tied to shame about the behavior, a therapist can help.

## 16.10 Loss of interest / low libido

Reduced desire to masturbate or for sex generally. Causes: depression (very common), stress, fatigue, sleep problems, low testosterone (check if other symptoms — fatigue, low mood, reduced morning erections, reduced muscle), medications (SSRIs, antiandrogens, opioids), relationship issues, hypothyroidism, chronic illness, alcohol, aging (gradual normal decline), and *habituation to a narrow pattern* — the same routine has stopped being interesting. **Do:** address sleep, stress, exercise, alcohol; vary the practice (Chapters 6, 11); if persistent with other symptoms, a doctor for a hormonal and mood assessment. Low desire without distress is not a disorder.

## 16.11 Numbness and reduced sensation

Distinguish: **transient numbness** after vibrator use or a very long session (normal; resolves in minutes to hours), **reduced sensitivity** from conditioning (16.3), **age-related decline** (gradual, normal, compensated with more varied and intense stimulation), and **true neuropathy** (persistent, often with tingling, may affect the perineum/thighs too; causes include diabetes, pudendal nerve compression from cycling or prolonged sitting, spinal issues, B12 deficiency, alcohol). Persistent numbness → doctor.

## 16.12 Erection won't go down (priapism)

An erection lasting more than 4 hours without stimulation is a medical emergency (ischemic priapism damages erectile tissue permanently). Causes: sickle cell, certain medications (trazodone, some antipsychotics, ED drugs — especially injected ones), cocaine, spinal injury. **Emergency room. Do not wait.**

## 16.13 Curvature
