Congenital curvature (present since puberty, stable, painless, <30°) is normal. New curvature, especially with pain, a palpable plaque, indentation, or shortening, suggests Peyronie's disease. Early urological assessment matters; treatments are more effective in the acute (first 12 months) phase.

## 16.14 Semen changes

Volume decrease: normal with frequent ejaculation, dehydration, age, alpha-blockers, and after long sessions (multiple emissions). Yellow: normal after abstinence; possibly urine mix. Clumpy/jelly-like: normal (coagulation before liquefaction). Watery/clear: normal with very frequent ejaculation; persistently very watery — semen analysis if fertility is a concern. Foul odor: possible infection. Blood: 16.6. Green/grey with pain or discharge: infection — doctor.

## 16.15 Lumps and bumps

**Normal:** pearly penile papules (small, uniform, flesh-colored dots in rows around the corona), Fordyce spots (small white/yellow dots on the shaft or scrotum — sebaceous glands), angiokeratomas on the scrotum (small dark red/purple dots), hair follicles, sebaceous cysts (small, mobile, sometimes with a central pore), lymphocele after vigorous sex (cord-like swelling behind the corona, resolves), varicocele ("bag of worms" in the scrotum), epididymis (soft structure on the back of each testis).

**See a doctor:** any lump *in* the testis (hard, painless); warts (cauliflower texture — HPV); ulcers or sores (syphilis, herpes, others); a growing, changing, or bleeding lesion; a plaque in the shaft (Peyronie's); molluscum (pearly dome-shaped bumps with a central dimple — contagious, benign, treatable).

## 16.16 Quick reference: when to seek help

| Symptom | Urgency |
|---|---|
| Erection >4 hours | Emergency |
| Popping sound, pain, swelling of erect penis | Emergency |
| Sudden severe testicular pain | Emergency |
| Foreskin stuck retracted, swelling | Emergency |
| Object stuck in rectum/urethra | Emergency |
| First-ever sudden severe headache at orgasm | Emergency |
| Blood in semen (recurrent, or age >40) | Days |
| Testicular lump | Days |
| New curvature / plaque | Weeks |
| Genital sores, warts, discharge | Days |
| Persistent numbness | Weeks |
| Painful ejaculation >2 weeks | Weeks |
| ED >3 months, esp. age >40 | Weeks (cardiovascular check) |
| PE or DE not responding to 3 months of self-help | Sex therapist / urologist |
| POIS pattern | Urologist / immunologist |
| Feeling out of control of sexual behavior | Sex therapist |

---

### Key takeaways

- **PE**: stop-start + pelvic floor release + breath + slowing down + arousal awareness; 8–12 weeks; SSRIs/topicals if needed.
- **DE**: identify and suspend the idiosyncratic method; substitute partner-like stimulation; reduce frequency temporarily; stop spectatoring; add inputs (vibration, prostate, zones); review medications.
- **Death grip**: 4-week recalibration to lighter, lubricated, slower, varied. Learned, not damage.
- **ED**: rule out normal fluctuation; cardiovascular health; pelvic floor; remove performance pressure; screen break if conditioned; PDE5i to break the anxiety cycle; new ED over 40 = heart check.
- **Pain**: pelvic floor release for most ejaculatory/pelvic pain; blue balls are benign; new curvature = urologist early.
- **Blood in semen** is almost always benign; check if recurrent or over 40.
- **POIS** is real and rare; not psychological.
- **Emergencies**: priapism >4 h, fracture, torsion, paraphimosis, stuck objects, first thunderclap orgasm headache.


---

# Chapter 17 — Life Stages & Circumstances

Solo sexuality changes across a lifetime and across circumstances. What's optimal at 22 isn't at 62; what works living alone doesn't in a shared apartment; a long-distance relationship, a new baby, a chronic illness, a disability, or a surgery each change the picture. This chapter covers the major contexts.

## 17.1 Young adulthood (18–29)

**Physiology.** Peak testosterone, shortest refractory periods, fastest arousal, most frequent spontaneous erections, highest average frequency of masturbation. Also: the least control — arousal comes fast, and the reflex is hair-trigger.

**Common patterns and their consequences.**

- **The adolescent habit persists.** Most men reach adulthood with a technique learned for speed and secrecy at 13–15 — fast, tight, often dry, often prone or hunched, often to visual material. This is the age at which that habit either gets examined or gets locked in for decades. Chapters 6, 8, 9 are the intervention.
- **High frequency is normal.** Daily or more is common and isn't a problem by itself. The 15.1 questions are the test.
- **Screen-dependence is most likely to have formed here.** Men who came of age with unlimited streaming pornography are the population where conditioning concerns (Chapter 14) are most relevant. A screen-free session per week is good range maintenance.
- **Partnered sex often starts here**, and the gap between solo habit and partnered reality shows up as PE (from rushing), situational ED (from performance anxiety or conditioning), or DE (from idiosyncratic technique). The fix is solo practice that resembles partnered conditions (lubricated, moderate, varied).
- **Shared housing.** Privacy is the practical constraint. Headphones, timing, a locked door, quick cleanup, and a stash for supplies. Shower sessions are a standard solution (silicone lube is waterproof).

**Priorities.** Build range (Chapter 6 inventory). Learn arousal control (Chapter 9) — it's easiest to learn young, and it pays off for life. Establish the lube habit. Get the screen relationship deliberate.

## 17.2 Adulthood (30–49)

**Physiology.** Gradual testosterone decline (~1%/year after 30 on average — barely noticeable in most men), refractory periods lengthening to 30 minutes–several hours, erections still reliable but more affected by fatigue, alcohol, stress. Spontaneous erections decrease; erections are more dependent on direct stimulation. Latency to orgasm typically increases somewhat — which is often experienced as *improved* control.

**Context.** Careers, relationships, often children. Time and privacy become scarce. Fatigue becomes the dominant limiting factor.

**Common patterns.**

- **The "quickie only" trap.** With less time, every session becomes a 5-minute stress release. Pleasure and skill both atrophy. The counter: one deliberate, longer session per week or two, scheduled if necessary.
- **Masturbation in a relationship** — normal and common (Chapter 15.7). Desire discrepancy is the most common sexual issue in couples; solo sex is the healthiest management tool. Secrecy causes more problems than the behavior.
- **New parents.** Sleep deprivation and total loss of privacy. Libido often drops (both partners); masturbation may be the only sexual outlet for months. This is normal. Shower sessions; sessions when the baby sleeps; not feeling guilty about it.
- **Erection quality changes** become noticeable in some men. If they coincide with weight gain, inactivity, or poor sleep, the intervention is cardiovascular health (Chapter 18). This is the decade where ED begins to correlate with heart health.
- **This is the age most men first notice prostate-related sensations** or become curious about prostate stimulation. It's a good time — the prostate is fully developed, and pelvic awareness is often better than in youth.

**Priorities.** Protect one quality session a week. Cardiovascular fitness. Pelvic floor training (Chapter 10) — it's preventive for both erectile and urinary function. Talk to your partner about solo sex.

## 17.3 Midlife and beyond (50+)

**Physiology.** More noticeable changes, with enormous individual variation:

- Erections need more direct stimulation and may be less rigid; the angle drops. Fully firm erections remain achievable for most men, especially with good vascular health.
- Refractory period: hours to a day or more.
- Ejaculate volume decreases; force decreases (pelvic floor training helps).
- Orgasm may feel less intense or more diffuse. Some men find it *more* satisfying — less urgent, more whole-body.
- Latency increases; DE becomes more common.
- Penile sensitivity declines gradually — more and more varied stimulation compensates.
- Testosterone continues declining; clinically low T affects a minority and is worth checking if there are symptoms (low libido, fatigue, low mood, loss of morning erections, muscle loss).
- The prostate enlarges (BPH) in most men, causing urinary symptoms. Alpha-blockers for this cause retrograde or reduced ejaculation. 5-alpha-reductase inhibitors (finasteride, dutasteride) can reduce libido and cause ED in some men.
- Cardiovascular disease, diabetes, and their medications become the dominant cause of erectile problems.

**Adaptations.**

- **More time, more stimulation, more variety.** Older men need a longer build-up and benefit most from Chapter 11's principles: multiple zones, vibration (very useful as sensitivity declines), temperature, prostate stimulation.
- **Erection is not required for orgasm.** Men who accept softer erections and continue with pleasure-focused stimulation often orgasm satisfyingly. Men who fixate on rigidity often lose the erection to anxiety.
- **Vibration.** Medical-grade and consumer vibrators are widely recommended for older men and men with neuropathy or post-surgical changes because they deliver stimulation that hands can't.
- **Pelvic floor training** — the evidence for erectile and continence benefits is strongest in this age group.
- **PDE5 inhibitors** — safe and effective for most men; daily low-dose tadalafil is commonly used and also helps BPH symptoms. Discuss with a doctor, especially regarding cardiac medications.
- **Regular sexual activity, including solo, is associated with preserved erectile function** ("use it or lose it") [Koskimäki 2008]. Erections oxygenate penile tissue; long periods without them are associated with fibrosis. This is the rationale for penile rehabilitation after prostatectomy.
- **Prostate stimulation** may be especially rewarding in this age group — the prostate is larger and easier to locate — and prostate orgasm doesn't depend on erection. Men with BPH should check with a urologist first; men with prostatitis may find it helps or hurts.
- **Lube becomes more important** — skin is thinner and drier.
- **Morning is often the best time** — testosterone and energy peak.

**Widowed, divorced, or single later in life.** Solo sex may become the primary sexual outlet after decades of partnered sex. Many men in this situation have never developed a solo practice beyond the functional and find the material in this guide new. It's a legitimate and valuable sexuality in its own right, not a consolation prize.

## 17.4 After prostate surgery or treatment

Radical prostatectomy (for cancer) removes the prostate and seminal vesicles: orgasm remains possible (often after a recovery period) but is **dry** — no ejaculate. Erectile function is frequently impaired, sometimes permanently, depending on nerve preservation. Radiation, hormone therapy, and BPH procedures have varying effects.

- **Orgasm without ejaculation** after prostatectomy is well-documented and for many men remains satisfying, sometimes altered in quality (more diffuse, sometimes with urine leakage at orgasm — "climacturia" — which is manageable).
- **Penile rehabilitation** — regular erections via PDE5 inhibitors, vacuum device, or injections, starting soon after surgery — is standard urological advice to preserve tissue and maximize recovery of natural erections.
- **Masturbation is encouraged** as part of rehabilitation by most urologists.
- **Vibration** is particularly helpful for reaching orgasm with reduced sensation or without erection.
- **The prostate is gone**, so prostate stimulation as such isn't available; some men find perineal and anal stimulation still pleasurable.
- Sexual medicine specialists and pelvic floor physiotherapists are the resources.

## 17.5 Disability and chronic illness

**Spinal cord injury.** Depending on level and completeness: reflexogenic erections often preserved with touch even when psychogenic erections are lost; orgasm may be experienced differently (often described as diffuse, sometimes non-genital); ejaculation frequently impaired. Vibratory stimulation (medical-grade, high-amplitude) is used clinically to induce ejaculation. Many men with SCI develop new erogenous zones at or above the injury level. Specialist sexual rehabilitation exists.

**Diabetes.** Neuropathy reduces sensation; vascular disease impairs erection; retrograde ejaculation is possible. Tight glucose control preserves function. Vibration compensates for reduced sensation. PDE5 inhibitors are effective for many.

**Multiple sclerosis, Parkinson's, and other neurological conditions.** Variable sexual effects. Timing sessions to medication and energy peaks; vibration; positioning aids; PDE5 inhibitors.

**Chronic pain.** Orgasm raises pain thresholds and releases endorphins — many chronic pain patients find masturbation genuinely analgesic. Positioning and pacing matter; heat before a session; short sessions when energy is low.

**Cardiac disease.** Sexual activity is safe for most cardiac patients; the exertion of masturbation is roughly equivalent to climbing two flights of stairs. Ask the cardiologist; the answer is almost always yes. Nitrate medications preclude PDE5 inhibitors.

**Mobility limitations.** Toys and devices become essential rather than optional — automatic strokers, hands-free vibrators, mounted devices, long-handled tools. Positioning cushions. Occupational therapists can advise on adaptations.

**Mental illness and its medications.** SSRIs commonly cause DE, reduced libido, and reduced sensation (Chapter 16). Antipsychotics can cause ED and reduced libido. Depression itself reduces desire. Discuss with the prescriber — dose, timing, switching, and adjunct medications can help. Masturbation is often one of the few reliable pleasures during depressive episodes and shouldn't be an additional source of guilt.

**Chronic illness generally.** Solo sex is often more accessible than partnered sex during illness — less energy, no performance concern, full control of pacing. It's a legitimate part of coping and quality of life.

## 17.6 Relationships

**Solo sex while partnered.** Normal, common, healthy. It manages desire discrepancy, maintains self-knowledge, provides an outlet during illness/pregnancy/travel/conflict, and is simply a different pleasure. Most sex therapists consider mutual acceptance of solo sex a marker of a healthy sexual relationship.

**Disclosure.** Openness is protective; secrecy is corrosive. A simple conversation — "I masturbate, probably X times a week, sometimes with porn/whatever; it's not about you; how do you feel about that?" — prevents most problems. Partners' feelings vary; hearing them out without defensiveness is the skill.

**Mutual masturbation** — masturbating together, watching each other, or each other's hands — is an underused practice that transfers solo skill to the partnership, teaches each partner what the other likes, and is intensely intimate for many couples.

**When a partner objects.** Explore why: insecurity ("am I not enough?"), moral belief, feeling deceived, or a specific concern (porn content, frequency displacing partnered sex). Each has a different response. Total prohibition of solo sex is an unusual demand; concerns about porn are common and negotiable.

**Long-distance.** Solo sex is the primary outlet, often synchronized with a partner (video, phone, teledildonic devices — see Chapter 20 for privacy). Shared masturbation across distance is intimacy, not a substitute for it.

**During pregnancy and postpartum.** Partner libido and availability vary; solo sex fills the gap without pressure. Normal and healthy.

**After a breakup or bereavement.** Solo sex may feel empty, may be a comfort, may bring emotional release (post-orgasm crying is common and healthy in grief). All normal.

## 17.7 Trans men and non-binary people with penises

For readers who've had phalloplasty or metoidioplasty, or who have a penis and identify as trans or non-binary:

- **Metoidioplasty** (releasing a testosterone-enlarged clitoris): highly sensitive, generally 3–8 cm, capable of erection (usually not penetrative), and orgasmic. Technique resembles clitoral stimulation more than penile — focused, lubricated, often lighter — with the anatomy chapter's frenulum/glans principles applying.
- **Phalloplasty**: sensation depends on nerve hookup and healing, typically developing over 1–2 years; the buried clitoris is often the main orgasmic source, stimulated through the base of the phallus; erection requires an implant or external device. Sex therapists and surgeons specializing in gender-affirming care are the resource.
- **Testosterone** for trans men on HRT increases libido, often dramatically, and enlarges the clitoris; many trans men report masturbation frequency increasing substantially after starting T.
- **Dysphoria** can complicate solo sex; approaches that focus on sensation rather than visual/anatomical attention (eyes closed, mindfulness) help many.
- The pelvic floor, prostate-adjacent (for those without prostates, the anterior vaginal wall / Skene's gland area is analogous), and erogenous zone material is broadly applicable.

For trans women and non-binary people assigned male at birth, on estrogen and/or anti-androgens: libido and erectile function typically decrease; sensation changes; orgasm often becomes more diffuse and whole-body; ejaculate reduces or stops. Masturbation approaches shift toward lighter touch, non-penile zones, and (for many) the prostate. This guide's pleasure-maximization and mindfulness chapters apply; the erection-focused material less so.

## 17.8 Circumstances

**No privacy** (dorm, barracks, shared room, living with parents as an adult). Shower sessions with silicone lube; timing around others' schedules; noise management (headphones, white noise, bedding); quick cleanup kit; discretion with toys and supplies (a locked box). Most men in this situation default to fast, silent, screen-based sessions — the mindful and slow approaches are harder but not impossible (a 15-minute quiet session is achievable).

**Travel.** Hotel sessions are often the best of the month — privacy, no obligations, a different environment. Bring lube (travel size, in checked luggage or under liquid limits). Toys in carry-on are routine and TSA doesn't care.

**Religious communities and moral conflict.** Chapter 19 covers the traditions. Practically: moral incongruence (Chapter 14, 15) is the source of most distress; resolving it — through study, a thoughtful advisor, or therapy — matters more than changing the behavior.

**Incarceration, institutional living.** Solo sex is typically the only sexual outlet; privacy is minimal; rules vary. Beyond the scope of this guide except to say it's normal and necessary.

**Military deployment, remote work.** Extended periods away from partners. Solo sex maintains sexual function and provides stress relief. Long-distance intimacy tools (Chapter 20 for security).

**Athletic training.** The "sex before competition" myth is unsupported — masturbation the night before doesn't impair performance [Stefani 2016 review]. Immediately before (within an hour) may reduce aggression/arousal slightly in some men; individual. Post-workout sessions are often excellent (Chapter 11).

---

### Key takeaways

- **20s**: build range, learn control, establish lube, make the screen relationship deliberate. Easiest time to learn skills that last.
- **30s–40s**: protect one quality session a week; cardiovascular health; pelvic floor; talk to your partner. ED begins to track heart health.
- **50+**: more time, more zones, vibration, prostate; erection isn't required for orgasm; PDE5i are safe for most; "use it or lose it" is real.
- **After prostate surgery**: dry orgasm is real and satisfying; penile rehabilitation matters; vibration helps.
- **Disability and illness**: solo sex is often the most accessible sexuality; toys become essential; vibration compensates for reduced sensation; medication effects are negotiable with prescribers.
- **Relationships**: solo sex while partnered is normal and healthy; secrecy is the problem; mutual masturbation transfers skill.
- **Trans and non-binary readers**: anatomy differs; sensation principles apply; specialist resources exist.
- **No privacy**: shower, timing, headphones, locked box. Quality is still possible in 15 quiet minutes.


---

# Chapter 18 — Body, Lifestyle & Sexual Function

Sexual function is a whole-body output. Erection is cardiovascular; sensation is neurological; desire is hormonal and psychological; stamina is muscular and respiratory. This chapter covers what actually moves the needle — exercise, sleep, weight, diet, substances — and, equally important, what doesn't (most supplements). Evidence ratings throughout.

## 18.1 Exercise — 🟢 the strongest lever

**Aerobic exercise improves erectile function.** A meta-analysis of randomized trials found that moderate-to-vigorous aerobic exercise (about 40 minutes, 4 times per week, for 6 months) significantly improved erectile function scores in men with ED from vascular, metabolic, or lifestyle causes [Gerbild 2018]. The effect size approaches that of PDE5 inhibitors in some analyses [Khera 2023 meta-analysis]. Mechanism: endothelial function, nitric oxide availability, blood pressure, weight, insulin sensitivity — all the things erection depends on.

**Resistance training** supports testosterone (modestly), body composition, and confidence. Not directly studied for erectile function as much as aerobic, but complementary.

**Pelvic floor training** — Chapter 10. Its own category of exercise with direct sexual effects.

**Flexibility and hip mobility** — relaxed hips = relaxed pelvic floor. Yoga has small studies showing improved sexual function in men [Dhikav 2010].

**Timing relative to sessions.** 30–90 minutes after moderate exercise: elevated blood flow, endorphins, often heightened sensation and arousal (Chapter 11). Immediately after exhaustive exercise: worse. Sessions during a rest day: fine.

**Cycling.** Long hours on a narrow saddle compress the perineum and pudendal nerve — associated with numbness and ED in heavy cyclists. Mitigations: cut-out or noseless saddle, standing regularly, proper fit, padded shorts. Recreational cycling is fine.

**Overtraining** — chronic excessive endurance training without recovery lowers testosterone and libido. Rare in recreational exercisers.

## 18.2 Sleep — 🟢

- **Testosterone is produced mainly during sleep**, especially during the first REM cycles. A week of 5-hour sleep reduced daytime testosterone by 10–15% in healthy young men [Leproult 2011].
- **Nocturnal erections** occur during REM; they're part of penile tissue maintenance.
- **Sleep apnea** is strongly associated with ED (via hypoxia, fragmented sleep, and reduced testosterone); treating it improves erectile function. Snoring + daytime fatigue + ED = get a sleep study.
- **Sleep deprivation** reduces libido, erection quality, sensation, and impulse control (relevant to compulsivity, Chapter 15).
- **Masturbation before sleep** is associated with faster sleep onset and better perceived sleep quality [Lastella 2019]. Reasonable as a sleep aid, if it doesn't become a compulsive pre-sleep ritual.
- **Late-night sessions** that cut into sleep are the worst trade in this guide: the session is low-quality (fatigue) and the sleep loss degrades everything.

## 18.3 Weight and metabolic health — 🟢

- Obesity is associated with ED (2–3× risk), lower testosterone (aromatization of T to estrogen in fat tissue; also lower SHBG dynamics), lower libido, and reduced sensation (neuropathy in diabetes).
- Weight loss improves erectile function and testosterone in obese men — in one trial, a third of obese men with ED regained normal function through diet and exercise alone [Esposito 2004].
- **Diabetes** is a major cause of ED (via vascular and nerve damage) and reduced sensation. Glucose control preserves function.
- **Metabolic syndrome** (abdominal obesity, high BP, high triglycerides, low HDL, insulin resistance) is the cluster most predictive of ED.
- **Blood pressure**: hypertension damages the arteries; many antihypertensives (beta-blockers, thiazides) impair erection; others (ACE inhibitors, ARBs, some calcium channel blockers) are neutral or slightly positive. Discuss with the prescriber if erections suffered after starting a medication.
- **Cholesterol**: atherosclerosis in penile arteries precedes coronary disease. Statins have mixed effects — improving erectile function in some men via vascular health, with occasional reports of the opposite.

## 18.4 Diet — 🟡

**What has evidence:**

- **Mediterranean-pattern diets** (vegetables, fruit, legumes, whole grains, fish, olive oil, nuts; low processed food) are associated with lower ED incidence and improved erectile function in trials [Esposito 2006; Bauer 2020 — Health Professionals Follow-up Study]. Mechanism: vascular health.
- **Flavonoid-rich foods** (berries, citrus, apples, red wine in moderation) were associated with reduced ED incidence in a large cohort [Cassidy 2016].
- **Nitrate-rich vegetables** (beets, leafy greens) support nitric oxide production — mechanistically relevant; direct erectile trials are small.
- **Hydration** affects ejaculate volume and general function. Not complicated.
- **Zinc**: deficiency impairs testosterone and sperm; supplementation helps only if deficient. Most men in developed countries aren't.

**What doesn't have evidence (despite marketing):**

- **"Semen volume" foods** (celery, pineapple, bananas, oysters): no controlled evidence for volume. Pineapple/citrus and reduced red meat/garlic/asparagus may modestly affect taste/smell — relevant to partners, not solo. Hydration and abstinence duration are the volume levers.
- **"Aphrodisiac" foods**: oysters (zinc — only helps if deficient), chocolate (mild mood effect), chili (capsaicin — mild endorphin release), watermelon (citrulline — trace amounts, far below supplement doses): placebo-range effects at best.
- **"Testosterone-boosting" foods**: no food meaningfully raises testosterone in men who aren't deficient in a specific nutrient. Adequate total calories, adequate fat (very low-fat diets lower T slightly), and adequate protein are the diet inputs; beyond that, it's body fat and sleep.

**Alcohol** — see 18.6.

## 18.5 Supplements — 🟠 mostly, with a few 🟡

The men's sexual supplement industry is enormous and largely unsupported. What the evidence says:

| Supplement | Claim | Evidence | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| **L-arginine** | NO precursor → erections | Mixed; high doses (5g+) show modest effect in some trials, often combined with pycnogenol | 🟡 weak-moderate for mild ED; not for healthy men |
| **L-citrulline** | Converts to arginine more efficiently | Small trials show modest improvement in mild ED (1.5g/day) | 🟡 weak-moderate |
| **Panax ginseng (Korean red)** | Erectile function | Several small RCTs and a Cochrane review find small, uncertain benefit | 🟡 weak |
| **Maca** | Libido | Small trials show possible libido effect; no erectile effect | 🟠 |
| **Tribulus terrestris** | Testosterone, libido | Does *not* raise testosterone in humans; possible mild libido effect | 🟠 for libido; 🔴 for T |
| **Fenugreek** | Testosterone, libido | Small trials; modest libido effects; T effect inconsistent | 🟠 |
| **Ashwagandha** | Testosterone, stress | Some trials show modest T increase (10–15%) and stress reduction; quality varies | 🟡 weak |
| **Tongkat ali** | Testosterone | Small trials, modest effects, low quality | 🟠 |
| **Horny goat weed (icariin)** | PDE5 inhibition | Weak PDE5 inhibitor in vitro; human evidence minimal | 🟠 |
| **Yohimbine** | ED | Some evidence for psychogenic ED; significant side effects (anxiety, BP, heart rate); prescription in some countries | 🟡 but risky |
| **Zinc** | Testosterone, sperm | Only if deficient | 🟡 conditional |
| **Vitamin D** | Testosterone | Only if deficient; association with T is real; supplementation effect modest | 🟡 conditional |
| **DHEA** | Testosterone precursor | Small effects in older men; hormone — not casual | 🟠 |
| **Magnesium** | Testosterone, sleep | Only if deficient; helps sleep in some | 🟡 conditional |
| **"Male enhancement" blends** | Everything | Frequently adulterated with unlabeled sildenafil/tadalafil analogs (FDA has issued hundreds of warnings) — this is why some "work"; also dangerous with nitrates | 🔴 avoid |
| **Pine pollen, deer antler, "T-boosters"** | Testosterone | No credible evidence | 🔴 |

**Bottom line:** if you have mild ED, L-citrulline or L-arginine + pycnogenol are the only supplements with reasonable evidence, and prescription PDE5 inhibitors work far better. If you're deficient in zinc, D, or magnesium, correct it. Nothing over the counter meaningfully raises testosterone in a healthy man. Avoid "enhancement" blends — they're frequently adulterated and the adulterants are the active ingredient.

## 18.6 Substances

### Alcohol — 🟢 well-characterized

- **Acute:** one drink may reduce anxiety and inhibition; beyond that, alcohol impairs erection (it's a CNS depressant and vasodilator that also reduces the erectile response), delays or prevents orgasm, reduces sensation, and impairs judgment. "Whiskey dick" is real and dose-dependent.
- **Chronic heavy use:** lowers testosterone, damages nerves (neuropathy), damages the liver (which affects hormone metabolism), and causes persistent ED and reduced libido. Reversible in early stages.
- **For solo sex:** a drink is fine; more than one degrades the experience.

### Tobacco/nicotine — 🟢

- The single most damaging common substance for erections. Nicotine constricts blood vessels acutely (an immediate effect on erection quality) and smoking causes long-term vascular damage. Smokers have roughly double the ED risk; quitting improves erectile function within months [Pourmand 2004].
- Vaping delivers nicotine and has the acute vasoconstrictive effect; the long-term vascular effect is less studied but not zero.

### Cannabis — 🟡 mixed

- **Low doses**: many men report enhanced sensation, time dilation (sessions feel longer), heightened attention to the body, and increased orgasm intensity. Survey data support this for a majority of users [Sun & Eisenberg 2017 found more frequent sex among users; Wiebe 2019 — subjective enhancement].
- **Higher doses**: distraction, anxiety, erectile softening (THC has vasoactive effects), difficulty reaching orgasm, sedation.
- **Chronic heavy use**: associated with lower libido and possibly reduced testosterone in some studies, inconsistent.
- **Practical**: if legal where you are and you use it, low doses (a small amount, or low-THC/higher-CBD) taken 30–60 minutes before a session enhance the experience for many men. High doses don't. Edibles' delayed onset makes dosing harder. Not a recommendation to start; information for those who use.

### Caffeine — 🟡

- Moderate intake (1–3 cups/day) is associated with *lower* ED risk in observational data [Lopez 2015], possibly via vascular effects.
- Large doses close to a session raise sympathetic tone (faster ejaculation, more anxiety, less relaxation). A coffee an hour before a slow session is counterproductive for many men.

### Poppers (alkyl nitrites) — 🟠 with real risks

- Smooth muscle relaxant; brief rush, warmth, anal sphincter relaxation, heightened sensation for 1–3 minutes. Used in gay sexual culture and increasingly beyond it.
- **Risks**: sudden blood pressure drop (dangerous with PDE5 inhibitors — potentially fatal hypotension), headache, dizziness, erectile softening (vasodilation), chemical burns if spilled on skin, methemoglobinemia with ingestion, and a specific form of retinal damage (maculopathy) linked to isopropyl nitrite formulations. Legal status varies.
- **Not recommended.** If used: never with ED drugs; never ingested; sitting or lying down.

### Stimulants (cocaine, methamphetamine, amphetamines, MDMA) — 🔴

- Intense wanting, impaired liking. Massively increased libido and compulsivity with impaired erection (vasoconstriction — "crystal dick"), delayed or absent orgasm, and prolonged, dissociated, sometimes injurious sessions. Methamphetamine in particular is associated with compulsive sexual behavior, hours-long sessions, tissue injury from lack of sensation, and high-risk behavior. MDMA impairs erection and orgasm in most men despite increased sensuality.
- Prescription stimulants (ADHD medication) at therapeutic doses: variable; some men report reduced libido or ED, others no effect. Talk to the prescriber.

### Opioids — 🔴

- Chronic use lowers testosterone (opioid-induced androgen deficiency), reduces libido, impairs orgasm. Acute use impairs orgasm and erection.

### Psychedelics — 🟠

- Not generally used for sex; effects are unpredictable; sensory enhancement is possible but attention is typically elsewhere. Not recommended in this context.

### Prescription drugs affecting sexual function

| Class | Common effect | Options |
|---|---|---|
| SSRIs/SNRIs | Delayed/absent orgasm, reduced libido, reduced sensation, sometimes ED | Dose reduction; switch to bupropion, mirtazapine, vortioxetine; adjunct bupropion or PDE5i; drug holidays (with prescriber) |
| Antipsychotics | ED, reduced libido, elevated prolactin | Switch to prolactin-sparing agent (aripiprazole) |
| Beta-blockers | ED, reduced libido | Switch to nebivolol or a different class |
| Thiazide diuretics | ED | Switch class |
| 5-ARIs (finasteride, dutasteride) | Reduced libido, ED, reduced ejaculate — in a minority; post-finasteride syndrome is reported but contested | Discuss; topical finasteride for hair may have fewer effects |
| Alpha-blockers (tamsulosin etc.) | Retrograde/reduced ejaculation | Usually tolerated; switch if bothersome |
| Antihistamines (sedating) | Reduced erection, dryness | Non-sedating alternatives |
| Opioids | As above | Minimize |
| Anti-androgens, GnRH agonists | Profound libido and erection loss | Expected effect of therapy |
| Benzodiazepines | Reduced libido, delayed orgasm | Minimize |

**Never stop a prescribed medication for sexual side effects without talking to the prescriber.** Almost every class has alternatives.

## 18.7 Testosterone: what actually matters

Because so much marketing targets it:

- **Normal range** is wide (roughly 300–1000 ng/dL total T; labs vary). Being at the lower end of normal is not deficiency.
- **Symptoms** of genuinely low T: low libido, ED (especially loss of morning erections), fatigue, low mood, reduced muscle mass, increased body fat, reduced body hair, hot flashes, infertility. Without symptoms, a number is not a disease.
- **Causes** of low T: obesity (the most common modifiable one), sleep apnea, opioids, anabolic steroid history, testicular injury, pituitary problems, chronic illness, aging.
- **What raises it**: losing body fat (if overweight), sleep, resistance training (modest, acute), correcting deficiencies (D, zinc), reducing alcohol, treating sleep apnea. **Not** masturbation abstinence (Chapter 4). **Not** supplements (18.5).
- **Testosterone replacement therapy** is appropriate for men with symptomatic, confirmed (two morning measurements) low T. It improves libido and energy; its effect on erections alone is modest (ED is usually vascular). It has side effects (infertility, polycythemia, possible cardiovascular considerations under ongoing study) and requires monitoring. Not a lifestyle drug.
- **Masturbation, ejaculation frequency, and testosterone**: no meaningful relationship. Chapter 4.

## 18.8 Stress and mental health — 🟢

- **Chronic stress** raises cortisol, which suppresses the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis (lower T), raises sympathetic tone (worse erections, faster ejaculation), and reduces libido and attention. Stress is a major cause of sexual difficulty in otherwise healthy men.
- **Depression** reduces desire, pleasure (anhedonia), and erectile function directly; its treatments often add sexual side effects. Treating depression is the priority; sexual function is negotiated with the prescriber.
- **Anxiety** is the primary cause of ED and PE in young men. Performance anxiety specifically is a self-fulfilling loop.
- **Interventions with evidence**: exercise (also treats mild-moderate depression and anxiety), sleep, mindfulness/meditation (small-to-moderate effects on sexual function in trials), therapy (CBT for anxiety and depression; sex therapy for sexual anxiety specifically).

## 18.9 Body image

Concerns about penis size, body weight, body hair, and general attractiveness impair sexual function through anxiety and self-monitoring — even in solo sex, where there's no one to impress. Men with size anxiety (nearly all of it unfounded — Chapter 2.8) often report less pleasure and more spectatoring. Interventions: accurate information (Chapter 2), reducing comparison sources (pornography selects for outliers and uses camera tricks), mindfulness (attention on sensation, not on appearance), and — for persistent distress — therapy. Grooming (trimming pubic hair) is a preference, not a requirement; it does make the penis look larger by removing visual obstruction, which is why it's common.

## 18.10 The summary hierarchy

If you want to improve sexual function through lifestyle, in order of impact:

1. **Quit smoking** (if you smoke).
2. **Aerobic exercise** 150+ minutes/week.
3. **Sleep** 7–9 hours; treat apnea.
4. **Lose excess weight**, especially abdominal.
5. **Limit alcohol** to ≤1 drink before sessions, moderate overall.
6. **Manage stress** and treat anxiety/depression.
7. **Pelvic floor training**.
8. **Mediterranean-pattern eating**.
9. **Review medications** with the prescriber.
10. Everything else (supplements, specific foods, timing) is marginal.

---

### Key takeaways

- **Erection is cardiovascular.** Aerobic exercise, not smoking, sleep, and weight are the levers — and they rival medication in effect size.
- **Sleep produces testosterone** and maintains penile tissue via nocturnal erections. Sleep apnea causes ED. Late-night sessions that cut sleep are a bad trade.
- **Diet**: Mediterranean pattern and flavonoid-rich foods have evidence. "Semen foods" and "aphrodisiacs" don't.
- **Supplements**: L-citrulline/L-arginine have weak evidence for mild ED; nothing over the counter meaningfully raises T; "enhancement" blends are often adulterated with prescription drugs.
- **Alcohol** beyond one drink impairs. **Nicotine** is the worst common substance for erections. **Cannabis** at low doses enhances for many; high doses impair. **Stimulants** produce wanting without function and drive compulsivity. **Poppers + ED drugs** can be fatal.
- **Medications** — SSRIs, beta-blockers, thiazides, antipsychotics, 5-ARIs — commonly impair; alternatives exist for nearly all. Talk to the prescriber.
- **Testosterone**: symptoms matter, not just numbers; fat loss, sleep, and treating apnea raise it; masturbation and supplements don't move it.
- **Stress, anxiety, depression, body image** are real sexual function inputs with real treatments.

# Chapter 19 — Culture, Religion, History & Stigma

Most of the anxiety men carry about masturbation is not from their own experience. It's inherited — from religious teaching, from 18th- and 19th-century medicine, from schoolyard folklore, from parents who inherited it in turn. Understanding where the shame came from is one of the most effective ways of putting it down. This chapter is a brief, descriptive history.

## 19.1 Antiquity: mostly indifferent

**Ancient Egypt.** The god Atum was said to have created the world by masturbating — the act appears in creation mythology without apparent shame. Pharaohs reportedly masturbated ceremonially into the Nile.

**Ancient Greece.** Masturbation was regarded as a natural, if lowly, release — associated with slaves, satyrs, and those without partners. Diogenes the Cynic famously masturbated in the marketplace, remarking that he wished he could relieve hunger as easily by rubbing his stomach. Greek disapproval concerned *excess* (a lack of self-mastery), not the act itself.

**Ancient Rome.** Similar. Martial jokes about it; Galen recommended it for releasing retained semen, which he thought could become toxic. Roman medicine also held that *excessive* ejaculation weakened the body — an idea that would return with force.

**Ancient China (Taoism).** Semen (*jing*) was considered a finite vital essence whose loss depleted the body. Taoist sexual manuals advised men to have frequent sex but to *retain* semen — via the perineal hold-back (Chapter 12) — while bringing partners to orgasm. Masturbation to ejaculation was discouraged as waste, though arousal itself was valued. This is the historical root of today's "semen retention" ideas.

**Ancient India (Ayurveda).** Semen (*shukra*) was similarly a concentrated essence, refined through many stages from food; its loss weakened. **Dhat syndrome** — anxiety about semen loss through masturbation, nocturnal emission, or urine, with fatigue and guilt — is a recognized culture-bound syndrome in South Asia, still common in clinics [Sumathipala 2004].

**Judaism.** The story of Onan (Genesis 38) — who "spilled his seed on the ground" rather than impregnate his dead brother's widow and was struck down — was interpreted in the Talmudic era as condemning "wasting seed" (*hotza'at zera levatalah*), and the prohibition is treated seriously in Orthodox halakha. The text itself concerns Onan's refusal of levirate duty (coitus interruptus), not masturbation; the association came later. Rabbinic literature contains strong condemnations (the Zohar calls it grave) alongside more pragmatic voices.

## 19.2 Christianity: from silence to sin

The New Testament doesn't mention masturbation. Early Church Fathers focused on lust and fornication; masturbation entered gradually.

**Medieval penitentials** (6th–11th centuries) list it among sexual sins with penances typically less severe than fornication. **Thomas Aquinas** (13th century) classified it as an "unnatural vice" — non-procreative, and therefore in his framework *worse* than fornication. This became the basis of Catholic teaching.

**The Catholic Church** today: the Catechism (§2352) calls masturbation "an intrinsically and gravely disordered action" while noting that culpability may be reduced by "affective immaturity, force of acquired habit, conditions of anxiety, or other psychological or social factors." Pastoral practice varies widely.

**Protestant traditions** vary enormously. Puritan and later evangelical traditions condemned it strongly; some contemporary evangelical communities maintain this, with substantial associated distress (the moral-incongruence research, Chapter 14, is largely a study of these populations). Mainline denominations have largely moved to neutrality or acceptance.

**Eastern Orthodoxy** treats it as a sin requiring confession, with varying severity. **Latter-day Saints** have historically taught strongly against it; the tone has softened but the teaching remains.

## 19.3 Islam

The Quran doesn't mention masturbation directly; verses on guarding chastity (23:5–7, 24:30–33) are cited. Hadith are sparse and of disputed authenticity. The Sunni schools differ: **Hanafi** and **Hanbali** jurists have generally considered it forbidden but permitted as the lesser evil to avoid fornication; **Maliki** and most **Shafi'i** scholars considered it forbidden; some in each school held it merely disliked (*makruh*). **Shia** jurisprudence generally forbids it. Contemporary scholars range from prohibition to conditional permissibility. Masturbation during Ramadan fasting hours invalidates the fast in all schools.

## 19.4 Other traditions

**Buddhism.** Monastic rules prohibit it for monks as a serious offense. For laypeople, the third precept (sexual misconduct) is generally interpreted as concerning harm to others; masturbation is not typically a violation.

**Hinduism.** Ranges from ascetic *brahmacharya* (celibacy, semen retention for spiritual power — the *shukra* concept) to Tantric traditions using sexual energy deliberately, to the majority of householders for whom it's a private matter with mild disapproval at most.

**Sikhism.** Discouraged as lust (*kaam*) but not a central concern.

**Indigenous and traditional cultures.** Enormous variation. Anthropological surveys [Ford & Beach 1951] found some societies indifferent, some permissive, some restrictive. No universal human prohibition exists.

## 19.5 The medical panic: 1710–1950

The most consequential chapter for modern attitudes is not religious but medical.

***Onania*** (anonymous, London, c. 1712) — *Onania; or, The Heinous Sin of Self Pollution, and all its Frightful Consequences* — was the first mass-market work to claim masturbation caused disease: weakness, discharge, epilepsy, consumption, insanity. It sold enormously and coined "onanism."

**Samuel-Auguste Tissot**, a respected Swiss physician, published *L'Onanisme* (1760), lending medical authority. Tissot held that semen was concentrated blood (one ounce equaled forty of blood) and that its loss — especially through masturbation, which he thought more draining than intercourse — caused weakness, memory loss, blindness, gout, tuberculosis, insanity, and death. The book was medical orthodoxy across Europe for over a century.

**19th-century medicine** built on Tissot. **Masturbatory insanity** was a standard psychiatric diagnosis; asylum records list it as a cause of admission. Physicians prescribed cold baths, bland diets, restraints, spiked rings, chastity devices, circumcision, clitoridectomy in girls, and in extreme cases cauterization or castration. **Sylvester Graham** (the cracker) and **John Harvey Kellogg** (the cereal) promoted bland diets specifically to reduce sexual excitement; Kellogg's *Plain Facts for Old and Young* (1877) lists 39 signs of the "secret vice" and recommends circumcision without anesthesia for boys, "as the brief pain attending the operation will have a salutary effect upon the mind." Boy Scout handbooks into the 1920s warned against it. Freud initially treated it as a cause of neurasthenia.

**Circumcision in the anglophone world** — its rise in the US, UK, Canada, and Australia in the late 19th and early 20th centuries was driven substantially by the belief that it reduced masturbation. The rationale later shifted as the masturbation theory collapsed, but the practice persisted in the US.

**The collapse.** By the early 20th century, evidence for Tissot's claims was recognized as nonexistent. **Havelock Ellis** (*Studies in the Psychology of Sex*, 1897–1928) dismantled the medical case. **Alfred Kinsey** (1948) documented that 92% of American men masturbated — making "masturbatory insanity" statistically absurd. By the 1950s mainstream medicine had abandoned the disease model. **Masters & Johnson** (1966) studied it in the laboratory as normal physiology.

But the folklore — hair on the palms, blindness, weakness, "wasting" — had two centuries of head start, and it persists in playgrounds, families, and the deep background of adult male anxiety.

## 19.6 Modern medicine and sex education

- The **American Medical Association** declared masturbation normal in 1972.
- The **WHO**, **Planned Parenthood**, the **NHS**, and virtually every mainstream medical body now describe it as a normal, healthy part of human sexuality.
- **Joycelyn Elders**, US Surgeon General, was fired in 1994 for suggesting masturbation might be mentioned in sex education — the stigma retained political force into recent decades.
- **Sex therapy** has used directed masturbation as standard treatment for anorgasmia, PE, and DE since the 1970s [LoPiccolo & Lobitz 1972].
- **Contemporary reversal**: the 2010s saw online movements (NoFap, "semen retention," "porn addiction" communities) revive several of Tissot's themes — depletion, vital energy, testosterone, brain damage — in neuroscience-flavored language. Chapters 4 and 14 address the claims. The historical parallel is instructive: same anxieties, new vocabulary.

## 19.7 Why the stigma persists

1. **Inherited silence.** Parents don't discuss it; schools don't teach it; the vacuum fills with folklore.
2. **Religious teaching**, for those raised in condemning traditions, creates the moral incongruence that predicts distress.
3. **Masculinity norms.** Masturbation is coded as what men do when they *can't* get sex. False (partnered men masturbate the most) but persistent.
4. **The secret/dirty loop.** Done in secret → shame → done faster and more furtively → more shame.
5. **Commercial interests.** Abstinence-coaching, supplement, and "reboot" industries profit from men believing something is wrong.
6. **Real problems generalized.** Compulsive use is real for a minority (Chapter 15) and gets generalized into "masturbation is a problem."

## 19.8 A pragmatic position

You don't need to adopt any particular view. But notice:

- The disease claims are false — thoroughly, on the evidence.
- Religious prohibitions are matters of faith and interpretation, not health; many religious men and their advisors find workable positions; unresolved conflict is worse for well-being than either consistent practice or consistent abstinence.
- The "vital essence" models (Taoist, Ayurvedic, Tissot) rest on a pre-scientific understanding of semen that is simply wrong — it is continuously produced and contains no finite vital principle. The subjective experiences these traditions describe (feeling energized during abstinence) are real; the explanations aren't.
- Nearly all men do it; nearly all men worry about it; the worry is the inheritance, not the evidence.

---

### Key takeaways

- **Antiquity** was mostly indifferent (Greece, Rome, Egypt) or concerned with semen-as-essence (Taoist China, Ayurvedic India). The Onan story concerns coitus interruptus, not masturbation.
- **Christianity** developed prohibition gradually; Aquinas made it "unnatural." Catholic teaching retains this; Protestant traditions vary; evangelical communities show the highest moral-incongruence distress.
- **Islam**: no direct Quranic mention; schools range from forbidden to disliked to conditionally permitted.
- **The medical panic (1712–1950)** — *Onania*, Tissot, Kellogg — invented the disease claims and drove circumcision, restraints, and bland diets. It collapsed under Ellis and Kinsey; its folklore persists.
- **Modern medicine** is unanimous: normal and healthy.
- **"Semen retention" and "reboot" movements** revive Tissot in new vocabulary.
- The stigma is **inherited, not evidenced**.


---

# Chapter 20 — Ethics, Privacy & Digital Safety

Solo sex is private by nature, but in the digital era it leaves traces: browser histories, app data, cloud backups, connected devices, and — for men who share content or engage in remote intimacy — images and video that can escape their control. This chapter covers the ethics of content and the practical security of a private sexual life.

## 20.1 Ethics of content — a short summary

Chapter 14 covered this in context; the essentials:

- **Illegal content** — anything involving minors, or real non-consent — is absolutely off-limits, causes direct harm, and carries severe criminal penalties in every jurisdiction. There is no gray area.
- **Consent of performers** — free tube sites host substantial non-consensual material (leaked private content, hidden-camera footage, "revenge porn"). Paying for content from verified performers or studios, or using platforms with real age and consent verification, is the ethical position. It also tends to be better content.
- **Deepfakes and AI-generated likenesses of real people** without consent are harmful and increasingly illegal.
- **Written fiction and audio by adult creators** have the fewest ethical concerns.
- **Fantasy in your own head** has no ethical dimension whatsoever.

## 20.2 Privacy: the threat model

What you're protecting against, roughly in order of likelihood:

1. **Household discovery** — a partner, parent, roommate, or child finding browser history, files, toys, or supplies. The most common and most consequential in daily life.
2. **Device loss or sharing** — a phone or laptop lost, sold, repaired, or borrowed with content on it.
3. **Cloud sync** — photos, videos, or downloads automatically backed up to accounts that others can access or that are subpoenable/hackable.
4. **Data collection** — adult sites and apps tracking and profiling; connected sex toys collecting usage data; data breaches exposing users.
5. **Sextortion and leaks** — content shared with another person (consensually at the time) being used against you, or platforms being breached.
6. **Workplace monitoring** — using work devices or networks.
7. **Legal exposure** — jurisdictions where adult content is illegal, or where possession of certain material is criminalized.

## 20.3 Device and browser hygiene

**Browsing.**
- **Private/incognito mode** prevents local history, cookies, and cache from being saved. It does *not* hide activity from your ISP, network administrator, or the sites themselves. It's for household privacy only.
- **A separate browser profile or browser** for adult use keeps autocomplete, history, and logins segregated. Firefox and Chrome both support profiles.
- **Clear autocomplete** — the URL bar suggesting an adult site in front of someone is the classic failure.
- **Ad and tracker blocking** (uBlock Origin or equivalent) reduces profiling and malware exposure. Adult sites are heavily monetized through aggressive advertising and are a common malware vector.
- **Don't log in to adult sites with an account tied to your real identity** unless you're comfortable with that association existing permanently in a database that may be breached. Use a dedicated email.
- **VPN** hides browsing from your ISP and network (home, work, school). It doesn't hide it from the site or from anyone with access to your device. Useful in jurisdictions with surveillance or blocking; choose a reputable, no-logs provider.
- **DNS**: your router or ISP may log DNS queries. Encrypted DNS (DoH/DoT) closes that gap.

**Files.**
- **Don't store content on shared or synced folders.** Desktop, Downloads, Photos, and Documents are commonly synced to cloud accounts automatically (iCloud, Google Photos, OneDrive, Dropbox). Check what's syncing.
- **Encrypted containers** (VeraCrypt, or an encrypted disk image on macOS, or a password-protected archive) for anything you keep.
- **Secure deletion** — emptying the trash doesn't remove data. On SSDs, full-disk encryption + deletion is the practical answer; on spinning drives, secure-erase tools.
- **Before selling, repairing, or giving away a device**: factory reset with encryption enabled first (so the reset renders old data unrecoverable).

**Phones.**
- Private browsing; a dedicated browser app; check that photos and downloads aren't auto-backed-up; app lock or hidden album features; notifications from adult apps or sites turned off (they appear on lock screens).
- **Lock screen** with a strong passcode, not just biometrics (biometrics can be compelled or used while you sleep; a passcode is stronger legal protection in most jurisdictions).

**Shared computers.** Assume nothing is private. Don't.

**Work devices and networks.** Never. Employers routinely monitor, and dismissal for adult content on work equipment is common and generally lawful.

## 20.4 Connected sex toys (teledildonics)

App-controlled vibrators, strokers, and remote-partner devices are increasingly common. Privacy considerations:

- **Data collection.** Several manufacturers have been found collecting usage data (intensity, duration, timestamps, and in one case audio) without adequate disclosure; there have been lawsuits and settlements. Read the privacy policy. Prefer devices that work locally via Bluetooth without a cloud account, or that let you opt out of data collection.
- **Account linkage.** Creating an account with your real email links your identity to the device and its data. Use a dedicated email.
- **Bluetooth security.** Early devices had trivially exploitable Bluetooth (anyone in range could connect). Newer ones are better; keep firmware updated; pair in private.
- **Remote partner features.** Video and control sessions transit the manufacturer's servers. Assume they could be intercepted or logged. Use with people you trust; be aware of what the platform stores.
- **Breach risk.** Adult-product companies are attractive targets and have been breached. Minimize what they hold about you.

## 20.5 Sharing content: sexting, cam, and self-recording

Many men record themselves, share images or video with partners, or perform on cam. Each carries risk that can be managed but not eliminated.

**The core principle: anything that leaves your device can be copied, kept, and shared without your consent — forever.** Screenshots defeat "disappearing" messages. Partners become ex-partners. Platforms get breached. Plan on the assumption that anything you send may become public, and decide what you're comfortable with on that basis.

**Risk reduction:**
- **Don't include your face** and identifying features (tattoos, distinctive rooms, visible documents) in the same frame as your genitals. This is the single most effective protection: content without identification has limited use to a blackmailer or a leaker.
- **Strip metadata** — photos carry EXIF data including GPS location and device identifiers. Most messaging apps strip it; sending as a file often doesn't. Tools exist to strip it manually.
- **Use end-to-end encrypted platforms** (Signal, or similar) for sharing with partners. Not SMS, not email, not standard social media DMs.
- **Verify who you're talking to** before sending anything. Sextortion typically begins with a stranger (often using a fake profile) soliciting content, then threatening to send it to your contacts unless paid. It's a large-scale organized crime. If you're targeted: **don't pay** (it never ends the demands), stop communicating, screenshot the threats, report to the platform and to law enforcement (the FBI, NCA, or equivalent take this seriously), and tell someone you trust. The threat is usually not carried out when payment is refused, and even when it is, it's survivable.
- **Cam platforms.** Understand that broadcasts are recorded by viewers, that region-blocking is imperfect, and that facial recognition exists. Performers who separate cam identity from real identity do so with deliberate effort: no face, or a persona, separate accounts and payment methods, no identifying background.
- **Minors.** If you are ever sent content by someone who turns out to be a minor, or if you're unsure of someone's age, stop, delete, and don't engage. Possession is a crime regardless of how you received it. If someone claiming to be a minor solicits you, it's likely a sextortion or entrapment setup — disengage entirely.

## 20.6 Physical privacy

- **Supplies and toys.** A lockable box or drawer. Not in a shared bathroom cabinet. Toys stored discreetly and cleaned promptly (odor is a giveaway). Lube bottles are recognizable — keep them with the toys.
- **Cleanup.** Towels or wipes used and disposed of; sheets washed on a normal schedule; a small trash bag for tissues.
- **Sound.** Headphones; awareness of thin walls; timing.
- **Doors.** A lock, or a habit of making an interruption require notice (a chair, a door that sticks). A jarring interruption is unpleasant and, in the case of children, requires a calm, non-shaming response ("I need some private time; I'll be out in a bit").
- **Deliveries.** Reputable retailers ship in plain packaging with a neutral sender name. Check before ordering to a shared address. Pick-up points exist.
- **Shared devices** in the home (smart TVs, tablets, family computers, voice assistants with screens) — don't use them, and be aware that casting or account sync can put content on them accidentally.

## 20.7 Privacy in relationships

- **Disclosure vs. privacy.** You don't owe a partner a log of every session. Most relationships function well on "yes, I masturbate; here's roughly the picture" without detail. Deception about the basic fact is corrosive; privacy about specifics is normal.
- **Snooping.** A partner who reads your history is violating your privacy, and the content they find is not the main problem. Address the trust issue.
- **Shared accounts.** Separate browser profiles, separate Google/Apple accounts. "Family" plans that share purchase histories or photos are a common exposure point.
- **Content of a partner.** Images or videos a partner shared with you are theirs. Don't keep them after a breakup without explicit agreement; don't share them, ever. Non-consensual sharing is a crime in most jurisdictions.

## 20.8 Legal notes

Laws vary enormously and change. General observations, not legal advice:

- **Adult pornography** is legal to possess and view in most Western countries and illegal in many others (much of the Middle East, parts of Asia and Africa). Travel with a device containing adult content into a restrictive jurisdiction is a real risk.
- **Age verification laws** for adult sites are expanding (UK, several US states, EU proposals). They typically require ID upload or third-party verification — a privacy trade-off. VPN use to circumvent them is common and its legal status varies.
- **Extreme content laws** — some jurisdictions criminalize possession of certain categories of legal-to-produce content (e.g., UK's "extreme pornography" provisions). Know your local law.
- **Public masturbation** is illegal everywhere. "Public" includes places where you can be seen from a public space, and cars in public.
- **Recording others** without consent is illegal; so is distributing intimate images without consent.
- **Sex toys** are legal in most places; a handful of jurisdictions (some US states historically; some countries) restrict sale. Travel with toys is fine in most places; check for restrictive destinations.
- **Workplace**: adult content on work devices or networks is grounds for dismissal nearly everywhere.

## 20.9 A privacy checklist

- [ ] Dedicated browser or profile for adult use; autocomplete cleared; tracker blocking on.
- [ ] Private browsing understood as household-only protection.
- [ ] Cloud sync checked; no adult content in synced folders.
- [ ] Encrypted container for anything stored.
- [ ] Full-disk encryption enabled on all devices.
- [ ] Strong passcode (not just biometrics) on phone.
- [ ] Dedicated email for any adult accounts; no real-identity logins.
- [ ] Connected toys: privacy policy read; data collection minimized; dedicated email.
- [ ] Any shared content: no face + genitals in one frame; metadata stripped; E2E encrypted platform; recipient verified.
- [ ] Know what to do if sextorted: don't pay, stop contact, screenshot, report, tell someone.
- [ ] Physical: locked storage; plain-packaging retailer; cleanup routine.
- [ ] Never on work devices or networks.
- [ ] Aware of local law, especially when traveling.

---

### Key takeaways

- **Content ethics**: nothing illegal, ever; prefer verified-consent performers and paid content; fiction and audio have the fewest concerns; fantasy has none.
- **Threat model**: household discovery is the most common risk; cloud sync and device disposal are the most common technical failures.
- **Browser**: dedicated profile, private mode (household-only protection), tracker blocking, no real-identity logins, VPN for network privacy.
- **Files**: no synced folders; encryption; secure deletion; encrypt-then-reset before disposal.
- **Connected toys** collect data; read policies, minimize, use a dedicated email.
- **Shared content**: face and genitals never in one frame; strip metadata; E2E encryption; assume anything sent is permanent. **Sextortion: don't pay, report, tell someone.**
- **Physical**: locked storage, plain packaging, cleanup, sound awareness.
- **Never on work devices.** Know local law when traveling.

# Chapter 21 — Practice Programs & Journaling

Everything in this guide becomes real through practice. This chapter converts the material into structured programs — a 4-week foundation, an 8-week intermediate, and an ongoing advanced practice — plus the journaling templates that make progress visible. Pick the one that matches where you are. Adapt freely; the structure is a scaffold, not a rule.

**General principles for all programs:**

- **Frequency:** 3–4 sessions per week is enough for skill development. More is fine if it's what you'd do anyway; the programs don't require it.
- **Not every session is a practice session.** Quick, unstructured sessions are allowed and encouraged alongside. Just don't let them be the *only* kind.
- **Lube, always,** for practice sessions.
- **Journal briefly** after each practice session. Sixty seconds. The template is at the end.
- **If something hurts, stop.** If something isn't working after two weeks, change it.
- **Pelvic floor exercises** (Chapter 10) run in parallel — 5–10 minutes daily, separate from sessions.

## 21.1 Foundation program (4 weeks)

**For:** men who've never deliberately examined their technique; men whose sessions are habitually fast, tight, dry, or screen-locked; anyone starting from the default.

**Goals:** establish lube; learn the arousal scale; break the single-technique habit; introduce slow, attentive practice; begin pelvic floor awareness.

### Week 1 — Slow down, lube up

- **Session 1 (20 min):** Chapter 8 transition ritual (5 min). Lubricated, full grip, at *half your habitual speed and pressure*. Notice the difference. Finish however you like. Journal.
- **Session 2 (20 min):** Same, plus: rate your arousal aloud (or mentally) every minute — "5… 6… 7…" Learn where the numbers are.
- **Session 3 (20 min):** Non-dominant hand only, lubricated, slow. Notice how much more attention it requires.
- **Daily:** Pelvic floor Phase 1 (Chapter 10.4) — 5 minutes of awareness, contraction and release.

### Week 2 — Repertoire

- **Session 1 (25 min):** Work through inventory items 1–7 from Chapter 6.11, two minutes each. Journal which felt best.
- **Session 2 (25 min):** Items 8–14.
- **Session 3 (25 min):** Items 15–20. Pick your three favorites from the whole inventory.
- **Daily:** Pelvic floor Phase 1.

### Week 3 — Attention

- **Session 1 (25 min):** Mindful masturbation (Chapter 8.6), eyes closed throughout, no screen, no goal. Orgasm optional.
- **Session 2 (25 min):** Screen or fantasy for the first 5 minutes to reach arousal 6; then eyes closed, attention on sensation for the rest.
- **Session 3 (25 min):** Your three favorite techniques, rotating every 2 minutes, slow, with one hand on a secondary zone (scrotum or perineum) throughout.
- **Daily:** Pelvic floor Phase 2 begins (flicks, holds, reverse Kegels).

### Week 4 — First edges

- **Session 1 (30 min):** Build slowly to 8. Stop completely. Breathe. Drop to 6. Rebuild. Two cycles, then finish.
- **Session 2 (30 min):** Three cycles, stopping at 8.5. Add long exhale and body relaxation at each stop.
- **Session 3 (30 min):** Free session using everything from the month. Finish with a technique you've never finished with before.
- **Daily:** Pelvic floor Phase 2.

**Week 4 review:** Compare your Week 1 and Week 4 journal entries. Most men see: more sensation at lower pressure, awareness of arousal level, a wider repertoire, longer sessions without trying, and a stronger orgasm after the edged sessions.

## 21.2 Intermediate program (8 weeks)

**For:** men who've completed the foundation or who already use lube, know the arousal scale, and have some variety.

**Goals:** reliable edging; pelvic floor as a tool; two-zone stimulation as default; mapping all erogenous zones; first toy exploration; screen independence; session design.

### Weeks 1–2 — Edging proficiency

- 3 sessions/week, 30–40 min. Stop-start edging (Chapter 9.3): stop at 8.5, drop to 6–7, rebuild. Target 4–5 cycles. Tools: long exhale, body relaxation, testicle traction. Journal cycles and closest approach.
- Pelvic floor Phase 2 daily; begin erect practice twice weekly.
- One screen-free session per week.

### Weeks 3–4 — Pelvic floor integration and mapping

- Session A (40 min): the **erogenous zone mapping exercise** (Chapter 11.3) — every zone, three touch types, rated. Journal fully.
- Session B (40 min): edging with **reverse Kegel at each peak**. Notice whether it drops arousal for you.
- Session C (40 min): edging with **pelvic floor pulsing during pauses** to hold arousal at 7 instead of letting it fall to 6.
- Repeat pattern week 4, now stopping at 9 rather than 8.5.
- Pelvic floor Phase 2, progressing holds.

### Weeks 5–6 — Layering and toys

- Session A (40 min): edging with two zones throughout — penis plus the highest-rated secondary zone from your mapping. Add a **third receptor type** (temperature via warmed/cooled lube or a cloth; or stretch via scrotal traction).
- Session B (40 min): **first toy session** — a bullet vibrator on the frenulum and perineum (Chapter 13.3), low setting, moving, alongside hand stimulation. Or, if prostate curiosity: external anal touch only (Chapter 12.1 Stage 1).
- Session C (30 min): quick session, but with one deliberate difference from your habit (standing; non-dominant hand; different finishing technique).
- Continue one screen-free session/week; consider a full **screen-free week** in week 6.

### Weeks 7–8 — Continuous edging and session design

- Session A (45 min): **continuous modulated edging** (Chapter 9.5) — no full stops; modulate technique to hold 8–9 for a target of 5 minutes, then 10.
- Session B (45 min): the **45-minute full session** template from Chapter 11.9, followed exactly once, then adapted.
- Session C (varies): your own design. Journal the design and the result.
- Pelvic floor Phase 3 begins — the exercises are now mostly *in* sessions.

**Week 8 review:** You should have: 5+ edge cycles reliably; reverse Kegel working as a brake; a map of your zones; a two-zone default; comfort with at least one toy; at least one full screen-free week completed; and a clear sense of what your best sessions have in common.

## 21.3 Advanced practice (ongoing)

**For:** men who've completed the intermediate program or equivalent.

There's no fixed schedule; there's a set of practices to rotate through and deepen. A reasonable rhythm: one "deep" session per week (60–90 min), one or two medium sessions (30–45 min), and quick sessions as desired.

### Practices

**Whole-body arousal** (Chapter 11.8). Long plateau (20+ min at 8–9), full non-pelvic relaxation, deep belly breathing with imagined direction of sensation outward, nipples and perineum involved, movement, sound. Track: tingling in extremities, warmth spreading, trembling — the markers of the nervous system routing arousal widely.

**Prostate development** (Chapter 12.1). Weekly or more, 30–45 min, with a toy. Stages 2→5. Combine with penile arousal at 8+ until prostate sensation is clearly pleasurable, then reduce penile input. Track: sensation quality (0–5), any waves or peaks, comfort.

**Non-ejaculatory orgasm** (Chapter 12.3). Emerges from edging depth. In each deep session, hold at 9 (not 9.5) with total relaxation and breath; at the first involuntary pulses, stop all stimulation and reverse Kegel; observe. Track: peaks without ejaculation, arousal drop afterward, refractory experience. Months, not weeks.

**Orgasm engineering** (Chapter 11.7). Vary one variable per session: pelvic floor flexed vs. released at orgasm; stimulation continued vs. stopped at first contraction; perineal pressure timed to contractions; slow vs. fast approach; standing vs. lying; exhale with sound vs. silent. Rate each. Build your personal optimal.

**Sensory expansion.** Rotate through receptor types deliberately: a "temperature session," a "vibration session," a "texture session," a "caress-speed session." Keep the nervous system surprised.

**Range maintenance.** Once a month: a deliberately minimal session — hands, no lube, no toys, no screen, ten minutes — to confirm the baseline still works. Once a month: a screen-free week. Once a quarter: two to four days of abstinence followed by a planned deep session (Chapter 11.2).

**Pelvic floor maintenance.** 5–10 min, 4–5×/week. Always including release work.

**Periodic re-mapping.** Every 3 months, repeat the erogenous zone mapping. Sensitivity changes with practice — especially nipples and prostate.

**Periodic honest check** (Chapter 15). Can I skip it easily? Do I enjoy it? Is my life better for it?

## 21.4 Problem-specific programs

Cross-references for men working on a specific issue:

- **Premature ejaculation:** Chapter 9.8's 8-week program + Chapter 10's 12-week pelvic floor program + Chapter 16.1. Emphasis on release, breath, slowness.
- **Delayed ejaculation / idiosyncratic technique:** Chapter 16.2's retraining (suspend the method, substitute partner-like stimulation, reduce frequency, align fantasy) — 4–8 weeks — + Chapter 11.3 mapping for unused inputs + Chapter 8 for spectatoring.
- **Death grip:** Chapter 16.3's 4-week recalibration.
- **Screen dependence / conditioning:** Chapter 14.4 — front-load then eyes closed; no browsing; 2–6 week porn break with fantasy-based sessions; audio.
- **Compulsive pattern:** Chapter 15.5 — reframe, change configuration (no screens, time limits, not in bed at night, planned sessions), address what's underneath, structured break, professional help if needed.
- **Hypertonic pelvic floor / pelvic pain:** Chapter 10.5 — release emphasis, stretching, breathing, self-massage, physiotherapy.
- **Erectile difficulty (psychogenic):** Chapter 16.4 — no-goal sessions with erection unmonitored; mindfulness; cardiovascular basics; pelvic floor; screen break if conditioned; PDE5i to break the cycle if needed.

## 21.5 Journaling templates

Keep it short. The value is in the pattern over weeks, not in any single entry. A notes app, a spreadsheet, or paper — whatever you'll actually use. Privacy considerations from Chapter 20 apply.

### Session log (60 seconds)

```
Date/time:
Duration:
Type: quick / practice / deep
Body state in: sleep (1-5) / stress (1-5) / days since last orgasm
Setting: screen / fantasy / audio / none
Techniques (main 2-3):
Zones (besides penis):
Toys:
Peak arousal held: [level] for [minutes]
Edge cycles:
Orgasm: Y / N / dry / multiple
Orgasm rating (1-10):
Orgasm quality: sharp / rolling / whole-body / muted
Afterglow (min):
New this session:
Note:
```

### Erogenous zone map (every 3 months)

```
Date:
Arousal level during mapping:
Zone           | Light | Firm | Stroke | Notes
Scrotum        |       |      |        |
Perineum       |       |      |        |
Nipples L/R    |       |      |        |
Inner thighs   |       |      |        |

Base           |       |      |        |
Lower abdomen  |       |      |        |
Neck/ears      |       |      |        |
Other:         |       |      |        |
(rate 0-5)
Top 3 secondary zones:

### Orgasm engineering log

```

Setting A:                    Rating:

### Monthly review (5 minutes)

```

Sessions logged:
Average orgasm rating:
Best session — what made it good:
Worst session — what made it poor:
Skills that improved:

Honest check — skip easily? enjoy it? life better? (Y/N each)
Body — any pain, irritation, numbness?
Pelvic floor — days trained:
Next month's focus:
```

## 21.6 A note on progress

Skill in solo sex follows the same curve as any skill: fast early gains (weeks 1–4 typically produce the most dramatic change), a plateau, then slower deepening over months and years. The plateau is not failure; it's the stage at which the fundamentals have become automatic and the subtle work begins.

Some things — prostate pleasure, non-ejaculatory orgasm, whole-body arousal — may take many months or may not develop for you. That's individual variation, not inadequacy. The intermediate states are pleasurable in themselves.

The single most reliable predictor of progress is *paying attention* — during sessions, and in the journal afterward. Men who track improve. Men who don't, drift back to habit.

---

### Key takeaways

- **Foundation (4 weeks):** lube, slow down, arousal scale, 20-item repertoire, mindful attention, first edges, pelvic floor awareness.
- **Intermediate (8 weeks):** reliable 5+ cycle edging, reverse Kegel as brake, erogenous zone mapping, two-zone default, layered receptor types, first toys, screen-free week, continuous edging, session design.
- **Advanced (ongoing):** whole-body arousal, prostate development, non-ejaculatory orgasm, orgasm engineering, sensory rotation, range maintenance, re-mapping, honest checks.
- **Problem-specific programs** cross-reference Chapters 9, 10, 14, 15, 16.
- **Journal** — 60 seconds per session, a map every quarter, a 5-minute monthly review. Tracking is the strongest predictor of progress.
- **Progress curve**: fast early gains, plateau, slow deepening. Individual variation is real; intermediate states are the point, not a consolation.
