# Chapter 22 — FAQ

Direct answers to the questions men actually ask. Each points to the chapter with the full discussion.

## Frequency and normalcy

**Is it normal to masturbate every day?**
Yes. Daily masturbation is common, especially under 40, and is not associated with any harm in the absence of compulsivity or physical symptoms. (Ch. 4, 15)

**Is it normal to masturbate several times a day?**
Common in younger men; not harmful by itself. Watch for skin irritation and ask the Chapter 15 questions (control, interference, enjoyment). (Ch. 15)

**Is it normal to rarely or never masturbate?**
Yes. Some men have low solo desire; some have partnered sex that meets their needs; some choose not to. Not masturbating is only a concern if it's driven by shame or anxiety that's causing other problems. (Ch. 15.6)

**How often do most men masturbate?**
Enormous range. Roughly: most men under 40 a few times a week; about a third of men "a few times a week or more"; frequency declines with age but with huge overlap. (Ch. 1, 4)

**I'm in a relationship. Is it normal to still masturbate?**
Yes — it's *most* common among partnered men aged 25–39. It's not a sign of dissatisfaction. Secrecy causes more problems than the behavior. (Ch. 15.7, 17.6)

**Am I addicted?**
Frequency doesn't answer this. Take the self-assessment in Chapter 15.4. The clinical question is: loss of control, real consequences, continued despite them, for 6+ months. Moral guilt alone doesn't qualify. (Ch. 15)

## Health

**Does masturbation lower testosterone?**
No. Baseline testosterone is unaffected. Brief fluctuations after orgasm or abstinence are small and clinically meaningless. (Ch. 4.3.1)

**Does abstaining raise testosterone?**
Not meaningfully. The most-cited study (a 7-day spike) is small, has been flagged as retracted, and shows a return to baseline afterward. (Ch. 4.3.1, 4.5)

**Does it cause hair loss?**
No. Hair loss is genetic and androgen-mediated; masturbation doesn't alter androgens. (Ch. 4.3.3)

**Does it cause erectile dysfunction?**
Ordinary masturbation doesn't. Very specific patterns — compulsive use, extreme grip, exclusive reliance on one stimulus — may contribute to *situational* difficulties in some men, and these are reversible. (Ch. 4.3.2, 14, 16.4)

**Does it cause premature ejaculation?**
Rushed, fast, tense masturbation can *train* a fast reflex. Slow, attentive masturbation with stop-start practice *treats* PE. The technique matters, not the act. (Ch. 9.8, 16.1)

**Does it affect the prostate?**
The largest study found men ejaculating ≥21 times/month had ~20% lower prostate cancer diagnosis risk than men at 4–7/month. Correlational, not proven. Certainly not harmful. (Ch. 4.2.1)

**Does it affect fertility or sperm quality?**
Frequent ejaculation (every 1–3 days) is associated with *better* sperm DNA quality. Long abstinence increases volume but reduces motility. (Ch. 4.2.8)

**Does it "waste" energy or semen?**
No. Semen is continuously produced; there's no finite reservoir. The subjective energy some men feel during abstinence is real but isn't from "retention." (Ch. 4.3.4, 19)

**Is it good for anything?**
Stress relief, sleep, possibly pain relief, pelvic floor and erectile maintenance, self-knowledge, sexual skill, and pleasure. (Ch. 4.2)

**Can I hurt myself?**
Friction burns, frenulum tears, and bruising are common and preventable with lube and moderate grip. Penile fracture (from bending an erect penis forcefully) and urethral injury (from inserting objects) are rare and serious. (Ch. 5)

**Why do I feel sad or empty afterward?**
Post-coital dysphoria — reported by ~40% of men at some point. Usually brief; hormonal shift and/or surfaced emotion. If it's shame-driven, Chapter 4 and 19 help. If persistent and distressing, a therapist. (Ch. 16.9)

**Why do I feel sick for days after orgasm?**
Possibly post-orgasmic illness syndrome (POIS) — rare, real, not psychological. See a urologist. (Ch. 16.8)

**Is blood in semen serious?**
Almost always benign, especially under 40 — a small vessel from vigorous activity. Recurrent, or over 40, or with other symptoms — see a doctor. (Ch. 16.6)

**My testicles ache after a long session without orgasm. Is that dangerous?**
"Blue balls" (vasocongestion) is real, benign, and resolves with ejaculation or time. Sudden severe testicular pain is different — emergency. (Ch. 16.5)

## Technique

**I've always done it one way. Does it matter?**
Yes. A single technique habituates (less pleasure over time) and can condition a narrow threshold (harder to orgasm any other way). Variety is both more pleasurable and protective. (Ch. 6)

**Should I use lube?**
Almost always yes for circumcised men; often for uncircumcised. It protects skin, adds sensation detail, and opens techniques impossible dry. (Ch. 6.6, 7)

**What lube?**
Silicone for hand sessions (lasts, few ingredients). Water-based for toys (check osmolality for anal). Coconut oil or jojoba if you want natural and don't use latex or TPE toys. Never numbing lubes. (Ch. 7)

**How hard should I grip?**
Lighter than you think. If you leave red marks or need a tight grip to feel anything, that's conditioning. Recalibrate over 4 weeks. (Ch. 6.5, 16.3)

**How fast?**
Slow for most of the session. Speed is for finishing, if at all. Most of the pleasure is in the slow build. (Ch. 6.4)

**What's the most sensitive part?**
For most men, the frenulum, then the corona and glans. Direct, lubricated fingertip attention to the frenulum is the highest-yield single technique. (Ch. 2.2, 6.2.3)

**I'm uncircumcised — anything different?**
Your foreskin provides gliding; lube is optional but adds a category. Glans is usually more sensitive — approach gradually. Lube under the foreskin with the foreskin gliding over it is a distinct technique. Return the foreskin after washing. (Ch. 6.7, 5.1)

**I'm circumcised — anything different?**
Lube is more important (sliding, not gliding). Glans is usually less sensitive, so direct glans techniques are more usable. Find your scar line — it's often the most sensitive zone. (Ch. 6.7)

**What's the best position?**
Supine for relaxation and long sessions; standing for firmer erections and intense orgasm; kneeling/side-lying for perineal and anal access. Vary. (Ch. 6.8)

**How do I last longer?**
Learn the arousal scale; stop-start at 8.5; long exhales; relax the body; reverse Kegel at the edge; slow down the whole session; pelvic floor training. 8–12 weeks. (Ch. 9, 10, 16.1)

**How do I finish faster / at all?**
If you struggle to orgasm: identify and suspend any idiosyncratic method (prone, death grip, specific fantasy), substitute partner-like stimulation, reduce frequency for a few weeks, add inputs (vibration, prostate, zones), stop watching yourself. (Ch. 16.2)

**What is edging?**
Approaching orgasm, backing off, repeating. Extends sessions and intensifies the eventual orgasm. Also the behavioral treatment for PE. (Ch. 9)

**How do I make orgasms more intense?**
Longer build-up (20–60 min), 5–10 edge cycles, two zones at once, pelvic floor strength, perineal pressure timed to contractions, long exhale with sound, full attention, no screen at the peak. (Ch. 11.7)

**Why does my erection go soft during long sessions?**
Normal. Erections fluctuate with stimulation level and attention. It returns. Pelvic floor contractions can restore it. Not a problem. (Ch. 3.2, 9.10)

**Can I orgasm without an erection?**
Yes. Full erection isn't required for orgasm or ejaculation. (Ch. 3.9)

## Pleasure

**What am I missing?**
Most men: the build-up (rushing to orgasm), secondary zones (scrotum, perineum, nipples), attention (on a screen instead of the body), variety (one technique), the afterglow (getting up immediately). (Ch. 11)

**What secondary zones are worth trying?**
Scrotum (cupping, tugging), perineum (firm pressure), nipples (develops with practice), inner thighs, external anal touch, and the prostate. Map them once. (Ch. 11.3)

**Does nipple stimulation do anything for men?**
About half of men report it increases arousal; sensitivity often develops with practice over weeks. (Ch. 2.7, 11.3)

**Should I try prostate stimulation?**
If you're curious, yes — it's the largest addition available, orientation-irrelevant, safe with basic precautions, and often intensifies penile orgasm even before it does anything on its own. Weeks to months. (Ch. 12.1)

**Does prostate play mean anything about my orientation?**
No. The prostate is innervated identically in all men. (Ch. 12.1)

**What is a "full-body orgasm"?**
Orgasmic sensation spreading beyond the pelvis — built from deep relaxation, breath, attention widening, multiple zones, movement, and sustained plateau. Develops over weeks. (Ch. 11.8)

**Can men have multiple orgasms?**
Yes, via non-ejaculatory orgasm (prostate route, edging-derived dry peaks, or the clench) — because the refractory period tracks ejaculation, not orgasm. Months of practice; not everyone gets there. (Ch. 12.3)

**Do I need toys?**
No. They add sensations hands can't produce (vibration, enveloping pressure, internal stimulation). A bullet vibrator is the best first purchase. (Ch. 13)

**Why does a vibrator feel so different?**
It recruits Pacinian receptors (deep vibration) that hands can't stimulate efficiently. (Ch. 11.3, 13.3)

**Is it better with or without porn?**
For arousal ("wanting"), porn is efficient. For sensation ("feeling"), it competes for attention. Most men find the hybrid — material to get to 6–7, then eyes closed — gives stronger orgasms than continuous watching. (Ch. 8.10, 14.4)

**How long should a session be?**
Any length. Skill develops in 20–60 minute sessions; pleasure peaks for most men at 30–60 minutes of plateau; beyond ~90 minutes returns diminish. Quick sessions have their place. (Ch. 6.9, 9.10)

**Is morning or evening better?**
Morning often produces firmer erections and more sensation (testosterone peak, rested). Evening is more convenient. Late-night exhausted sessions are the worst version. (Ch. 11.4)

**How long should I wait between sessions for the best orgasm?**
2–4 days for most men. Beyond a week, some men find sensitivity paradoxically drops. (Ch. 11.4)

## Pornography and fantasy

**Is watching porn bad for me?**
For most men, no measurable harm. For a minority, compulsive use causes real problems. Porn is fiction and a poor educator. Keep the range wide; watch for the pattern, not the number. (Ch. 14)

**Does porn cause ED?**
Not porn per se. Problematic/compulsive use is associated with ED; conditioning and anxiety are the likely mechanisms; it's reversible. (Ch. 14.3)

**I can only get hard to porn. What do I do?**
A 2–6 week break from porn (not necessarily from masturbation), with fantasy-based, attentive sessions. Arousal to non-screen stimuli typically returns in 2–4 weeks. (Ch. 14.4)

**Is my fantasy content a problem?**
If it's legal and stays in your head, no. Fantasy isn't a wish or a diagnosis; nearly everyone has fantasies they'd never want to enact. (Ch. 14.1)

**What is "gooning"?**
Prolonged edging with continuous porn, seeking a trance-like state. As an occasional intensity setting — a legitimate choice. As a nightly, escalating, identity-forming pattern that displaces sleep and life — a description of compulsive behavior. (Ch. 15.3)

**Should I do NoFap / a reboot?**
Abstinence has no physiological benefits. The psychological benefits some men report are real and likely come from breaking a compulsive pattern. A structured break from *porn* is what most men who benefit actually needed; masturbation itself isn't the problem. Avoid shame-based framing. (Ch. 4.5, 14.4)

## Safety

**Is anal play safe?**
Yes with: lots of low-osmolality lube, flared-base toys only, slow entry at high arousal, no numbing agents, washing before anything touches the penis. (Ch. 5.7, 12.1)

**Can I use a household object?**
No. Not in the anus (retrieval surgery), not in the urethra (strictures), not as a ring (emergency removal), not a vacuum cleaner. Only things designed for the purpose. (Ch. 5.3)

**How long can I wear a cock ring?**
20–30 minutes maximum. Remove at any pain, numbness, cold, or color change. Never sleep in one. Never an unsized rigid ring. (Ch. 13.4)

**Is a lump on my penis or testicle serious?**
Pearly penile papules, Fordyce spots, and sebaceous cysts are normal. A hard lump *in* a testicle needs a doctor within days. A plaque in the shaft with new curvature — urologist. (Ch. 16.15)

**When should I see a doctor?**
Emergencies: erection >4 hours, popping sound + swelling, sudden severe testicular pain, foreskin stuck retracted, stuck object, first thunderclap headache at orgasm. Soon: recurrent blood in semen, testicular lump, new curvature, sores/warts, persistent numbness. (Ch. 5.9, 16.16)

**Is breath play / choking during masturbation ever safe alone?**
No. It kills. This is the only flat prohibition in the guide. (Ch. 12.5)

## Life and relationships

**Should I tell my partner I masturbate?**
Yes, in general terms. Secrecy is corrosive; detail is optional. (Ch. 15.7, 17.6)

**My partner is upset that I masturbate. What now?**
Find out why — insecurity, moral belief, deception, specific concern about porn or frequency. Each has a different response. Total prohibition is an unusual demand; porn negotiation is common. (Ch. 17.6)

**I'm over 50 and things are changing. Is that normal?**
Yes. More stimulation needed, softer erections, longer refractory, less volume. Adapt: more time, more zones, vibration, prostate, pelvic floor, PDE5i if wanted. Erection isn't required for orgasm. (Ch. 17.3)

**I'm on antidepressants and can't finish. Options?**
Talk to the prescriber: dose, switching (bupropion, mirtazapine), adjuncts, timing. Add vibration and inputs. Don't stop the medication on your own. (Ch. 16.2, 18.6)

**Can I masturbate the night before a competition/exam?**
Yes. The "sex before competition" myth is unsupported. Immediately before (within an hour), individual. (Ch. 17.8)

**I have no privacy. What can I do?**
Shower sessions (silicone lube), timing, headphones, locked storage, a 15-minute quiet mindful session is achievable. (Ch. 17.8)

## Miscellaneous

**Why is my semen yellow / clumpy / watery?**
Yellow after abstinence: normal. Clumpy/gel then liquefying: normal. Watery with frequent ejaculation: normal. Brown/red: blood (usually benign). Green/grey with pain: infection. (Ch. 16.14)

**Why does the first ejaculation after a long gap have more volume?**
Seminal vesicles and prostate fill over 3–5 days, then plateau. (Ch. 3.4)

**Why can't I get hard again right after?**
The refractory period — likely prolactin-driven, tied to ejaculation. Minutes in youth, hours later. Non-ejaculatory orgasms have little or none. (Ch. 3.7)

**Is my penis normal?**
Almost certainly. Average erect length ~13 cm; curvature <30° is normal; size varies more flaccid than erect; nerve density, not length, determines what you feel. (Ch. 2.8)

**What's the single most useful thing in this guide?**
Slow down, use lube, and put your attention on the sensation instead of the screen or the finish line. Everything else builds on that. (Ch. 6, 8, 11)

# Chapter 23 — Glossary

**Anejaculation** — Absence of ejaculation despite orgasm or adequate stimulation. May be retrograde (into the bladder) or true (no emission).

**Anhedonic ejaculation** — Ejaculation without the pleasurable sensation of orgasm.

**Anorgasmia** — Inability to reach orgasm despite adequate stimulation and desire.

**Arousal scale** — The 1–10 scale used throughout this guide: 6 cruising, 7 rising, 8 committed, 8.5 approaching, 9 the edge, 9.5 point of no return, 10 orgasm.

**Balanitis** — Inflammation of the glans; usually yeast or bacterial; often triggered by moisture, soap, or diabetes.

**Blue balls (epididymal hypertension)** — Testicular ache from prolonged arousal without ejaculation. Vasocongestion. Benign; resolves.

**BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia)** — Non-cancerous prostate enlargement common with age; causes urinary symptoms.

**Bulbospongiosus** — Pelvic floor muscle wrapping the bulb of the penis; produces the rhythmic contractions of ejaculation.

**Bulbourethral (Cowper's) glands** — Produce pre-ejaculate.

**cGMP** — The second messenger that relaxes penile smooth muscle to allow erection; broken down by PDE5.

**Circumcision** — Surgical removal of the foreskin.

**Clench (Kegel block)** — Sustained maximum pelvic floor contraction at the point of no return to block ejaculation while allowing orgasm.

**Coital cephalalgia** — Headache at or near orgasm. Usually benign; first occurrence warrants evaluation.

**Corona (coronal ridge)** — The flared rim at the base of the glans; nerve-dense.

**Corpora cavernosa** — The paired erectile bodies on the top/sides of the penis.

**Corpus spongiosum** — The erectile body surrounding the urethra on the underside; expands into the glans.

**CPPS (chronic pelvic pain syndrome / chronic non-bacterial prostatitis)** — Persistent pelvic pain often attributed to pelvic floor tension.

**CSBD (Compulsive Sexual Behaviour Disorder)** — ICD-11 code 6C72. Persistent failure to control intense sexual impulses resulting in repetitive behavior, with life impairment, for 6+ months. Excludes distress from moral disapproval alone.

**Death grip** — Lay term for habitual very tight, fast masturbation leading to a learned high stimulation threshold.

**Delayed ejaculation (DE)** — Marked delay in or absence of ejaculation despite adequate stimulation.

**Dhat syndrome** — South Asian culture-bound anxiety syndrome about semen loss.

**Dry orgasm** — Orgasm without ejaculation. See non-ejaculatory orgasm.

**Dysorgasmia** — Painful orgasm/ejaculation.

**Edging** — Repeatedly approaching orgasm and backing off before it occurs.

**Emission** — Phase 1 of ejaculation: semen moves into the prostatic urethra under sympathetic control. The point of no return.

**Epididymis** — Coiled tube on the back of each testis where sperm mature.

**Erectile dysfunction (ED)** — Persistent inability to attain or maintain an erection sufficient for satisfactory sexual activity.

**Expulsion** — Phase 2 of ejaculation: rhythmic pelvic floor contractions propel semen out.

**Fordyce spots** — Small, normal sebaceous glands visible as white/yellow dots on the shaft or scrotum.

**Foreskin (prepuce)** — The retractable double-layered skin covering the glans in uncircumcised men.

**Frenulum** — The band of tissue on the underside connecting glans to shaft/foreskin; usually the most sensitive spot.

**Frenulum breve** — A short frenulum that restricts glans movement or tears during erection.

**Glans** — The head of the penis.

**Gooning** — Prolonged edging with continuous pornography seeking a trance-like state; also the associated subculture.

**Hematospermia** — Blood in semen. Usually benign.

**Hypertonic pelvic floor** — Chronically tight pelvic floor muscles; associated with PE, pelvic pain, ED.

**Idiosyncratic masturbatory style** — Perelman's term for a technique (pressure, speed, position) that a partner can't replicate; a cause of DE.

**IELT (intravaginal ejaculatory latency time)** — Time from penetration to ejaculation; the clinical measure in PE research.

**Injaculation** — Taoist term for blocking ejaculation via perineal pressure at orgasm, causing retrograde flow.

**Ischiocavernosus** — Pelvic floor muscle compressing the roots of the penis; increases rigidity.

**Kegel** — Voluntary contraction of the pelvic floor muscles.

**Levator ani** — The deep pelvic floor muscle group including the pubococcygeus.

**Lymphocele** — Firm, cord-like, painless swelling behind the corona from a temporarily blocked lymph vessel after vigorous activity. Resolves.

**Meatus** — The urethral opening at the tip.

**Milking** — Expressing prostatic fluid by prostate massage; also a single-direction stroking technique.

**Mindful masturbation** — Solo practice with attention on sensation, without a goal.

**Moral incongruence** — Conflict between one's sexual behavior and one's moral beliefs about it; the strongest predictor of distress about pornography use.

**Myotonia** — Involuntary muscle tension during arousal.

**NEO (non-ejaculatory orgasm)** — Orgasm without ejaculation; typically little or no refractory period.

**Nitric oxide (NO)** — The signaling molecule released by parasympathetic nerves to initiate erection.

**NoFap** — Online community and practice of abstaining from pornography and/or masturbation.

**Onanism** — Archaic term for masturbation, from the Genesis story of Onan.

**Osmolality** — Concentration of dissolved particles; lubricants far above body osmolality (~300 mOsm/kg) damage epithelial cells. WHO: ideal <380, maximum 1,200.

**Paraphimosis** — Foreskin trapped behind the glans and swelling. Emergency.

**PC muscle (pubococcygeus)** — Pelvic floor muscle; the usual target of "Kegel" instructions.

**PDE5 (phosphodiesterase type 5)** — Enzyme that ends erection by breaking down cGMP. Inhibited by sildenafil, tadalafil, etc.

**PE (premature ejaculation)** — Ejaculation sooner than desired with inability to delay and distress; clinically ~≤1 min (lifelong) or bothersome reduction (acquired).

**Pearly penile papules** — Small, uniform, normal bumps in rows around the corona.

**Pelvic floor** — The muscular sling at the base of the pelvis; the voluntary control panel for erection, ejaculation, and orgasm.

**Perineum** — The area between scrotum and anus; pressure here reaches the bulb of the penis and toward the prostate.

**Peyronie's disease** — Fibrous plaque in the tunica albuginea causing curvature, pain, or deformity.

**Phimosis** — Inability to retract the foreskin.

**PIED ("porn-induced erectile dysfunction")** — Popular but not clinically established term for ED attributed to pornography.

**Plateau** — Sustained high arousal before orgasm (Masters & Johnson's second phase).

**POIS (post-orgasmic illness syndrome)** — Rare condition of flu-like symptoms for days after ejaculation.

**Point of no return (PONR)** — The moment emission begins; ejaculation will follow regardless. Arousal 9.5.

**Post-coital dysphoria** — Sadness, irritability, or emptiness after orgasm.

**Pre-ejaculate** — Clear fluid from the bulbourethral glands during arousal.

**Priapism** — Erection lasting >4 hours without stimulation. Emergency.

**Prolactin** — Hormone surging after orgasm; probable driver of satiety and the refractory period.

**Prostate** — Walnut-sized gland below the bladder; produces ~25% of semen; stimulable through the rectal wall; a source of orgasm.

**Prostate-induced orgasm** — Orgasm from prostate stimulation, often non-ejaculatory and described as more whole-body.

**Pudendal nerve** — Carries sensation from the genitals and motor commands to the pelvic floor.

**Raphe** — The midline seam running from the frenulum down the underside of the penis, scrotum, and perineum.

**Refractory period** — Post-ejaculation period during which further orgasm is difficult or impossible.

**Retrograde ejaculation** — Semen entering the bladder instead of exiting; from medications, diabetes, surgery, or deliberate blocking.

**Reverse Kegel** — Deliberate relaxation and gentle downward release of the pelvic floor.

**Ridged band** — Specialized sensory tissue just inside the tip of the foreskin.

**Ruined orgasm** — Stimulation stopped at or just after the PONR; ejaculation with muted or absent orgasmic sensation.

**Semen retention** — Practice of avoiding ejaculation based on the belief that semen contains vital essence. Not supported physiologically.

**Seminal vesicles** — Paired glands producing ~65% of semen volume.

**Sensate focus** — Masters & Johnson's therapy technique of goal-free touch with attention on sensation.

**Sextortion** — Blackmail using intimate images.

**Smegma** — Normal accumulation of shed cells and oils under the foreskin.

**Sounding** — Inserting rods into the urethra. Significant injury risk.

**Spectatoring** — Self-monitoring during sex; inhibits arousal and orgasm.

**Squeeze technique** — Masters & Johnson's PE method: firm squeeze below the glans at high arousal.

**Stop-start technique** — Semans' PE method: stop stimulation at high arousal, resume when it subsides.

**Supernormal stimulus** — A stimulus engineered beyond anything in nature (e.g., internet pornography), which may shift reference points.

**Sympathetic / parasympathetic** — The two branches of the autonomic nervous system. Parasympathetic drives erection; sympathetic drives emission/ejaculation.

**Tantra / Taoist sexual practice** — Traditions using breath, attention, and (in Taoism) semen retention for extended sexual experience.

**Teledildonics** — App-controlled or remote-partner sex toys.

**Testosterone** — Primary male sex hormone; produced mainly during sleep; unaffected by masturbation frequency.

**TPE/TPR** — Thermoplastic elastomer/rubber; porous, non-toxic material of most strokers.

**Tunica albuginea** — Fibrous sheath around the erectile bodies (and around each testis). Tears in penile fracture.

**Vas deferens** — Tube carrying sperm from the epididymis toward the urethra.

**Veno-occlusion** — Compression of draining veins during erection that traps blood.

**Whole-body (full-body) orgasm** — Orgasmic sensation spreading beyond the pelvis.

## Books

**Chia M, Arava DA.** *The Multi-Orgasmic Man*. HarperOne; 1996. — Taoist-derived practices; the source of the "clench" technique and much online material. Useful for technique; its physiological claims are not evidence-based.

**Ellis H.** *Studies in the Psychology of Sex* (7 vols). 1897–1928. — Historical; dismantled the masturbation-disease model.

**Kinsey AC, Pomeroy WB, Martin CE.** *Sexual Behavior in the Human Male*. Saunders; 1948.

**Laqueur TW.** *Solitary Sex: A Cultural History of Masturbation*. Zone Books; 2003. — The definitive history of the panic. Scholarly.

**Ley DJ.** *The Myth of Sex Addiction*. Rowman & Littlefield; 2012. — Critique of the addiction model. Polemical but well-sourced.

**Nagoski E.** *Come As You Are*. Simon & Schuster; 2015. — Written for women; the dual-control model and mindfulness material apply broadly.

**Perel E.** *Mating in Captivity*. Harper; 2006. — Desire in long-term relationships.

**Stengers J, Van Neck A.** *Masturbation: The History of a Great Terror*. Palgrave; 2001. — Companion to Laqueur.

**Tissot SA.** *L'Onanisme*. 1760. — Historical primary source of the disease model.

**Zilbergeld B.** *The New Male Sexuality*. Bantam; 1999. — Classic sex-therapy text for men; sensible on masturbation, PE, DE, and myths.

## Organizations and resources

- **AASECT** (American Association of Sexuality Educators, Counselors and Therapists) — aasect.org — directory of certified sex therapists (US).
- **COSRT** (College of Sexual and Relationship Therapists) — cosrt.org.uk — UK equivalent.
- **ISSM** (International Society for Sexual Medicine) — issm.info — patient information on sexual dysfunction.
- **Sexual Medicine Society of North America** — smsna.org — patient resources.
- **Planned Parenthood** — plannedparenthood.org — plain-language sexual health information.
- **NHS** — nhs.uk — UK sexual health guidance.
- **American Urological Association** — urologyhealth.org — patient information on ED, PE, Peyronie's, BPH.
- **Herman & Wallace Pelvic Rehabilitation Institute** — hermanwallace.com — directory of pelvic floor physiotherapists (many treat men).
- **POIS support** — poiscenter.com — patient community and research updates for post-orgasmic illness syndrome.
- **Cyber Civil Rights Initiative** — cybercivilrights.org — help with non-consensual image sharing and sextortion.
- **National Center for Missing & Exploited Children (US) / Internet Watch Foundation (UK)** — for reporting illegal content.

## A note on evidence quality in this field

Sexual health research is under-funded relative to its importance, dominated by self-report, prone to both moral and advocacy bias, and often based on small samples. Several of the most-cited studies in popular discourse (the Jiang testosterone paper, the Park pornography review, the Haake immune study) are weak. This guide has tried to weight the evidence honestly, note where claims rest on thin ground, and distinguish clinical consensus from folklore in either direction. Readers who encounter confident claims elsewhere — for or against masturbation, pornography, abstinence, or any technique — are encouraged to ask: how big was the sample, was it controlled, who funded it, and does the conclusion match the data?
